)

{algorithm2e}
\DontPrintSemicolon

\text{GreedyMerge}(\text{Set}_{1},\text{Set}_{2})
\KwData
\text{Set}_{1}
is a set of clusters of red colored vertices and
\text{Set}_{2}
is a set of clusters of blue colored vertices

\KwData
Output: Return a fair set of clusters
\mathcal{F}
.
Sort the clusters in
\text{Set}_{1}
in non-decreasing order based on their sizes
Sort the clusters in
\text{Set}_{2}
in non-decreasing order based on their sizes
Initialize a set,
\mathcal{F}=\emptyset

\For
CRed in
\text{Set}_{1}
and CBlue in
\text{Set}_{2}

\If
|CRed|>|CBlue|

Take a subset,
CRed^{\prime}\subseteq CRed
such that
|CRed^{\prime}|=|CBlue|

CRed=CRed\setminus CRed^{\prime}

Let,
Fair=CBlue\cup CRed^{\prime}

\text{Set}_{2}=\text{Set}_{2}\setminus CBlue

\Else

Take a subset,
CBlue^{\prime}\subseteq CBlue
such that
|CBlue^{\prime}|=|CRed|

CBlue=CBlue\setminus CBlue^{\prime}

Let,
Fair=CBlue^{\prime}\cup CRed

\text{Set}_{1}=\text{Set}_{2}\setminus CRed

Add the cluster
Fair
to the set
\mathcal{F}

\Return
\mathcal{F}
.

A.2
Analysis of the Algorithm

In this section, we prove
Theorem

1
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be the output of
Section

A.1
on input
\mathcal{D}
. It is easy to see that
\mathcal{F}
is a fair clustering, and the
Section

A.1
runs in
O(n)
time. Therefore, it suffices to show that

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{D})=\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{*},\mathcal{D}),

(1)

where
\mathcal{C}^{*}
is any closest fair clustering to
\mathcal{D}
.

To accomplish this, we show that we can construct a fair clustering
\mathcal{C}^{f}
from
\mathcal{C}^{*}
such that
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{f},\mathcal{D})=\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{*},\mathcal{D})
, while the properties of
\mathcal{C}^{f}
, which are established through the construction, ensure that
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{f},\mathcal{D})\geq\textit{dist}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{D})
. By the definition of
\mathcal{C}^{*}
, it follows the
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{*},\mathcal{D})=\textit{dist}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{D})
. The detailed construction is provided in the following section.

A.2.1
Optimal Fair Clustering Properties & Greedy Merge Technique

Let
\mathcal{C}^{0}
be an arbitrary clustering of
V
. We construct a clustering
\mathcal{C}^{f}
from
\mathcal{C}^{0}
by applying a sequence of four fairness-preserving transformations:

\mathcal{C}^{(0)}\to\mathcal{C}^{(1)}\to\mathcal{C}^{(2)}\to\mathcal{C}^{(3)}\to\mathcal{C}^{(4)}=\mathcal{C}^{f},

such that

\forall_{0\leq i\leq 3},\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{(i+1)},\mathcal{D})\leq\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{(i)},\mathcal{D}),

and
\mathcal{C}^{(i)}
satisfies all the
i
properties (detailed below) established prior to that step. Since these transformations preserve fairness, if
\mathcal{C}^{0}
is fair, then all
\mathcal{C}^{i}
are fair.

We now describe the properties and the transformations.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an arbitrary clustering of
V
. Recall that the input clustering is
\mathcal{D}=\{D_{1},D_{2},\ldots,D_{\phi}\}
.

Property 1
.

For each cluster
C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}
, if there exists no index
k
such that
C_{i}\subseteq D_{k}
, then the sets
R_{i,k}\subseteq\textit{red}\left(C_{i}\right)
and
B_{i,k}\subseteq\textit{blue}(C_{i})
cannot both be non-empty while simultaneously satisfying the conditions

\displaystyle R_{i,k}\subseteq D_{K},b_{i,k}\subseteq D_{K}.

We construct a clustering
\mathcal{C}^{1}
from a clustering
\mathcal{C}^{0}
as follows.

Construction of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
:

For each
k\in[|\mathcal{D}|]
and each cluster
C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{(0)}
, let
R_{i,k}
and
B_{i,k}
be the maximal subsets of red and blue points in
C_{i}
such that:

\displaystyle R_{i,k}\subseteq\textit{red}\left(C_{i}\right),B_{i,k}\subseteq\textit{blue}(C_{i}),R_{i,k}\cup B_{i,k}\subseteq D_{k}.

For all
i,\ k
, cut two subsets
R^{\prime}_{i,k}
and
B^{\prime}_{i,k}
of size
\min(|R_{i,k}|,|B_{i,k}|)
from
R_{i,k}
and
B_{i,k}
respectively, and construct a new cluster as
R^{\prime}_{i,k}\cup B^{\prime}_{i,k}
. The new clustering
\mathcal{C}^{1}
consists of these new clusters
R^{\prime}_{i,k}\cup B^{\prime}_{i,k}
and the clusters which are the remaining of
C_{i}
. It can be seen that
\mathcal{C}^{1}
satisfies
1
.

Lemma 13
.

The constructed clustering
\mathcal{C}^{1}
satisfies
1
and if
\mathcal{C}^{0}
is fair, then
\mathcal{C}^{1}
is fair and

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{(1)},\mathcal{D})\leq\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{(0)},\mathcal{D}).

We employ the following fact.

Fact 1
.

Let
C_{i}
be a fair cluster. Then, for any subset
S\subseteq\textit{red}\left(C_{i}\right)
(or
S\subseteq\textit{blue}(C_{i})
), we have
|C_{i}|\geq 2|S|.

Indeed, as
C_{i}
is fair,
|\textit{red}\left(D_{i}\right)|=|\textit{blue}(D_{i})|
. Therefore,
|C_{i}|=2|\textit{red}\left(C_{i}\right)|\geq 2|S|
.

Proof of
Lemma

13
:

Suppose that
\mathcal{C}^{0}
is fair, then each
C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{0}
is fair. Since each newly constructed cluster
R^{\prime}_{i,k}\cup B^{\prime}_{i,k}
contains an equal number of red and blue points, it is fair. Furthermore, an equal number of red and blue points are split from each
C_{i}
; the remaining clusters also preserve fairness.

It remains to show
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{(1)},\mathcal{D})\leq\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{(0)},\mathcal{D})
.

As for each
k\in[|\mathcal{D}|]
, we move
\min(|R_{i,k}|,|B_{i,k}|)
points from each cluster
C_{i}
to form new clusters, therefore

\displaystyle\>\>\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{(2)},\mathcal{D})-\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{(1)},\mathcal{C}^{*})

\displaystyle=\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{(1)}}\big(\sum_{0\leq k\leq|\mathcal{D}|}\min(|R_{i,k}|,|B_{i,k}|)\left\lvert|R_{i,k}|-|R_{i,k}|\right\rvert

\displaystyle\>\>-\sum_{0\leq j<k\leq|\mathcal{D}|}2\min(|R_{i,k}|,|B_{i,k}|)(|R_{i,j}|+|B_{i,j}|)\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{(1)}}\min(|R_{i,k}|,|B_{i,k}|)\big(\sum_{0\leq k\leq|\mathcal{D}|}\left\lvert|R_{i,k}|-|B_{i,k}|\right\rvert-\sum_{0\leq k\leq|\mathcal{D}|}2\left\lvert|R_{i,k}|+|B_{i,k}|\right\rvert\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{(1)}}\min(|R_{i,k}|,|B_{i,k}|)\big(\sum_{0\leq k\leq|\mathcal{D}|}\left\lvert|R_{i,k}|-|B_{i,k}|\right\rvert-\sum_{j\neq k}(|R_{i,j}|+|B_{i,j}|)\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{(1)}}\min(|R_{i,k}|,|B_{i,k}|)\big(\sum_{0\leq k\leq|\mathcal{D}|}\left\lvert|R_{i,k}|-|B_{i,k}|\right\rvert-|C_{i}|+|R_{i,k}|+|B_{i,k}|)\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{(1)}}\min(|R_{i,k}|,|B_{i,k}|)\big(\sum_{0\leq k\leq|\mathcal{D}|}\left\lvert|R_{i,k}|-|B_{i,k}|\right\rvert-|C_{i}|+|R_{i,k}|+|B_{i,k}|)\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{(1)}}\min(|R_{i,k}|,|B_{i,k}|)\big(\sum_{0\leq k\leq|\mathcal{D}|}-|C_{i}|+2\max(|R_{i,k}|,|B_{i,k}|)\big)

\displaystyle\leq 0,

where the inequality is due to
1
,
|C_{i}|\geq 2\max(|R_{i,k}|,|B_{i,k}|))
.

\jmlrQED

Property 2
.

For each
k\in[|\mathcal{D}|]
, there exists at most one cluster
C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}
such that
C_{i}\subseteq D_{k}
.

We construct a clustering
\mathcal{C}^{2}
from a clustering
\mathcal{C}^{1}
as follows.

Construction of
\mathcal{C}^{2}
:

For each
k\in[|\mathcal{D}|]
, let
\mathcal{I}_{k}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{1}
be the maximal size subset of clusters such that for each
C_{i}\in\mathcal{I}_{k}
,
C_{i}\subseteq D_{k}
. Merge all
C_{i}\in\mathcal{I}_{k}
. The new clustering
\mathcal{C}^{2}
consists of these new merged clusters and the remaining clusters of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
. It can be seen that
\mathcal{C}^{2}
preserves
1
while also satisfying
2
.

Lemma 14
.

The new clustering
\mathcal{C}^{(2)}
satisfies
1
and
2
, and if
\mathcal{C}^{(1)}
is fair then
\mathcal{C}^{(2)}
is also fair, and

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{(2)},\mathcal{D})\leq\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{(1)},\mathcal{D}).

Proof:
Suppose that
\mathcal{C}^{1}
is fair. Then each cluster
C_{i}
in clustering
\mathcal{C}^{(1)}
is fair, the new clustering
\mathcal{C}^{(2)}
, which consists of a union of a subset of these clusters, must also be fair. This follows from the fact that fairness is preserved under the union of fair clusters.

We now show
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{(2)},\mathcal{D})\leq\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{(1)},\mathcal{D})
. Indeed, since
\mathcal{C}^{(2)}
results from merging clusters, it follows that

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{(2)},\mathcal{D})-\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{(1)},\mathcal{D})=-\sum_{i<j,C_{i},C_{j}\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}|C_{i}||C_{j}|\leq 0,

which is equivalent to
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{(2)},\mathcal{D})\leq\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{(1)},\mathcal{D})
.

\jmlrQED

Property 3
.

For each
C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}
, one of the following conditions hold:

1

There exists a
k\in[|\mathcal{D}|]
such that
C_{i}\subseteq D_{k}
.

2

There exists a partition of
C_{i}
into two disjoint subsets
R_{i,k}
and
B_{i,j}
, such that there exists indices
k,j\in[|\mathcal{D}|]
(where
k\neq j
) satisfying:

R_{i,k}\subseteq D_{k},B_{i,j}\subseteq D_{j}.

Suppose that
\mathcal{C}^{2}
is fair. We construct a clustering
\mathcal{C}^{3}
from a clustering
\mathcal{C}^{2}
as follows.

Construction of
\mathcal{C}^{3}
:

For each
k\in[|\mathcal{D}|]
and each cluster
C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{(2)}
, let
R_{i,k}
and
B_{i,k}
be the maximal subsets of red and blue points in
C_{i}
such that:

\displaystyle R_{i,k}\subseteq\textit{red}\left(C_{i}\right),B_{i,k}\subseteq\textit{blue}(C_{i}),R_{i,k}\cup B_{i,k}\subseteq D_{k}.

From
2
, for each
i,\ k
, at least one of
R_{i,k}
or
B_{i,k}
must be empty. Now we define the new clustering
\mathcal{C}^{(3)}
by applying
Section

A.1
to each
C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{(2)}
on the input cluster sets:

\displaystyle\bigcup_{k\in[|\mathcal{D}|]}R_{i,k},\bigcup_{k\in[|\mathcal{D}|]}B_{i,k}.

Lemma 15
.

If
\mathcal{C}^{2}
is fair, then the new clustering
\mathcal{C}^{(3)}
satisfies
1
,
2
, and
3
. Moreover,
\mathcal{C}^{3}
is also fair and

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{(3)},\mathcal{D})\leq\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{(2)},\mathcal{D}).

Proof:
Suppose that
\mathcal{C}^{2}
is fair.

We show that
\mathcal{C}^{3}
is also fair. Indeed, since
Section

A.1
selects an equal number of red and blue points and merges them together, the resulting clustering remains fair. Consequently,
\mathcal{C}^{(3)}
preserves fairness.

It is straightforward to verify that
1
and
2
continue to hold, as no new cluster
C
has been introduced such that there exists an index
k
satisfying
C\subseteq D_{k}
.

To see that
3
also holds, recall that each cluster constructed by
Section

A.1
consists of two disjoint subsets of
R_{i,k}\subseteq D_{k}
and
B_{i,j}\subseteq D_{j}
for some indices
i,j,k
.

Finally, we show that
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{(3)},\mathcal{D})\leq\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{(2)},\mathcal{D})
. Define
\alpha_{j,k}(C_{i})
as the number of blue and red points that
Section

A.1
merges from the subsets of
R_{i,j}
and
B_{i,j}
. Note that for each
i,\ j
,
Section

A.1
merges a subset of
R_{i,j}
and
B_{i,j}
at most once. We have

\displaystyle\>\>\>\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{(3)},\mathcal{D})-\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{(2)},\mathcal{D})

\displaystyle=\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{(2)}}\bigg[\sum_{0\leq j,k\leq|\mathcal{D}|}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\alpha_{j,k}(C_{i})(R_{i,j}-\alpha_{j,k}(C_{i})+\frac{1}{2}\alpha_{j,k}(C_{i})(B_{i,j}-\alpha_{j,k}(C_{i})

\displaystyle\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>-\dfrac{1}{2}\alpha_{j,k}(|C_{i}|-R_{i,j})-\dfrac{1}{2}\alpha_{j,k}(|C_{i}|-B_{i,k})\bigg)\bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{(2)}}\frac{1}{2}\alpha_{j,k}(C_{i})\bigg[\sum_{0\leq j,k\leq|\mathcal{D}|}2\bigg(-|C_{i}|+R_{i,j}+B_{i,k}-\alpha_{j,k}(C_{i})\bigg)\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq 0,

where the inequality is due to
|C_{i}|\geq R_{i,j}+B_{i,k}
.

\jmlrQED

Property 4
.

For each
k\in[|\mathcal{D}|]
, there exists a cluster
C_{i}
such that
C_{i}
is the maximal fair subset of
D_{k}
.

Before introducing the construction for
\mathcal{C}^{4}
from the clustering
\mathcal{C}^{3}
, we establish a lower bound on the distance from an input clustering consisting solely of monochromatic clusters to any of its fair clustering. This serves as a key component in bounding the distance from
\mathcal{D}
to
\mathcal{C}^{4}
. We begin with an input clustering
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{R}\cup\mathcal{B}
, where
\mathcal{R}=\{R_{1},R_{2},\dots,R_{t}\}
consists of monochromatic clusters containing only red points, and
\mathcal{B}=\{B_{1^{\prime}},B_{2^{\prime}},\dots,B_{m^{\prime}}\}
consists of monochromatic clusters containing only blue points. Moreover we have
\sum_{i\in[t]}{R}_{i}=\sum_{j\in[m^{\prime}]}{B}_{j}
. This ensures the existence of a fair clustering of
\mathcal{C}
.

Lemma 16
.

The distance from
\mathcal{C}
to any fair clustering
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}
of the input clustering
\mathcal{C}
that satisfies
3
is:

\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in[t]}|{R}_{i}|^{2}+\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{j\in[m^{\prime}]}|{B}_{j}|^{2}.

Proof:
Consider any arbitrary fair clustering
\mathcal{\tilde{C}}
satisfying
3
. Each cluster
\tilde{C}_{k}\in\mathcal{\tilde{C}}
can be further decomposed into two disjoint subsets,
\tilde{R}_{k,i}
and
\tilde{B}_{k,j}
where
\tilde{R}_{k,i}\subseteq R_{i}
and
\tilde{B}_{k,j}\subseteq B_{j}
for some
i\in[t]
and
j\in[m^{\prime}]
.

Further as
\mathcal{\tilde{C}}
is fair
|\tilde{R}_{k,i}|=|\tilde{B}_{k,j}|
. Thus, each cluster
\tilde{C}_{k}\in\mathcal{\tilde{C}}
pays
|\tilde{R}_{k,i}||\tilde{B}_{k,j}|=\dfrac{1}{2}(|\tilde{R}_{k,i}|^{2}+|\tilde{B}_{k,j}|^{2})
. Further to separate
\tilde{R}_{k,i}
from
R_{i}
the cost required is
\dfrac{1}{2}|\tilde{R}_{k,i}||R_{i}\setminus\tilde{R}_{k,i}|
; similarly to separate
\tilde{B}_{k,j}
from
B_{j}
the cost required is
\dfrac{1}{2}|\tilde{B}_{k,j}||B_{j}\setminus\tilde{B}_{k,j}|
.
Thus the total distance from
\mathcal{C}
to
\mathcal{\tilde{C}}
is:

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{\tilde{C}},\mathcal{C})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[t]}\sum_{\tilde{R}_{k,i}\subseteq R_{i}}\dfrac{1}{2}[|\tilde{R}_{k,i}|^{2}+|\tilde{R}_{k,i}|(|R_{i}|-|\tilde{R}_{k,i}|)]+\sum_{j\in[m^{\prime}]}\sum_{\tilde{B}_{k,j}\subseteq B_{j}}\dfrac{1}{2}[|\tilde{B}_{k,j}|^{2}+|\tilde{B}_{k,j}|(|B_{j}|-|\tilde{B}_{k,j}|)]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[t]}\sum_{\tilde{R}_{k,i}\subseteq R_{i}}\dfrac{1}{2}|\tilde{R}_{k,i}||R_{i}|+\sum_{j\in[m^{\prime}]}\sum_{\tilde{B}_{k,j}\subseteq B_{j}}\dfrac{1}{2}|\tilde{B}_{k,j}||B_{j}|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[t]}\dfrac{1}{2}|R_{i}|^{2}+\sum_{j\in[m^{\prime}]}\dfrac{1}{2}|B_{j}|^{2}.

\jmlrQED

Let
\mathcal{C}^{3}
is a fair clustering satisfying
1
,
2
, and
3
. We construct a clustering
\mathcal{C}^{4}
from
\mathcal{C}^{3}
as follows.

Construction of
\mathcal{C}^{4}
:

By
1
and
2
, for each
k
, in
\mathcal{C}^{3}
, there exists at most one cluster containing both subset of
\textit{red}\left(D_{k}\right)
and a subset of
\textit{blue}(D_{k})
. If such a cluster exists (denoted as
C_{\ell_{k}}
), then
C_{\ell_{k}}\subseteq D_{k}
.

Next, based on
3
, any remaining cluster can be partitioned into two disjoint subsets
R_{i,k}
and
B_{i,j}
, such that there exist indices
k,j\in[|\mathcal{D}|]
(where
k\neq j
) satisfying:

\displaystyle R_{i,k}\subseteq\textit{red}\left(D_{k}\right),\quad B_{i,j}\subseteq\textit{blue}(D_{j}).

Each such cluster is then split into its disjoint subsets
R_{i,k}
and
B_{i,j}
.

For each
k
, we define two disjoint subsets:

•

\mathcal{I}_{k}^{r}\subseteq\bigcup_{\forall C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}R_{i,k}
, which is the largest subset of clusters
R_{i,k}
such that
R_{i,k}\in D_{k}
;

•

\mathcal{I}_{k}^{b}\subseteq\bigcup_{\forall C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}B_{i,k}
, which is the largest subset of clusters
B_{i,k}
such that
B_{i,k}\in D_{k}
.

For each
k
, we sort the non-empty clusters in
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{r}
in non-increasing order based on their size and denote the
i
-th cluster in this ordering as
R_{i}(k)
. Similarly, we sort the clusters in
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{b}
in non-increasing order based on their size and denote the
i
-th cluster in this ordering as
B_{i}(k)
.

We then compute:

\displaystyle\min\left(\sum_{R_{i}(k)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{r}}|R_{i}(k)|,\sum_{B_{i}(k)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{b}}|B_{i}(k)|\right).

Without loss of generality, assume that:
\sum_{R_{i}(k)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{r}}|R_{i}(k)|
is the smaller of the two sums.

We then identify the index
t
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{t}|B_{i}(k)|\leq\sum_{R_{i}(k)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{r}}|R_{i}(k)|<\sum_{i=0}^{t+1}|B_{i}(k)|.

For all
R_{i,k}\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{r}
we select
{R_{i}(k)}
and
{B_{i}(k)}
from indices
1
to
t
along with a portion
\alpha_{k}
from
B_{t+1}(k)
, where:

\displaystyle\alpha_{k}=\sum_{i=0}^{t+1}|B_{i}(k)|-\sum_{R_{i}(k)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{r}}|R_{i}(k)|.

We proceed by merging these subsets with
C_{\ell_{k}}
, provided that
C_{\ell_{k}}
exists. The resultant cluster, denoted by
C_{\ell_{k}}
, represents the maximal fair subset of
D_{k}
.

To construct the collection of all such maximal fair clusters, we define the set:

\mathcal{M}=\{C_{\ell_{k}}|k\in[|\mathcal{D}|]\},

where
\mathcal{M}
contains all the maximal fair clustering derived from the input clusters.

As for each
k\in[|\mathcal{D}|]
, the maximal fair part is removed, and by the end of this process, at least one of the sets of
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{r}
or
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{b}
is empty. If
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{r}
is empty, then all
B_{i}(k)
from
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{b}
are merged into a cluster
B_{k}
, where
|B_{k}|=|\textit{red}\left(D_{k}\right)|-|\textit{blue}(D_{k})|
. Conversely, if
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{b}
is empty, then all
R_{i}(k)
from
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{r}
are merged into a cluster
R_{k}
, where
|R_{k}|=|\textit{red}\left(D_{k}\right)|-|\textit{blue}(D_{k})|
. Define

•

\mathcal{R}=\{R_{k}\mid\mathcal{I}_{k}^{r}\text{is non-empty}\}
, which consists of red monochromatic clusters
R_{k}
.

•

\mathcal{B}=\{B_{k}\mid\mathcal{I}_{k}^{b}\text{is non-empty}\}
, which consists of blue monochromatic clusters
B_{k}
.

Finally, we apply
Section

A.1
to the input subsets:

\text{Set}_{1}=\mathcal{R},\text{Set}_{2}=\mathcal{B}

Suppose that
\mathcal{F}
denotes the output of the algorithm.

We define
\mathcal{C}^{(4)}
as the union of maximal fair clusters
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{F}
, formally expressed as:

\mathcal{C}^{(4)}=\mathcal{M}\cup\mathcal{F}.

Lemma 17
.

The new clustering
\mathcal{C}^{(4)}
is fair and satisfies all properties
1
,
2
,
3
, and
4
. Moreover

\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{(4)},\mathcal{D})\leq\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{(3)},\mathcal{D})
.

Proof:
It is straightforward to verify that for each
k\in[|\mathcal{D}|]
,
C_{\ell_{k}}
remains fair as it is the maximal fair subset of
D_{k}
. The rest of the clusters are the result of
Section

A.1
, which construct fair clusters. Therefore
\mathcal{C}^{(4)}
remains fair.

Furthermore, considering the input structure of
Section

A.1
, for each
k\in[|\mathcal{D}|]
either
R_{k}
or
B_{k}
is necessarily empty. This guarantees that
1
,
2
hold.

Additionally, given the mechanism by which
Section

A.1
operates, it is straightforward to verify that
3
is also satisfied. Moreover, since
\mathcal{C}^{(4)}
is defined as the union of maximal fair clusters
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{F}
, if follows that
4
is also satisfied.

It remains to show that
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{(4)},\mathcal{D})\leq\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{(3)},\mathcal{D})
. Applying
Lemma

16
, we establish that the distance from
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{R}\cup\mathcal{B}
to any fair clustering
\mathcal{F}
(the output of
Section

A.1
) is

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{R_{i}\in\mathcal{R}}|{R}_{i}|^{2}+\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{B_{i}\in\mathcal{B}}|{B}_{j}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in[|\mathcal{D}|]}\left(|\textit{red}\left(D_{k}\right)|-|\textit{blue}(D_{k})|\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in[|\mathcal{D}|]}\left(\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|R_{i}(k)|-\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|B_{i}(k)|\right)^{2}.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\>\>\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{(4)},\mathcal{D})-\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C}^{(3)},\mathcal{D})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in[|\mathcal{D}|]}\bigg[-\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|R_{i}(k)|^{2}-\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|B_{i}(k)|^{2}-\sum_{i<j}|R_{i}(k)||R_{j}(k)|-\sum_{i<j}|B_{i}(k)||B_{j}(k)|-\sum_{i,j}|R_{i}(k)||B_{j}(k)|

\displaystyle\>+2\min(\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|R_{i}(k)|,\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|B_{i}(k)|)\left(\max(\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|R_{i}(k)|,\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|B_{i}(k)|)-\min(\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|R_{i}(k)|,\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|B_{i}(k)|\right)

\displaystyle\>+\dfrac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|R_{i}(k)|-\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|B_{i}(k)|\right)^{2}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\in[|\mathcal{D}|]}\bigg[-\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|R_{i}(k)|^{2}-\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|B_{i}(k)|^{2}-\sum_{i<j}|R_{i}(k)||R_{j}(k)|-\sum_{i<j}|B_{i}(k)||B_{j}(k)|-\sum_{i,j}|R_{i}(k)||B_{j}(k)|

\displaystyle\>+2\min(\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|R_{i}(k)|,\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|B_{i}(k)|)\max(\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|R_{i}(k)|,\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|B_{i}(k)|)

\displaystyle\>+\dfrac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|R_{i}(k)|-\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|B_{i}(k)|\right)^{2}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in[|\mathcal{D}|]}\bigg[-\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|R_{i}(k)|^{2}-\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|B_{i}(k)|^{2}-\sum_{i<j}|R_{i}(k)||R_{j}(k)|-\sum_{i<j}|B_{i}(k)||B_{j}(k)|-\sum_{i,j}|R_{i}(k)||B_{j}(k)|

\displaystyle\>+2(\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|R_{i}(k)|)(\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|B_{i}(k)|)+\dfrac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|R_{i}(k)|-\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|B_{i}(k)|\right)^{2}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in[|\mathcal{D}|]}\bigg[-\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|R_{i}(k)|^{2}-\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|B_{i}(k)|^{2}-\sum_{i<j}|R_{i}(k)||R_{j}(k)|-\sum_{i<j}|B_{i}(k)||B_{j}(k)|

\displaystyle\>+2(\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|R_{i}(k)|)(\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|B_{i}(k)|)+\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|R_{i}(k)|^{2}+\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|B_{i}(k)|^{2}+\sum_{i<j}|B_{i}(k)||B_{j}(k)|

\displaystyle\>+\sum_{i<j}|R_{i}(k)||R_{j}(k)|-(\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|R_{i}(k)|)(\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|B_{i}(k)|)\bigg]

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i,j}|R_{i}(k)||B_{j}(k)|+(\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|R_{i}(k)|)(\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}}|B_{i}(k)|)

\displaystyle=0.

\jmlrQED

Proof of
Theorem

1
:

Given a fair optimal clustering
\mathcal{C}^{*}
, we transform it to
\mathcal{C}^{f^{*}}
(say) such that it satisfies all the properties,
1
,
2
,
3
, and
4
and
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{C}^{f^{*}})\leq\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{C}^{*})
.

Now, we prove
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{F})\leq\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{C}^{f^{*}})
where
\mathcal{F}
is the output of the
Section

A.1
.

In
Section

A.1
, we divide a cluster
D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}
into two sets, FairPart and NotFairPart. Note that FairPart is the maximal fair subset of
D_{i}
. Let’s denote the maximal fair subset of
D_{i}
by
M_{i}
and
M=\bigcup_{i\in[|\mathcal{D}|]}M_{i}
.

Hence,

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{F})=\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\{M_{k}\mid k\in[|\mathcal{D}|]\})+\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{F}\setminus\{M_{k}\mid k\in[|\mathcal{D}|]\})+\textit{cost}(M,V\setminus M)

(2)

where
\textit{cost}(M,V\setminus M)
counts the number of pairs
(u,v)
such that
u\in M
and
v\in V\setminus M
but
u
and
v
are present in the same cluster
D_{k}
(say) in
\mathcal{D}
. Recall that
V
is the domain.

Now in
\mathcal{C}^{f^{*}}
by
4
we get for each
i\in[|D|]
there exists
C_{j}\in\mathcal{C}^{f^{*}}
such that
C_{j}
is the maximal fair subset of
D_{i}
. Since
C_{j}
is a maximal subset of
D_{i}
, we can assume
C_{j}=M_{i}
(maximal FairPart that we cut from
D_{i}
) because
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},{C}^{f^{*}})
is independent of points present in
C_{j}
it depends only on the size of
C_{j}
.

Hence,

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{C}^{f^{*}})=\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\{M_{k}\mid k\in[|\mathcal{D}|]\})+\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{C}^{f^{*}}\setminus\{M_{k}\mid k\in[|\mathcal{D}|]\})+\textit{cost}(M,V\setminus M)

(3)

By comparing
Eq.

2
and
Eq.

3
we get that to show
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{F})\leq\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{C}^{f^{*}})
we need to prove
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{F}\setminus\{M_{k}\mid k\in[|\mathcal{D}|]\})\leq\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{C}^{f^{*}}\setminus\{M_{k}\mid k\in[|\mathcal{D}|]\})
.

The set
D_{i}\setminus M_{i}
is either a red monochromatic set or a blue monochromatic set. If
D_{i}\setminus M_{i}
is red monochromatic, we denote it with
R_{i}
, and if
D_{i}\setminus M_{i}
is blue monochromatic, then we denote it with
B_{i}
. Thus initially we have a set of red monochromatic clusters
\mathcal{R}=\{R_{1},R_{2},\ldots R_{t}\}
and blue monochromatic clusters
\mathcal{B}=\{B_{1},B_{2},\ldots,B_{m}\}
where both
t
and
m
are less than
|\mathcal{D}|
.

Now,
\mathcal{C}^{f^{*}}\setminus\{M_{k}\mid k\in[|\mathcal{D}|]\}
is an optimal fair clustering on
\mathcal{R}\cup\mathcal{B}
that satisfies
3
.

Thus, from
Lemma

16
we get

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{C}^{f^{*}}\setminus\{M_{k}\mid k\in[|\mathcal{D}|]\})\geq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in[t]}|R_{i}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j\in[m]}|B_{j}|^{2}

(4)

Again since
Section

A.1
takes
\mathcal{R}\cup\mathcal{B}
as input and construct a fair clustering
\mathcal{F}\setminus\{M_{k}\mid k\in[|\mathcal{D}|]\}
that satisfies
3
we get,

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{F}\setminus\{M_{k}\mid k\in[|\mathcal{D}|]\})\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in[t]}|R_{i}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j\in[m]}|B_{j}|^{2}

(5)

Hence, from
Eq.

2
,
Eq.

3
,
Eq.

4
and
Eq.

5
we conclude
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{F})\leq\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{C}^{f^{*}})
.

\jmlrQED

Appendix B
Constant Approximation for Closest Fair Clustering

In
Appendix

A
, we show how to find an optimal closest fair clustering to any input clustering when the total number of blue and red points are equal in the data set. Now, we focus on a more general case when the (irreducible) ratio between the blue and red points is
p/q
for some positive integers
p,q\geq 1
, and provide an approximation algorithm. To show our results, we take a two-stage process. First, we design approximation algorithms that find a close balanced cluster – for each cluster, the number of blue points is a multiple of
p
, and the number of red points is a multiple of
q
. Next, we design an approximation algorithm to find a close fair clustering to an already balanced clustering. Finally, we combine both these algorithms to establish our approximation guarantee for the closest fair clustering problem.

In this section, we first describe the combination step, i.e., how to use an approximation algorithm for the closest balanced clustering problem and an approximation algorithm for the closest fair clustering problem when the inputs are balanced clustering.

Theorem 18
.

Consider any
\alpha,\beta\geq 1
. Suppose there is a
t_{1}(n)
-time algorithm Algorithm-A() that, given any clustering on
n
points, produces an
\alpha
-close
Balanced clustering
, and a
t_{2}(n)
-time algorithm Algorithm-B() that, given any
Balanced clustering
on
n
points, produces a
\beta
-close
Fair clustering
. Then there is an
O(t_{1}(n)+t_{2}(n)+n)
-time algorithm that, given any clustering on
n
points, produces an
\left(\alpha+\beta+\alpha\beta\right)
-close
Fair clustering
.

Proof:
Consider the following simple algorithm: Given a clustering
\mathcal{D}
as input, first run Algorithm-A() to generate a
Balanced clustering

\mathcal{T}
, and then run Algorithm-B() with
\mathcal{T}
as input to generate a
Fair clustering

\mathcal{F}
. Finally, output
\mathcal{F}
.

It is straightforward to see that the running time of the above algorithm is
O(t_{1}(n)+t_{2}(n)+n)
. We now claim that
\mathcal{F}
is an
\left(\alpha+\beta+\alpha\beta\right)
-close
Fair clustering
of the input clustering
\mathcal{D}
.

Let
\mathcal{F}^{*}
be an (arbitrary) closest
Fair clustering
of the input
\mathcal{D}
, and
\mathcal{T}^{*}
be an (arbitrary) closest
Balanced clustering
of the input
\mathcal{D}
. Since by the definition, any
Fair clustering
is also a
Balanced clustering
, we get

\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*})\leq\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{F}^{*}).

(6)

Now, by the guarantee provided by Algorithm-A(), we get

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T})

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\cdot\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*})

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\cdot\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{F}^{*})

\displaystyle\text{(By \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:opt-fair-bal})}.

(7)

Next, let
\mathcal{C}^{*}
be an (arbitrary) closest
Fair clustering
of
\mathcal{T}
(recall,
\mathcal{T}
is an output of Algorithm-A()). Then, by the guarantee provided by Algorithm-B(), we get

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle\leq\beta\cdot\textit{dist}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{C}^{*})

\displaystyle\leq\beta\cdot\textit{dist}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}^{*})

(Since
\mathcal{F}^{*}
is a valid
Fair clustering
)

\displaystyle\leq\beta\cdot\left(\textit{dist}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{D})+\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{F}^{*})\right)

(By the triangle inequality)

\displaystyle\leq\beta(\alpha+1)\cdot\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{F}^{*})

\displaystyle\text{(By \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:output-bal})}.

(8)

Thus, finally, we deduce that

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle\leq\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T})+\textit{dist}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})

(By the triangle inequality)

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\cdot\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{F}^{*})+\beta(\alpha+1)\cdot\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{F}^{*})

(By
Appendix

B
and
Appendix

B
)

\displaystyle=(\alpha+\beta+\alpha\beta)\cdot\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{F}^{*})

and that concludes the proof.

\jmlrQED

Now, as a corollary of the above theorem, we get our
O(1)
-approximation results for the closest fair clustering problem. In
Appendix

E
, we present an algorithm to make an already balanced cluster fair; more specifically, we show the following theorem.

Theorem 19
.

Let
\ratio=p/q
where
p\neq q
are coprime positive integers. There exists a linear time algorithm that, given any
Balanced clustering

\mathcal{T}
, finds a fair clustering
\mathcal{F}
such that

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})\leq 3\textit{dist}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}^{*}),

where
\mathcal{F}^{*}
is a closest fair clustering to
\mathcal{T}
.

Approximation guarantee when
p>1,q=1
.

In
Appendix

C
, we present an approximation algorithm to make any arbitrary clustering with the ratio between blue and red points being
p
for some integer
p>1
; more specifically, we show the following result.

Theorem 20
.

Consider an integer
p>1
. There is an algorithm that, given a clustering
\mathcal{D}
over a set of
n
red-blue colored points where the ratio between the total number of blue and red points is
p
, outputs a
3.5
-close
Balanced clustering
of
\mathcal{D}
in time
O(n\log n)
.

Now, as an immediate corollary of
Theorem

18
, by
Theorem

20
and
Theorem

19
, we get the following result.

See
2

Approximation guarantee when
p,q>1
.

, we present an approximation algorithm to make any arbitrary clustering with the ratio between blue and red points being
p/q
for some integer
p,q>1
, where
p,q
are coprime; more specifically, we show the following result.

.

Consider two integers
p,q>1
. There is an algorithm that, given a clustering
\mathcal{D}

red-blue colored points where the irreducible ratio between the total number of blue and red points is

, outputs a

of

Appendix C
Closest balanced clustering for
p>1,q=1

In this section, given a clustering
\mathcal{D}=\{D_{1},D_{2},\ldots,D_{\phi}\}
of a set of red-blue colored points, where the irreducible ratio of blue to red points is
p
for some integer
p>1
, we construct a balanced clustering
\mathcal{T}=\{T_{1},T_{2},\ldots,T_{\gamma}\}
. That is, in the resulting clustering
\mathcal{T}=\{T_{1},T_{2},\ldots,T_{\gamma}\}
, every cluster
T_{i}\in\mathcal{T}
satisfies the condition that
|\textit{blue}(T_{i})|
is a multiple of
p
. Our main result for this section is the following:

See
20

We provide the algorithm to find a
3.5
-close
Balanced clustering
in
Section

C.1
and provide the analysis of the algorithm in
Section

C.2

C.1
Details of the Algorithm

Before describing our algorithm, let us first introduce a few notations that we use later.

For any input cluster
D_{i}
, if its number of blue points is not a multiple of
p
, then we define its surplus as the minimum number of blue points whose removal makes the number of blue points a multiple of
p
, and we define its deficit as the minimum number of blue points whose addition makes the number of blue points to be a multiple of
p
. Formally, we define them as follows.

•

Surplus of
D_{i}
,
\textit{s}{(D_{i})}
: For any cluster
D_{i}\subseteq V
,
\textit{s}{(D_{i})}=|\textit{blue}(D_{i})|\mod p
.

•

Deficit of
D_{i}
,
\textit{d}(D_{i})
: For any cluster
D_{i}\subseteq V
if
p\nmid|\textit{blue}(D_{i})|
then
\textit{d}(D_{i})=p-\textit{s}{(D_{i})}
otherwise
\textit{d}(D_{i})=0
.

We call the operation of taking some points,
S
(say) from a cluster
D_{i}
, that is
D_{i}=D_{i}\setminus S
as
cut
and the operation of merging some points
M
(say) to a cluster
D_{i}
that is
D_{i}=D_{i}\cup M
as
merge
. Let us define some more terms that we need to describe our algorithms.

•

Cut cost,
\kappa(D_{i})
: For any cluster
D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}
,
\kappa(D_{i})=(\textit{s}{(D_{i})}\cdot(|D_{i}|-\textit{s}{(D_{i})})
.

•

Merge cost,
\mu(D_{i})
: For any cluster
D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}
,
\mu(D_{i})=d(D_{i})\cdot|D_{i}|
.

Description of the algorithm

Given a set,
\mathcal{D}
, we partition the clusters
D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}
into two types based on the
\textit{s}{(D_{i})}
value of the cluster
D_{i}
. If
\textit{s}{(D_{i})}\leq p/2
we call these clusters as
cut
clusters and if
\textit{s}{(D_{i})}>p/2
we call these clusters as
merge
clusters. Thus

•

\textsc{cut}=\{D_{i}\mid\textit{s}{(D_{i})}\leq p/2\}
.

•

\textsc{merge}=\{D_{i}\mid\textit{s}{(D_{i})}>p/2\}
.

We first sort the clusters
D_{i}\in\textsc{merge}
based on the value of
(\kappa(D_{i})-\mu(D_{i}))
in
non-increasing
order. Our algorithm
AlgoforGeneral()
cuts a set of
\textit{s}{(D_{i})}
blue points from each cluster
D_{i}\in\textsc{cut}
and merges them to clusters in
merge
sequentially starting from the beginning in the sorted list. In each cluster
D_{j}\in\textsc{merge}
, we merge a set of
\textit{d}(D_{j})
blue points. Now, we have two cases: after a certain point of time, the remaining set of clusters in
cut
becomes empty, and we are only left with some clusters in
merge
; in this case, we call our subroutine
AlgoforMerge()
. To remove any ambiguity, we refer to the remaining clusters in
merge
at this point as
\textsc{merge}^{\prime}
, and the set of clusters created from
cut
after cutting the surplus points
s(D_{i})
from each cluster
D_{i}\in\textsc{cut}
be
newcut
.

Another case is when the remaining set of clusters in
merge
becomes empty, and we are only left with some clusters in
cut
. In this case, we call our subroutine
AlgoforCut()
. Again, To remove any ambiguity, we refer to the remaining set of clusters in
cut
as
\textsc{cut}^{\prime}
.

The subroutine
AlgoforMerge()
takes two sets
\textsc{newcut},\textsc{merge}^{\prime}
as input. In
AlgoforMerge()
we calculate the sum of
\textit{d}(D_{i})
for each of the remaining clusters
D_{i}\in\textsc{merge}^{\prime}
(where
\textsc{merge}^{\prime}
is the remaining set of
merge
clusters). Let,
W=\sum_{D_{j}\in\textsc{merge}^{\prime}}\textit{d}(D_{j})
. Now, each cluster
D_{k}\in\textsc{newcut}\cup\textsc{merge}^{\prime}
the set
\textit{blue}(D_{k})
is divided into
(|\textit{blue}(D_{k})|-\textit{s}{(D_{k})})/p
subsets of size
p
and one subset of size
\textit{s}{(D_{k})}
. Let us number the subset of size
\textit{s}{(D_{k})}
as
0
, and all other subsets of size
p
are numbered arbitrarily. Let us denote the
z
th subset of a cluster
D_{k}
by
W_{k,z}
.

Each of these subsets is attached with a cost,

\displaystyle\kappa_{0}(D_{j})=\textit{s}{(D_{j})}\left(|D_{j}|-\textit{s}{(D_{j})}\right)

cutting cost for
0
th subset

\displaystyle\kappa_{z}(D_{j})=p\left(|D_{j}|-(zp+\textit{s}{(D_{j})})\right)

cutting cost for
z
th subset of size
p
where
z\neq 0

The algorithm
AlgoforMerge()
is called when the remaining set of clusters are in
merge
. In this case, ideally, we would like to merge in every cluster because, for the clusters
D_{i}\in\textsc{merge}
, we have
\textit{s}{(D_{i})}>p/2
and thus
\mu(D_{i})<\kappa(D_{i})
but to make in each cluster
|\textit{blue}(D_{i})|
a multiple of
p
we must cut from some cluster. So, now we would like to cut from those clusters
D_{i}
where
\kappa(D_{i})-\mu(D_{i})
is minimum because even if we pay the
cut-cost
for
D_{i}
, it is not too much compared to the
merge-cost
of
D_{i}
. After the
AlgoforGeneral()
is over, there are clusters where the number of blue points is already a multiple of
p
, and it may happen that it is better to cut of subset of size
p
from those clusters rather than from the cluster
D_{i}
which has the minimum
\kappa(D_{i})-\mu(D_{i})
. Now, we describe the algorithm
AlgoforMerge()
step by step.

At each iteration of the algorithm, among the clusters
D_{k}\in\textsc{newcut}\cup\textsc{merge}^{\prime}
, the algorithm chooses the cluster
D_{k}
if the
cost
(W_{k,v_{k}})
is minimum among all clusters. Here,
v_{k}
denotes the least numbered subset that is not cut from
D_{k}
.
cost
(W_{k,v_{k}})
is defined as follows

\displaystyle\textit{cost}(W_{k,v_{k}})

\displaystyle=\kappa_{v_{k}}(D_{k})\,\,\text{for $v_{k}\geq 1$}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{0}(D_{k})-\mu(D_{k})\,\,\text{for $v_{k}=0$}

(9)

We call
\textit{cost}(W_{k,v_{k}})
as the cost attached with the
v_{k}
th subset of
D_{k}
.

If multiple clusters have a minimum cost, then the algorithm chooses one of them arbitrarily. After that, the algorithm cuts a subset
W_{k,v_{k}}
from the cluster
D_{k}
. Then it deletes
D_{k}
from the set
\textsc{merge}^{\prime}
and then merges that subset sequentially starting from the first cluster in the set
\textsc{merge}^{\prime}
which is sorted according to the
\kappa(D_{i})-\mu(D_{i})
of the clusters
D_{i}
. We keep on doing the above operation until the total deficit,
W
of the clusters reaches
0
. Later, we prove that to make the total deficit zero we need to cut exactly
W/p
such subsets.

The subroutine
AlgoforCut()
takes the set
\textsc{cut}^{\prime}
as input. In
AlgoforCut()
, we cut a set of
\textit{s}{(D_{i})}
points from each of the clusters
D_{i}\in\textsc{cut}^{\prime}
, and with these surplus points, we create
extra
clusters of size
p
. We do this operation sequentially until the number of blue points becomes a multiple of
p
in all the clusters.

We provide the pseudocode of the algorithms
AlgoforGeneral()
,
AlgoforCut()
,
AlgoforMerge()
in
Section

C.1
,
Section

C.1
and
Section

C.1
respectively. Let the output of the algorithm
AlgoforGeneral()
be
\mathcal{T}
.

Runtime analysis of
AlgoforGeneral()
:
The expensive step in the
AlgoforGeneral()
is that in the first step of the algorithm, we sort the clusters in
merge
which would take
O\left(|\textsc{merge}|\,\,\log(|\textsc{merge}|)\right)=O(n\log n)
time. The algorithm uses a point
v\in V
at most twice, once while cutting the point
v
from its parent cluster
D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}
(say) and another when merging the point
v
to some cluster
D_{j}\neq D_{i}
. Hence, the cutting and merging process of the algorithm can take at most
O(n)
time (recall,
|V|=n
).

In the subroutine
AlgoforMerge()
, at each iteration, the algorithm needs to find the cluster that has the minimum cost attached to its least-numbered subset. The find-Min operation can be done in
O(1)
by implementing a priority queue with a Fibonacci heap. Hence, overall the time complexity of
AlgoforGeneral()
is
O(|\textsc{merge}|\,\,\log(|\textsc{merge}|)+|V|)=O(n\log n)
.

{algorithm2e}
\DontPrintSemicolon

\text{AlgoforGeneral()}(\mathcal{D})
\KwData
Input set of clusters
\mathcal{D}
, in each
D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}
the surplus of
D_{i}
,
\textit{s}{(D_{i})}
is within
0<\textit{s}{(D_{i})}<p

\KwResult
A set of clusters
\mathcal{T}
, such that in each cluster
T_{i}\in\mathcal{T}
the number of blue points is a multiple of
p
.

\textsc{cut},\textsc{merge},\textsc{newcut},\mathcal{T}^{cm}\leftarrow\emptyset

\For

D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}

\lIf
\textit{s}{(D_{i})}=0

add
D_{i}
to the set
newcut

\ElseIf
\textit{s}{(D_{i})}<p/2

add
D_{i}
to the set
cut

\Else

add
D_{i}
to the set
merge

Sort the clusters in
D_{m}\in\textsc{merge}
based on their
(\kappa(D_{m})-\mu(D_{m}))
in non-increasing order

\While

\textsc{cut}\neq\emptyset
and
\textsc{merge}\neq\emptyset

\For
D_{i}\in\textsc{cut}

\For
D_{j}\in\textsc{merge}

Let,
k=min(\textit{s}{(D_{i})},\textit{d}(D_{j}))
.

cut a set of
k
blue points from
D_{i}
and add to
D_{j}

\If
k=\textit{s}{(D_{i})}

\textsc{cut}=\textsc{cut}\setminus\{D_{i}\}
.

\textsc{newcut}=\textsc{newcut}\cup D_{i}

\If

k=\textit{d}(D_{j})

\textsc{merge}=\textsc{merge}\setminus\{D_{j}\}
.

\mathcal{T}^{cm}=\mathcal{T}^{cm}\cup D_{j}

\If
\textsc{cut}=\emptyset

\Return
\mathcal{T}^{cm}\cup\text{AlgoforMerge()}(\textsc{newcut},\textsc{merge})

\Else

\Return
\mathcal{T}^{cm}\cup\textsc{newcut}\cup\text{AlgoforCut()}(\textsc{cut})

{algorithm2e}
\DontPrintSemicolon

\text{AlgoforCut()}(\textsc{cut}^{\prime})
\KwData
Set of clusters
\textsc{cut}^{\prime}
, in each cluster
C_{i}\in\textsc{cut}^{\prime}
the surplus of
C_{i}
,
\textit{s}{(C_{i})}\leq p/2
.

\KwResult
Set of clusters
\mathcal{T}^{c}
, such that in each cluster
T_{i}\in\mathcal{T}^{c}
the number of blue points is a multiple of
p
.
ExtraSum =
\sum\textit{s}{(D_{i})}

m=\frac{ExtraSum}{p}

Initialize
m
many sets
E_{1},E_{2},\ldots E_{m}
to
\emptyset

\tcp
m
many extra clusters
Initialize
m
many variables
\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ldots\ell_{n}
to
p

\tcp
leftover space of extra clusters
Boolean
flag=0

\For
C_{i}\in\textsc{cut}^{\prime}

\For
j=1
to
n

Add
k=min(\ell_{j},\textit{s}{(C_{i})})
many blue points from
C_{i}
to the cluster
E_{j}

\ell_{j}=\ell_{j}-k

\Return
\textsc{cut}^{\prime}\cup\{E_{1},E_{2},\ldots,E_{m}\}

{algorithm2e}
\DontPrintSemicolon

\text{AlgoforMerge()}(\textsc{newcut},\textsc{merge}^{\prime})
\KwData
Two clustering
newcut
and
\textsc{merge}^{\prime}
such that for all clusters
D_{i}\in\textsc{newcut}
we have
p\mid|\textit{blue}(D_{i})|
and for clusters
D_{j}\in\textsc{merge}^{\prime}
we have
s(D_{j})>p/2
respectively. In
\textsc{merge}^{\prime}
, the clusters
D_{j}\in\textsc{merge}^{\prime}
are sorted based on their
(\kappa(D_{j})-\mu(D_{j}))

\KwResult
A clustering
\mathcal{T}
, such that for all clusters
T_{i}\in\mathcal{T}
,
p\mid\textit{blue}(T_{i})
.

W\leftarrow\sum_{D_{j}\in\textsc{merge}^{\prime}}\textit{d}(D_{j})

\For
D_{k}\in(\textsc{newcut}\cup\textsc{merge}^{\prime})

Initialize a variable
v_{k}
to
0

\While

W\neq 0

Take the cluster
D_{k}\in(\textsc{newcut}\cup\textsc{merge}^{\prime})
for which
\textit{cost}(W_{k,v_{k}})
is minimum

\tcp

\textit{cost}(W_{k,v_{k}})=\kappa_{v_{k}}(D_{k})
if
v_{k}\geq 1
, for
v_{k}=0

\textit{cost}(W_{k,v_{k}})=\kappa_{0}(D_{k})-\mu(D_{k})

\While
\textit{size}(W_{k,v_{k}})\neq 0

\tcp

\textit{size}(W_{k,v_{k}})
is the size
v_{k}
th subset of
D_{k}

\For
D_{j}\in\textsc{merge}^{\prime}

\gamma=\min(\textit{d}(D_{j}),\textit{size}(W_{k,v_{k}}))

cut
\gamma
many blue points from
D_{k}
and merge to
D_{j}

\If
\gamma=\textit{d}(D_{j})

\textit{size}(W_{k,v_{k}})=\textit{size}(W_{k,v_{k}})-\gamma

\If
\gamma=\textit{size}(W_{k,v_{k}}))

\textit{d}(D_{j})=\textit{d}(D_{j})-\gamma

W=W-p

v_{k}=v_{k}+1

\For

D_{j}\in\textsc{merge}^{\prime}

\If
\textit{d}(D_{j})=0

update
\textsc{merge}^{\prime}=\textsc{merge}^{\prime}\setminus\{D_{j}\}

\Return
\mathcal{T}=\textsc{newcut}\cup\textsc{merge}^{\prime}

C.2
Approximation Guarantee

In this section, we prove that
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T})\leq 3.5\,\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*})
which is stated formally in
Theorem

20

Our proof is divided into the following two cases:

•

Merge
case: When
\sum_{D_{i}\in\textsc{cut}}\textit{s}{(D_{i})}\leq\sum_{D_{j}\in\textsc{merge}}\textit{d}(D_{j})
; and

•

Cut
case: When
\sum_{D_{i}\in\textsc{cut}}\textit{s}{(D_{i})}>\sum_{D_{j}\in\textsc{merge}}\textit{d}(D_{j})
.

In
Section

C.2.2
, we argue that in the merge case
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T})\leq 3\,\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*})
.

Lemma 22
.

If
\sum_{D_{i}\in\textsc{cut}}\textit{s}{(D_{i})}\leq\sum_{D_{j}\in\textsc{merge}}\textit{d}(D_{j})
, the algorithm
AlgoforGeneral()
produces a
Balanced clustering

\mathcal{T}
such that
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T})\leq 3\;\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*})
.

In
Section

C.2.3
, we argue that in the cut case
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T})\leq 3.5\,\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*})
.

Lemma 23
.

If
\sum_{D_{i}\in\textsc{cut}}\textit{s}{(D_{i})}>\sum_{D_{j}\in\textsc{merge}}\textit{d}(D_{j})
, the algorithm
AlgoforGeneral()
produces a
Balanced clustering

\mathcal{T}
such that
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T})\leq 3.5\;\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*})
.

It is straightforward to see that to conclude the proof of
Theorem

20
, it suffices to show
Lemma

22
and
Lemma

23
. In
Section

C.2.1
, we discuss some important properties of
\mathcal{T}^{*}
that we need to prove
Lemma

22
and
Lemma

23
. In
Section

C.2.2
we prove
Lemma

22
and in
Section

C.2.3
we prove
Lemma

23
.

C.2.1
Structure of
\mathcal{T}^{*}

To prove
Lemma

22
and
Lemma

23
, we need an important property about the structure of
\mathcal{T}^{*}
. We need to define some notations to state that important property of
\mathcal{T}^{*}
.

Consider any point
v\in V
. Suppose in the clustering
\mathcal{D}
,
v\in D_{i}
, for some
i
, and in the clustering
\mathcal{T}^{*}
,
v\in T^{*}_{j}
, for some
j
. Note, the number of pairs
(u,v)
such that
u\in D_{i}
but
v\notin T^{*}_{j}
is given by

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{T^{*}_{k}\neq T^{*}_{j}}\left\lvert D_{i}\cap T^{*}_{k}\right\rvert

(10)

Similarly the number of pairs
(u,v)
such that
u\notin D_{i}
but
v\in T^{*}_{j}
is given by

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{D_{k}\neq D_{i}}\left\lvert D_{k}\cap T^{*}_{j}\right\rvert

(11)

Thus by the definition of the distance function, we have

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*})=\sum\limits_{v\in V}1/2\cdot\left(\sum\limits_{k\mid T^{*}_{k}\neq T^{*}_{j}}\left\lvert D_{i}\cap T^{*}_{k}\right\rvert+\sum\limits_{k\mid D_{k}\neq D_{i}}\left\lvert D_{k}\cap T^{*}_{j}\right\rvert\right)

(12)

.

The
1/2
comes before the expressions
10
and
11
because we are counting a pair
(u,v)
twice inside these expressions.
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*})
counts the total number of pairs that are together in
\mathcal{D}
but separated by
\mathcal{T}^{*}
and the pairs that are in different clusters in
\mathcal{D}
but together in
\mathcal{T}^{*}
.

Let us denote the cost paid by
\mathcal{T}^{*}
for a vertex
v\in V
that belongs to
D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}
for some
i
and also to
T^{*}_{j}\in\mathcal{T}^{*}
for some
j
by the notation
\textsc{OPT}_{v}
where

\textsc{OPT}_{v}=1/2\cdot\left(\sum\limits_{k\mid T^{*}_{k}\neq T^{*}_{j}}\left\lvert D_{i}\cap T^{*}_{k}\right\rvert+\sum\limits_{k\mid D_{k}\neq D_{i}}\left\lvert D_{k}\cap T^{*}_{j}\right\rvert\right)

.

Let us denote the cost paid by
\mathcal{T}^{*}
for a cluster
D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}
by

\textsc{OPT}_{D_{i}}=\sum_{v\in D_{i}}\textsc{OPT}_{v}

.

Now, we are ready to state the important property of
\mathcal{T}^{*}
. We show that if for a cluster
D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}
, if
\textit{s}{(D_{i})}\leq p/2
then

\textsc{OPT}_{D_{i}}\geq s(D_{i})(|D_{i}|-s(D_{i}))+\frac{1}{2}s(D_{i})(p-s(D_{i}))

that is
\mathcal{T}^{*}
must pay at least the cost
s(D_{i})(|D_{i}|-s(D_{i}))+1/2s(D_{i})(p-s(D_{i}))
for the cluster
D_{i}
if the surplus of
D_{i}
is less than or equal to
p/2
.

Again if
\textit{s}{(D_{i})}>p/2
then

\textsc{OPT}_{D_{i}}\geq(p-s(D_{i}))(|D_{i}|-s(D_{i}))+\frac{1}{2}s(D_{i})(p-s(D_{i}))

that is
\mathcal{T}^{*}
must pay at least the cost
(p-s(D_{i}))(|D_{i}|-s(D_{i}))+\frac{1}{2}s(D_{i})(p-s(D_{i}))
for the cluster
D_{i}
if the surplus of
D_{i}
is greater than
p/2
.

We state this formally in the following lemma.

Lemma 24
.

For a cluster
D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}
we have

1.

if
\textit{s}{(D_{i})}\leq p/2
then
\textsc{OPT}_{D_{i}}\geq s(D_{i})(|D_{i}|-s(D_{i}))+\frac{1}{2}s(D_{i})(p-s(D_{i}))
.

2.

if
\textit{s}{(D_{i})}>p/2
then
\textsc{OPT}_{D_{i}}\geq(p-s(D_{i}))(|D_{i}|-s(D_{i}))+\frac{1}{2}s(D_{i})(p-s(D_{i}))
.

For proving
Lemma

24
, we need the help of the following proposition.

Proposition 25
.

Consider any integer
p\geq 2
, and
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{t}\in[p]
, for
t\geq 1
. Let
s=\sum_{i=1}^{t}\alpha_{i}\mod p
. Then

\sum_{i=1}^{t}\alpha_{i}\left(p-\alpha_{i}\right)\geq s\left(p-s\right)
.

Proof:
Suppose,
(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})\mod p=s_{1}
, hence
(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})=kp+s_{1}
where
k\in\{0,1\}
.

First, we prove

\alpha_{1}(p-\alpha_{1})+\alpha_{2}(p-\alpha_{2})\geq s_{1}(p-s_{1})

Now,

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}(p-\alpha_{1})+\alpha_{2}(p-\alpha_{2})

\displaystyle=\,\,

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}p-\alpha_{1}^{2}+\alpha_{2}p-\alpha_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\,\,

\displaystyle p(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})-(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})^{2}+2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}

\displaystyle=\,\,

\displaystyle p(kp+s_{1})-(kp+s_{1})^{2}+2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}

\displaystyle(\text{Replacing}\,\,(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})\,\,\text{with}\,\,(kp+s_{1}))

\displaystyle=\,\,

\displaystyle\left(kp+s_{1}\right)\left(p-\left(kp+s_{1}\right)\right)+2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}

(13)

Case
1
: When
k=0
then
Eq.

13
becomes

s_{1}(p-s_{1})+2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\geq s_{1}(p-s_{1})

Case
2
: When
k=1
then
Eq.

13
becomes

\displaystyle(p+s_{1})(-s_{1})+2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}

\displaystyle=\,\,

\displaystyle(p-s_{1})s_{1}+2(\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}-s_{1}p)

\displaystyle=\,\,

\displaystyle(p-s_{1})s_{1}+2(\left(s_{1}p+r(p-r-s_{1})\right)-s_{1}p)

\displaystyle(\text{Replacing}\,\,\alpha_{1}\,\,\text{with}\,\,(p-r)\,\,\text{and}\,\,\alpha_{2}\,\,\text{with}\,\,(s_{1}+r))

\displaystyle=\,\,

\displaystyle(p-s_{1})s_{1}+2(\left(s_{1}p+r(p-\alpha_{2})\right)-s_{1}p)

\displaystyle\geq\,\,

\displaystyle(p-s_{1})s_{1}

\displaystyle(\textbf{as}\,\,\alpha_{2}\in[p])

Hence, we get

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}(p-\alpha_{1})+\alpha_{2}(p-\alpha_{2})\geq s_{1}(p-s_{1})

\displaystyle(\text{where}\,\,s_{1}=(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})\mod p)

(14)

Now,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{t}\alpha_{i}\left(p-\alpha_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{1}(p-\alpha_{1})+\alpha_{2}(p-\alpha_{2})+\sum_{i=3}^{t}\alpha_{i}(p-\alpha_{i})

\displaystyle\geq s_{1}(p-s_{1})+\alpha_{3}(p-\alpha_{3})+\sum_{i=4}^{t}\alpha_{i}(p-\alpha_{i})

\displaystyle(\text{using}\,\,\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{equn:prop-main},\,\,\text{assume}\,\,s_{1}=(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})\mod p)

\displaystyle\geq s_{2}(p-s_{2})+\alpha_{4}(p-\alpha_{4})+\sum_{i=5}^{t}\alpha_{i}(p-\alpha_{i})

\displaystyle(\text{using}\,\,\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{equn:prop-main},\,\,\text{assume}\,\,s_{2}=(s_{1}+\alpha_{3})\mod p)

\displaystyle\geq s(p-s)

\displaystyle(\text{Proved})

\jmlrQED

Proposition 26
.

Given positive integers
n
,
p
, and
x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{t}
such that
n=x_{1}+x_{2}+\dots+x_{t}
. Let
b,b_{1},b_{2},\dots,b_{t}<p
be nonnegative integers satisfying
b\leq n
,
b_{i}\leq x_{i}
, and
b=(b_{1}+b_{2}+\dots+b_{t})(\mod{p})
. Let
q_{i}=p-b_{i}(\mod{p})
. Consider
A=\sum_{i<j}x_{i}x_{j}+\sum_{i=1}^{t}(x_{i}-b_{i})q_{i}
. Then

1.

If
b\leq p/2
, then
A\geq b(n-b)
.

2.

If
b>p/2
, then
A\geq(p-b)(n-b)
.

Proof:
Without loss of generality, assume that
x_{1}\leq x_{2}\leq\dots\leq x_{t}
.

(
1
) Suppose that
b\leq p/2
. We show that
A\geq b(n-b)
.

As
x_{t}=\max\{x_{i}\}
, it follows that
A\geq\sum_{i<j}x_{i}x_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{t}x_{i}\left(\dfrac{n-x_{i}}{2}\right)\geq n\left(\dfrac{n-x_{t}}{2}\right)
. Additionally,
A\geq x_{t}(n-x_{t})+(x_{t}-b_{t})q_{t}
. Let
B=n\left(\dfrac{n-x_{t}}{2}\right)
, and
C=x_{t}(n-x_{t})+(x_{t}-b_{t})q_{t}
. Then
A\geq B
and
A\geq C
. Therefore, it suffices so that
B\geq b(n-b)
or
C\geq b(n-b)
. We proceed by cases.

Case 1
:
x_{t}\leq b
.

•

If
n\leq x_{t}+b
, then
C-b(n-b)>x_{t}(n-x_{t})-b(n-b)=(x_{t}-b)(n-x_{t}-b)\geq 0
.

•

If
n>x_{t}+b
, then

\displaystyle B-b(n-b)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}(n(n-x_{t})-2b(n-b))

\displaystyle\geq\dfrac{1}{2}(n(n-x_{t})-b(n-b)-b(n-x_{t}))

\displaystyle(\text{as }b\geq x_{t})

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}(n-b)(n-x_{t}-b)

\displaystyle\geq 0.

\displaystyle(\text{as }n\geq b\text{ and }n>x_{t}+b)

Case 2:

x_{t}>b
.

•

If
n\geq x_{t}+b
, then
C-b(n-b)>x_{t}(n-x_{t})-b(n-b)=(x_{t}-b)(n-x_{t}-b)\geq 0
.

•

If
n<x_{t}+b
, then
b>n-x_{t}=x_{1}+x_{2}+\dots+x_{t-1}\geq b_{1}+b_{2}+\dots+b_{t-1}
. Note that
b=(b_{1}+b_{2}+\dots+b_{t})(\mod{p})
. It follows that
b_{t}=b-(b_{1}+b_{2}+\dots+b_{t-1})(\mod{p})=b-(b_{1}+b_{2}+\dots+b_{t-1})
. Hence,
0<b_{t}<b
and
q_{t}=p-b_{t}(\mod p)=p-b_{t}>p-b\geq b
, as
b\leq p/2
. This leads to

\displaystyle C-b(n-b)

\displaystyle=(x_{t}-b)(n-x_{t}-b)+(x_{t}-b_{t})q_{t}

\displaystyle>(x_{t}-b)(n-x_{t}-b)+(x_{t}-b)q_{t}

\displaystyle(\text{as }b_{t}<b)

\displaystyle=(x_{t}-b)(n-x_{t}-b+q_{t})

\displaystyle\geq 0.

\displaystyle(\text{as }x_{t}\geq b,n\geq x_{t},\text{ and }q_{t}>b)

(
2
) Suppose that
b>p/2
. We show that
A\geq(p-b)(n-b)=a(n-b)
, where
a=p-b\leq p/2<b
. As before, we have
A\geq B
and
A\geq C
, where
B=n\left(\dfrac{n-x_{t}}{2}\right)
, and
C=x_{t}(n-x_{t})+(x_{t}-b_{t})q_{t}
. Similarly, we proceed by cases.

Case 1:

x_{t}\leq a
.

•

If
n\leq x_{t}+a
, then
C-a(n-b)\geq x_{t}(n-x_{t})-a(n-a)=(x_{t}-a)(n-x_{t}-a)\geq 0
.

•

If
n>x_{t}+a
, then

\displaystyle B-a(n-b)

\displaystyle\geq\dfrac{1}{2}(n(n-x_{t})-2a(n-a))

\displaystyle\geq\dfrac{1}{2}(n(n-x_{t})-a(n-a)-a(n-x_{t}))

\displaystyle(\text{as }a\geq x_{t})

\displaystyle=(n-a)(n-x_{t}-a)

\displaystyle\geq 0.

\displaystyle(\text{as }n\geq a\text{ and }n\geq x_{t}+a).

Case 2:

x_{t}>a
.

•

If
n\geq x_{t}+a
, then
C-a(n-b)\geq x_{t}(n-x_{t})-a(n-a)=(x_{t}-a)(n-x_{t}-a)\geq 0
.

•

If
n<x_{t}+a
, then
a>n-x_{t}=x_{1}+x_{2}+\dots+x_{t-1}\geq b_{1}+b_{2}+\dots+b_{t-1}
. Note that
b=(b_{1}+b_{2}+\dots+b_{t})(\mod{p})
. It follows that
b_{t}=b-(b_{1}+b_{2}+\dots+b_{t-1})(\mod{p})=b-(b_{1}+b_{2}+\dots+b_{t-1})
. Hence,
0<b_{t}<b
, and
q_{t}=p-b_{t}(\mod{p})=p-b_{t}
. Therefore,
q_{t}=p-b+b_{1}+b_{2}+\dots b_{t-1}\geq p-b=a
. If
b_{t}\leq a
, it follows that

\displaystyle C-a(n-b)

\displaystyle\geq x_{t}(n-x_{t})+(x_{t}-b_{t})q_{t}-a(n-b)

\displaystyle\geq(x_{t}-a)(n-x_{t}-a)-(x_{t}-a)q_{t}

\displaystyle(\text{as }b_{t}<a)

\displaystyle=(x_{t}-a)(n-x_{t}+q_{t}-a)

\displaystyle\geq 0.

\displaystyle(\text{as }x_{t}>a,\ n\geq x_{t}\text{ and }q_{t}\geq a)

It remains to consider
b_{t}>a
. We have

\displaystyle\>C-a(n-b)

\displaystyle=\left(x_{t}(n-x_{t})-b_{t}(n-a)+x_{t}(b_{t}-a)\right)+b_{t}(n-a)-x_{t}(b_{t}-a)+(x_{t}-b_{t})q_{t}-a(n-b)

\displaystyle=(x_{t}-b_{t})(n-x_{t}-a)+(b_{t}-a)(n-x_{t})+a(b-b_{t})+(x_{t}-b_{t})q_{t}

\displaystyle\geq(x_{t}-b_{t})(n-x_{t}-a+q_{t})+(b_{t}-a)(n-x_{t})+a(b-b_{t})

\displaystyle\geq 0.

This concludes the proof.

\jmlrQED

Now we are ready to prove
Lemma

24

Proof of
Lemma

24
:

Suppose in
\mathcal{T}^{*}
, the cluster
D_{i}
is split into
t
parts
X_{i,1},\ldots,X_{i,t}
; more specifically,

•

D_{i}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{t}X_{i,j}
, and

•

\forall j\neq k\in[t]
,
X_{i,j}\cap X_{i,k}=\emptyset
.

For each part
X_{i,j}
, consider a set
S_{i,j}\subseteq X_{i,j}
that consists of
(\textit{blue}(X_{i,j})\mod p)
many blue points. Now, since
\mathcal{T}^{*}
is a
Balanced clustering
we get for the clusters
X_{i,k}
for
k\in\{1,\ldots,t\}
at least
(p-|S_{i,k}|)
blue points from clusters other than
D_{i}
must be merged. Let, these set of
(p-|S_{i,k}|)
blue points be
S_{i,k}^{\prime}
. Hence we get,

\displaystyle\textsc{OPT}_{D_{i}}\geq\sum_{j<k}|X_{i,j}||X_{i,k}|+\sum_{k=1}^{t}|X_{i,k}\setminus S_{i,k}||S_{i,k}^{\prime}|+1/2\sum_{k=1}^{t}|S_{i,k}||S_{i,k}^{\prime}|

(15)

\displaystyle\text{Note that}\,\,\sum_{k=1}^{t}|S_{i,k}|\mod p=s_{i}

recall
\textit{s}{(D_{i})}=s_{i}

Hence, from
Proposition

25
we get,

\displaystyle 1/2\sum_{k=1}^{t}|S_{i,k}||S_{i,k}^{\prime}|

\displaystyle=\,\,

\displaystyle 1/2\sum_{k=1}^{t}|S_{i,k}|(p-|S_{i,k}|)

\displaystyle\geq\,\,

\displaystyle 1/2\,\,s_{i}\cdot(p-s_{i})

(16)

It suffices to show that

\displaystyle\sum_{j<k}|X_{i,j}||X_{i,k}|+\sum_{k=1}^{t}|X_{i,k}\setminus S_{i,k}||S^{\prime}_{i,k}|\geq\textit{s}{(D_{i})}(|D_{i}|-\textit{s}{(D_{i})}),

(17)

when
\textit{s}{(D_{i})}\leq p/2
, and

\displaystyle\sum_{j<k}|X_{i,j}||X_{i,k}|+\sum_{k=1}^{t}|X_{i,k}\setminus S_{i,k}||S^{\prime}_{i,k}|\geq(p-\textit{s}{(D_{i})})(|D_{i}|-\textit{s}{(D_{i})}),

(18)

when
\textit{s}{(D_{i})}>p/2
.

To this end, we let
|D_{i}|=n,\ \textit{s}{(D_{i})}=b<p
,
|X_{i,j}|=x_{j}
,
|S_{i,j}|=b_{j}<p
, and
|S^{\prime}_{i,j}|=q_{j}
. Then,
n=x_{1}+x_{2}+\dots x_{t}
,
b=(b_{1}+b_{2}+\dots+b_{t})(\mod{p})
,
q_{j}=(p-b_{j})(\mod{p})
, and
x_{j}\geq b_{j}
, for all
j
. This allows us to apply
Proposition

26
, which claims the correctness of (
17
) and (
18
).

\jmlrQED

C.2.2
Approximation guarantee in the merge case

In this section, we prove that if
\sum_{D_{i}\in\textsc{cut}}\textit{s}{(D_{i})}\leq\sum_{D_{i}\in\textsc{merge}}\textit{d}(D_{i})
, that is in the merge case, the distance between input clustering
\mathcal{D}
and the output clustering
\mathcal{T}
of the algorithm
AlgoforGeneral()
,
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T})
is at most
3\,\,\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*})
. That we restate formally below.

See
22

To prove
Lemma

22
, here we need to recall and also define some costs paid by our algorithm
AlgoforGeneral()
. For a cluster
T_{k}\in\mathcal{T}
we define
\pi(T_{k})
as a parent of
T_{k}
iff
\pi(T_{k})\in\mathcal{D}
and
T_{k}
is formed either by cutting some points from
\pi(T_{k})
or by merging some points to
\pi(T_{k})
.

•

cut-algo
: The set of clusters
D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}
from where the algorithm has cut some blue points.

•

merge-algo
: The set of clusters
D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}
to where the algorithm has merged some blue points. We have
\textsc{merge-algo}=\mathcal{D}\setminus\textsc{cut-algo}
.

•

Cost of cutting the
z
th subset of size
p
from
D_{i}
where
z\in\{0,\ldots,(|\textit{blue}(D_{i})|-s_{i})/p\}
:

–

\kappa_{0}(D_{i})=s_{i}(|D_{i}|-s_{i})
.

–

For
z\in\{1,\ldots,(|\textit{blue}(D_{i})|-s_{i})/p\}
:
\kappa_{z}(D_{i})=p\,(|D_{i}|-(s_{i}+zp))
.

Let
y_{i,z}
take the value
1
if the algorithm cuts the
z
th subset from
D_{i}
.
Hence, we define

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{T})=\sum_{D_{i}\in\textsc{cut-algo}}\sum_{z=0}^{t}y_{i,z}\kappa_{z}(D_{i})

(19)

•

Cost of merging in a cluster
D_{j}
which is
\mu(D_{j})
.
Hence, we define

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{2}(\mathcal{T})=\sum_{D_{j}\in\textsc{merge-algo}}\textit{d}(D_{j})|D_{j}|

(20)

•

Inter-cluster cost paid by the vertices that belong to a subset of size
p
of
D_{i}
that we cut from
D_{i}
in the algorithm
AlgoforMerge()
:

–

(Intercluster cost)
\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{T})
:
\sum\limits_{T_{k}\in\mathcal{T}}|(T_{k}\setminus\pi(T_{k}))\setminus D_{i}||(T_{k}\setminus\pi(T_{k}))\cap D_{i}|
.

If the algorithm
AlgoforGeneral()
cuts a subset
W_{i,z}
from a cluster
D_{i}
then
we can upper bound
\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{T})
by

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{T})\leq|W_{i,z}|(p-|W_{i,z}|)

(21)

•

Intracluster cost within the vertices that belong to a subset of size
p
of
D_{i}
that we cut from
D_{i}
in the algorithm
AlgoforMerge()
:

–

(Intracluster cost)
\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{T})
:
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}T_{k},T_{j}\in\mathcal{T}\\
k\neq j\end{subarray}}|(T_{k}\setminus\pi(T_{k}))\cap D_{i}||(T_{j}\setminus\pi(T_{j}))\cap D_{i})|
.

If the algorithm
AlgoforGeneral()
cuts a subset
W_{i,z}
from a cluster
D_{i}
and it further gets split into parts
W^{(1)}_{i,z},W^{(2)}_{i,z},\ldots,W^{(t)}_{i,z}
then
we can upper bound
\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{T})
by

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{T})\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{t}|W^{(k)}_{i,z}|(|W_{i,z}|-|W^{(k)}_{i,z}|)

(22)

Now we prove the following claim that we need to prove
Lemma

22
.

Claim 1
.

Suppose in
\mathcal{T}^{*}
, a cluster
D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}
gets split into
t
parts
X_{i,1},X_{i,2},\ldots,X_{i,t}
more specifically,

•

X_{i,j}\subseteq T_{r_{j}}^{*}
for some
T_{r_{j}}^{*}\in\mathcal{T}^{*}
.

•

D_{i}=\bigcup\limits_{j=1}^{t}X_{i,j}
and

•

X_{i,j}\cap X_{i,k}=\emptyset\,\,\forall j\neq k
.

then

1.

Either
\exists j\in[t]
, such that
X_{i,j}=T_{r_{j}}^{*}
(for some
T_{r_{j}}^{*}\in\mathcal{T}^{*})
and
\textit{red}\left(D_{i}\right)\subseteq X_{i,j}
and
|X_{i,\ell}|<p

\forall\,\,\ell\neq j
.

2.

or
|X_{i,\ell}|<p
,
\forall\,\,\ell\in[t]
.

Now, to prove the previous claim, we need to prove the other claims below.

Claim 2
.

Consider a partition
X_{i,j}
of
D_{i}
. Suppose,
X_{i,j}\subseteq T_{r_{j}}^{*}
(for some
T_{r_{j}}^{*}\in\mathcal{T}^{*}
) then
|T_{r_{j}}^{*}\setminus X_{i,j}|\leq s_{i,j}
where
s_{i,j}=|\textit{blue}(X_{i,j})|\mod p
.

Proof:
Suppose for a partition
X_{i,j}
,
|T_{r_{j}}^{*}\setminus X_{i,j}|>s_{i,j}
. To prove this first we construct
\mathcal{M}
from
\mathcal{T}^{*}
such that
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{M})<\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*})
. This would contradict the fact that
\mathcal{T}^{*}
is the closest
Balanced clustering
and thus we will conclude
|T_{r_{j}}^{*}\setminus X_{i,j}|\leq s_{i,j}
.

Construction of
\mathcal{M}
from
\mathcal{T}^{*}
:

\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{T}^{*}\setminus\{T_{r_{j}}^{*}\}\cup\{(X_{i,j}\setminus S_{i,j}),(T_{r_{j}}^{*}\setminus(X_{i,j}\setminus S_{i,j}))\}
.

That is
\mathcal{M}
we remove the set
T_{r_{j}}^{*}
from
\mathcal{T}^{*}
and add two sets
(X_{i,j}\setminus S_{i,j})
and
(T_{r_{j}}^{*}\setminus(X_{i,j}\setminus S_{i,j}))
where
S_{i,j}\subseteq\textit{blue}(X_{i,j})
such that
|S_{i,j}|=s_{i,j}
.

Note that,

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{M})=\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*})+|X_{i,j}\setminus S_{i,j}||S_{i,j}|-|X_{i,j}\setminus S_{i,j}||T_{r_{j}}^{*}\setminus X_{i,j}|

This is because,

•

Reason behind the term
|X_{i,j}\setminus S_{i,j}||S_{i,j}|
: The pairs
(u,v)
such that
u\in(X_{i,j}\setminus S_{i,j})
and
v\in S_{i,j}
are not counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*})
because
u
and
v
are present in the same clusters
D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}
and
T_{r_{j}}^{*}\in\mathcal{T}^{*}
. In
\mathcal{M}
, since, these pairs
u
and
v
belongs to different clusters
(X_{i,j}\setminus S_{i,j})
and
(T_{r_{j}}^{*}\setminus(X_{i,j}\setminus S_{i,j}))
respectively, hence, these pairs are counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{M})
.

•

Reason behind the term
|X_{i,j}\setminus S_{i,j}||T_{r_{j}}^{*}\setminus X_{i,j}|
: The pairs
(u,v)
such that
u\in(X_{i,j}\setminus S_{i,j})
and
v\in T_{r_{j}}^{*}\setminus X_{i,j}
are counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*})
because such pairs
u
and
v
are present in the different clusters in
\mathcal{D}
but in the same cluster
T_{r_{j}}^{*}\in\mathcal{T}^{*}
. In
\mathcal{M}
, since, these pairs
u
and
v
belongs to different clusters
(X_{i,j}\setminus S_{i,j})
and
(T_{r_{j}}^{*}\setminus(X_{i,j}\setminus S_{i,j}))
respectively, hence, these pairs are not counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{M})
.

Now,

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{M})

\displaystyle=\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*})+|X_{i,j}\setminus S_{i,j}||S_{i,j}|-|X_{i,j}\setminus S_{i,j}||T_{r_{j}}^{*}\setminus X_{i,j}|

\displaystyle<\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*})+|X_{i,j}\setminus S_{i,j}|s_{i,j}-|X_{i,j}\setminus S_{i,j}|s_{i,j}

\displaystyle(\textbf{as}|S_{i,j}|=s_{i,j}\,\,\text{and}\,\,|\mathcal{T}^{*}\setminus X_{i,j}|>s_{i,j}).

\displaystyle=\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*}).

\jmlrQED

Claim 3
.

There exists at most one partition
X_{i,j}
such that
|X_{i,j}|\geq p

Proof:
Let us assume there exists two partitions
X_{i,j}
and
X_{i,j^{\prime}}
such that
|X_{i,j}|\geq p
,
|X_{i,j^{\prime}}|\geq p
and
X_{i,j}\subseteq T_{r_{j}}^{*}
and
X_{i,j^{\prime}}\subseteq T_{r_{j^{\prime}}}^{*}
for some
T_{r_{j}}^{*}
and
T_{r_{j}^{\prime}}^{*}
in
\mathcal{T}^{*}
.

Without loss of generality, assume,
|T_{r_{j}}^{*}\setminus X_{i,j}|\leq|T_{r_{j^{\prime}}}^{*}\setminus X_{i,j^{\prime}}|
. Now we construct
\mathcal{M}
from
\mathcal{T}^{*}
in such a way that
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{M})<\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*})
which is a contradiction, and thus we will conclude that there exists at most one partition
X_{i,j}
such that
|X_{i,j}|\geq p
.

Construction of
\mathcal{M}
from
\mathcal{T}^{*}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{T}^{*}\setminus\left\{T_{r_{j}}^{*},T_{r_{j^{\prime}}}^{*}\right\}\cup\left\{\left(T_{r_{j}}^{*}\cup(X_{i,j^{\prime}}\setminus S_{i,j^{\prime}})\right),\left(T_{r_{j^{\prime}}}^{*}\setminus(X_{i,j^{\prime}}\setminus S_{i,j^{\prime}})\right)\right\}

That is in
\mathcal{M}
we remove sets
T_{r_{j}}^{*}
and
T_{r_{j^{\prime}}}^{*}
from
\mathcal{T}^{*}
and add two sets
\left(T_{r_{j}}^{*}\cup(X_{i,j^{\prime}}\setminus S_{i,j^{\prime}})\right)
and
\left(T_{r_{j^{\prime}}}^{*}\setminus(X_{i,j^{\prime}}\setminus S_{i,j^{\prime}})\right)
where
S_{i,j^{\prime}}\subseteq\textit{blue}(X_{i,j^{\prime}})
such that
|S_{i,j^{\prime}}|=(\textit{blue}(X_{i,j^{\prime}})\mod p)
.

Note that,

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{M})=\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*})+|(X_{i,j^{\prime}}\setminus S_{i,j^{\prime}})||T_{r_{j}}^{*}\setminus X_{i,j}|+|(X_{i,j^{\prime}}\setminus S_{i,j^{\prime}})||S_{i,j^{\prime}}|

\displaystyle-|(X_{i,j^{\prime}}\setminus S_{i,j^{\prime}})||T_{r_{j^{\prime}}}^{*}\setminus X_{i,j^{\prime}}|-|(X_{i,j^{\prime}}\setminus S_{i,j^{\prime}})||X_{i,j}|

This is because

•

The reason behind the term
|(X_{i,j^{\prime}}\setminus S_{i,j^{\prime}})||T_{r_{j}}^{*}\setminus X_{i,j}|
:
The pairs
(u,v)
such that
u\in(X_{i,j^{\prime}}\setminus S_{i,j^{\prime}})
and
v\in T_{r_{j}}^{*}\setminus X_{i,j}
are not counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*})
because
u
and
v
are present in different clusters both in
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{T}^{*}
. In
\mathcal{M}
, since, these pairs
u
and
v
belong to the same cluster
\left(T_{r_{j}}^{*}\cup(X_{i,j^{\prime}}\setminus S_{i,j^{\prime}})\right)
. Hence, these pairs are counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{M})
.

•

The reason behind the term
|(X_{i,j^{\prime}}\setminus S_{i,j^{\prime}})||S_{i,j^{\prime}}|
:
The pairs
(u,v)
such that
u\in(X_{i,j^{\prime}}\setminus S_{i,j^{\prime}})
and
v\in S_{i,j^{\prime}}
are not counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*})
because
u
and
v
are present in the same clusters
D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}
and
T_{r_{j^{\prime}}}^{*}\in\mathcal{T}^{*}
. In
\mathcal{M}
, since, these pairs
u
and
v
belongs to different clusters
\left(T_{r_{j}}^{*}\cup(X_{i,j^{\prime}}\setminus S_{i,j^{\prime}})\right)
and
\left(T_{r_{j^{\prime}}}^{*}\setminus(X_{i,j^{\prime}}\setminus S_{i,j^{\prime}})\right)
respectively. Hence, these pairs are counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{M})
.

•

The reason behind the term
|(X_{i,j^{\prime}}\setminus S_{i,j^{\prime}})||T_{r_{j^{\prime}}}^{*}\setminus X_{i,j^{\prime}}|
:
The pairs
(u,v)
such that
u\in(X_{i,j^{\prime}}\setminus S_{i,j^{\prime}})
and
v\in T_{r_{j^{\prime}}}^{*}\setminus X_{i,j^{\prime}}
are counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*})
because such pairs
u
and
v
are present in the different clusters in
\mathcal{D}
but in the same cluster
T_{r_{j^{\prime}}}^{*}\in\mathcal{T}^{*}
. In
\mathcal{M}
, since, these pairs
u
and
v
belongs to different clusters
\left(T_{r_{j}}^{*}\cup(X_{i,j^{\prime}}\setminus S_{i,j^{\prime}})\right)
and
\left(T_{r_{j^{\prime}}}^{*}\setminus(X_{i,j^{\prime}}\setminus S_{i,j^{\prime}})\right)
respectively. Hence, these pairs are not counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{M})
.

•

The reason behind the term
|(X_{i,j^{\prime}}\setminus S_{i,j^{\prime}})||X_{i,j}|
:
The pairs
(u,v)
such that
u\in(X_{i,j^{\prime}}\setminus S_{i,j^{\prime}})
and
v\in X_{i,j}
are counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*})
because such pairs
u
and
v
are present in the same cluster
D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}
but in different clusters in
\mathcal{T}^{*}
. In
\mathcal{M}
, since, these pairs
u
and
v
belongs to same cluster
\left(T_{r_{j}}^{*}\cup(X_{i,j^{\prime}}\setminus S_{i,j^{\prime}})\right)
. Hence, these pairs are not counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{M})
.

Now,

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{M})

\displaystyle=\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*})+|(X_{i,j^{\prime}}\setminus S_{i,j^{\prime}})||T_{r_{j}}^{*}\setminus X_{i,j}|+|(X_{i,j^{\prime}}\setminus S_{i,j^{\prime}})||S_{i,j^{\prime}}|

\displaystyle-|(X_{i,j^{\prime}}\setminus S_{i,j^{\prime}})||T_{r_{j^{\prime}}}^{*}\setminus X_{i,j^{\prime}}|-|(X_{i,j^{\prime}}\setminus S_{i,j^{\prime}})||X_{i,j}|

\displaystyle=\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*})+|(X_{i,j^{\prime}}\setminus S_{i,j^{\prime}})|\left(|T_{r_{j}}^{*}\setminus X_{i,j}|-|T_{r_{j^{\prime}}}^{*}\setminus X_{i,j^{\prime}}|\right)+|(X_{i,j^{\prime}}\setminus S_{i,j^{\prime}})|\left(|S_{i,j^{\prime}}|-|X_{i,j}|\right)

\displaystyle<\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*})+|(X_{i,j^{\prime}}\setminus S_{i,j^{\prime}})|\left(|T_{r_{j}}^{*}\setminus X_{i,j}|-|T_{r_{j^{\prime}}}^{*}\setminus X_{i,j^{\prime}}|\right)+|(X_{i,j^{\prime}}\setminus S_{i,j^{\prime}})|\left(|S_{i,j^{\prime}}|-p\right)

\displaystyle(\textbf{as}\,\,|X_{i,j}|\geq p)

\displaystyle<\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*})

\displaystyle(\textbf{as}\,\,|T_{r_{j}}^{*}\setminus X_{i,j}|\leq|T_{r_{j^{\prime}}}^{*}\setminus X_{i,j^{\prime}}|\,\,\text{and}\,\,|S_{i,j^{\prime}}|<p).

\jmlrQED

Now from
3
, we get that there exists at most one partition
X_{i,j}
such that
|X_{i,j}|\geq p
the following claims are related to the partition
X_{i,j}
. Recall each partition
X_{i,\ell}\subseteq T_{r_{\ell}}^{*}
for all
\ell\in[t]
where
T_{r_{\ell}}^{*}\in\mathcal{T}^{*}
.

Claim 4
.

|\textit{blue}(\bigcup\limits_{\ell\neq j}X_{i,\ell})|\geq(p-s_{i,j})

Proof:
Suppose for contradiction,

|\textit{blue}(\bigcup_{\ell\neq j}X_{i,\ell})|<(p-s_{i,j})

Now we prove,

|\textit{blue}(\bigcup_{\ell\neq j}X_{i,\ell})|<(p-s_{i,j})\Rightarrow|\textit{blue}(\bigcup_{\ell\neq j}X_{i,\ell})|=s_{i}-s_{i,j}

\displaystyle\left(|\textit{blue}(X_{i,j})|+|\textit{blue}(\bigcup_{\ell\neq j}X_{i,\ell})|\right)\mod p=s_{i}.

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\,\,|\textit{blue}(X_{i,j})|\mod p+|\textit{blue}(\bigcup_{\ell\neq j}X_{i,\ell})|\mod p=s_{i}.

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\,\,s_{i,j}+|\textit{blue}(\bigcup_{\ell\neq j}X_{i,\ell})|\mod p=s_{i}.

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\,\,|\textit{blue}(\bigcup_{\ell\neq j}X_{i,\ell})|\mod p=s_{i}-s_{i,j}

(23)

Since,
|\textit{blue}(\bigcup\limits_{\ell\neq j})X_{i,\ell}|<(p-s_{i,j})
and due to
Eq.

23
we have

|\textit{blue}(\bigcup\limits_{\ell\neq j}X_{i,\ell})|=s_{i}-s_{i,j}

Now we prove if
|\textit{blue}(\bigcup_{\ell\neq j}X_{i,\ell})|=s_{i}-s_{i,j}
then there exists a clustering
\mathcal{M}
such that
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{M})<\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*})
which is a contradiction and thus we conclude our claim which is
|\textit{blue}(\bigcup\limits_{\ell\neq j}X_{i,\ell})|\geq(p-s_{i})
.

Construction of
\mathcal{M}
from
\mathcal{T}^{*}

Note that since
\textit{blue}(T_{r_{j}}^{*})\mod p=0
and by
2
we get
|T_{r_{j}}^{*}\setminus X_{i,j}|\leq s_{i,j}
thus

|\textit{blue}(T_{r_{j}}^{*}\setminus X_{i,j})|=p-s_{i,j}>s_{i}-s_{i,j}

Let
Y\subseteq\textit{blue}(T_{r_{j}}^{*}\setminus X_{i,j})
such that
|Y|=s_{i}-s_{i,j}
.

Here, the construction of
\mathcal{M}
from
\mathcal{T}^{*}
is a bit more involved, so first, we provide an informal description of the construction, and then we give the formal description.

Recall, the cluster
D_{i}
is split into
t
partitions
X_{i,1},X_{i,2},\ldots,X_{i,t}
in
\mathcal{T}^{*}
. Among these partitions
|X_{i,j}|\geq p
and each of these partitions
X_{i,\ell}\subseteq T^{*}_{r_{\ell}}
for some
T^{*}_{r_{\ell}}\in\mathcal{T}^{*}
.

While constructing
\mathcal{M}
from
\mathcal{T}^{*}
we do the following steps

1.

Divide
Y
into
(t-1)
disjoint parts
Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{j-1},Y_{j+1},\ldots,Y_{t}
such that

•

|Y_{\ell}|=|\textit{blue}(X_{i,\ell})|

\forall\ell\neq j
and

•

\bigcup_{\ell\neq j}Y_{\ell}=Y

2.

Remove
t
clusters
T_{r_{\ell}}^{*}
for all
\ell\in[t]
from
\mathcal{T}^{*}
.

3.

Add
t
new clusters to
\mathcal{M}

•

First cluster :
D_{i}\cup(T^{*}_{r_{j}}\setminus(X_{i,j}\cup Y))

•

Remaining
(t-1)
clusters :
(T^{*}_{r_{\ell}}\setminus X_{i,\ell})\cup Y_{\ell})\,\,\forall\ell\neq j
.

We move the red points of
X_{i,\ell}
for all
\ell\neq j
to the set
T_{r_{j}}^{*}
.

The main part of the above construction is that we have divided
Y
into
(t-1)
disjoint parts
Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{j-1},Y_{j+1},\ldots,Y_{t}
such that
|Y_{\ell}|=|\textit{blue}(X_{i,\ell})|\,\,\forall\ell\neq j
. After that, we swap the points in
\textit{blue}(X_{i,\ell})
with the points in
Y_{\ell}
.

Formally,

\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{T}^{*}\setminus\{T^{*}_{r_{\ell}}\mid\ell\neq j\}\cup\left\{\left(D_{i}\cup\left(T_{r_{j}}^{*}\setminus\left(X_{i,j}\cup Y\right)\right)\right),\left(\left(T_{r_{\ell}}^{*}\setminus X_{i,\ell}\right)\cup Y_{\ell}\right)\mid\ell\neq j\right\}

where
Y_{\ell}\subseteq Y
such that

•

|Y_{\ell}|=|\textit{blue}(X_{i,\ell})|

\forall\ell\neq j
and

•

Y_{m}\cap Y_{n}=\emptyset

\forall m\neq n
and
m,n\in[t]
.

•

\bigcup_{\ell\neq j}Y_{\ell}=Y
.

Note that,

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{M})=\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*})

\displaystyle+\left|\bigcup_{\ell\neq j}X_{i,\ell}\right|\left|T^{*}_{r_{j}}\setminus(X_{i,j}\cup Y)\right|+|Y|\left|T^{*}_{r_{j}}\setminus(X_{i,j}\cup Y)\right|+\sum_{\ell\neq j}|Y_{\ell}||T^{*}_{r_{\ell}}\setminus X_{i,\ell}|

\displaystyle-\left|\bigcup_{\ell\neq j}X_{i,\ell}\right|\left|X_{i,j}\right|-|Y||X_{i,j}|-\sum_{\ell\neq j}|\textit{blue}(X_{i,\ell})||T^{*}_{r_{\ell}}\setminus X_{i,\ell}|

This is because

•

Reason behind the term
\left|\bigcup_{\ell\neq j}X_{i,\ell}\right|\left|T^{*}_{r_{j}}\setminus(X_{i,j}\cup Y)\right|
: The pairs
(u,v)
such that
u\in\bigcup_{\ell\neq j}X_{i,\ell}
and
v\in T_{r_{j}}^{*}\setminus(X_{i,j}\cup Y)
are not be counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*})
because
u
and
v
are present in different clusters both in
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{T}^{*}
. In
\mathcal{M}
, since these pairs
u
and
v
belong to the same cluster, these pairs are counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{M})
.

•

Reason behind the term

Claim 5

X_{i,j}=T_{r_{j}}^{*}
(for some

).

Proof:

. at first we prove

.

Claim 6
.

\textit{red}\left(D_{i}\right)\subseteq X_{i,j}
.

Proof:
Suppose not then
\textit{red}\left(\bigcup_{\ell\neq j}X_{i,\ell}\right)\neq\emptyset

Construction of
\mathcal{M}
from
\mathcal{T}^{*}

:

\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{T}^{*}\setminus\left\{T^{*}_{r_{\ell}}\,\,\middle|\,\,(\textit{red}\left(D_{i}\right)\cap T^{*}_{r_{\ell}})\neq\emptyset\right\}\cup\left\{\left(T_{r_{j}}^{*}\cup\textit{red}\left(\bigcup\limits_{\ell\neq j}X_{i,\ell}\right)\right),\left(T^{*}_{r_{\ell}}\setminus\textit{red}\left(D_{i}\right)\,\,\middle|\,\,i\neq\ell\right)\right\}

Note that,

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{M})

\displaystyle<\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*})+p\left|\textit{red}\left(\bigcup_{\ell\neq j}X_{i,\ell}\right)\right|-\left|X_{i,j}\right|\left|\textit{red}\left(\bigcup_{\ell\neq j}X_{i,\ell}\right)\right|

\displaystyle<\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T}^{*})

\displaystyle(\textbf{as}\,\,|X_{i,j}|>p)

•

Reason behind the term
p\left|\textit{red}\left(\bigcup_{\ell\neq j}X_{i,\ell}\right)\right|
: The pairs
(u,v)
such that

they are counted in

. Now, since we know that for all
\ell\neq j
,

hence the total cost paid for these pairs is at most
p\,\,|\textit{red}\left(\bigcup_{\ell\neq j}X_{i,\ell}\right)|
.

•

. Now, since in
