\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}}\textsc{OPT}^{r}_{D_{i}}+2\sum_{D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}}\textsc{OPT}^{b}_{D_{i}}.

\displaystyle\leq 4\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*}).

Note that, (
67
) is obtained by using (
59
), (
60
), (
61
), and (
62
). The proof is complete.

\jmlrQED

Claim 20
.

\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{Q})+\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{Q})\leq 2\,\,\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})
.

Proof:
Applying the same arguments for
18
in the proof of
15
, we have

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{E^{r}_{j}\in\mathcal{E}^{r}}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{h_{i}}|Y^{r}_{j,t}|(q-|Y^{r}_{j,t}|)\right)+\sum_{D_{i}\in\textsc{RCut}}\left(\sum_{1\leq j<t\leq r_{i}}|R_{i,j}||R_{i,t}|\right)\leq\dfrac{3}{2}\sum_{D_{i}\in\textsc{RCut}}\dfrac{\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})}(q-\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})})}{2},

(68)

and

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{E^{b}_{j}\in\mathcal{E}^{b}}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{k_{i}}|Y^{b}_{j,t}|(q-|Y^{b}_{j,t}|)\right)+\sum_{D_{i}\in\textsc{BCut}}\left(\sum_{1\leq j<t\leq b_{i}}|B_{i,j}||B_{i,t}|\right)\leq\dfrac{3}{2}\sum_{D_{i}\in\textsc{BCut}}\dfrac{\textit{$s_{b}$}{(D_{i})}(p-\textit{$s_{b}$}{(D_{i})})}{2}.

(69)

Combining (
65
), (
66
), (
68
), and (
69
), it follows that

\displaystyle\>\>\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{Q})+\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{Q})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{D_{i}\in\textsc{RedCut}\cap\textsc{BlueMerge}}\dfrac{\textit{$d_{b}$}(D_{i})^{2}}{2}+\sum_{D_{i}\in\textsc{RedMerge}\cap\textsc{BlueCut}}\dfrac{\textit{$d_{r}$}(D_{i})^{2}}{2}

\displaystyle\>+\sum_{D_{i}\in\textsc{RedMerge}\cap\textsc{BlueMerge}}\dfrac{(\textit{$d_{r}$}(D_{i})+\textit{$d_{b}$}(D_{i}))^{2}}{2}

\displaystyle\>+\sum_{D_{i}\in\textsc{RedCut}\setminus\textsc{RCut}}\dfrac{\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})}^{2}}{2}+\sum_{D_{i}\in\textsc{BlueCut}\setminus\textsc{BCut}}\dfrac{\textit{$s_{b}$}{(D_{i})}^{2}}{2}

\displaystyle\>+\dfrac{3}{2}\sum_{D_{i}\in\textsc{RCut}}\dfrac{\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})}(q-\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})})}{2}+\dfrac{3}{2}\sum_{D_{i}\in\textsc{BCut}}\dfrac{\textit{$s_{b}$}{(D_{i})}(p-\textit{$s_{b}$}{(D_{i})})}{2}.

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\{\textsc{RedCut},\textsc{RedMerge}\}\\
B\in\{\textsc{BlueCut},\textsc{merge}\}\end{subarray}}\left(\sum_{D_{i}\in R\cap B}\dfrac{\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})}\textit{$d_{r}$}(D_{i})+\textit{$s_{b}$}{(D_{i})}\textit{$d_{b}$}(D_{i})}{2}\right)\right),

where the last inequality is due to the following observations:

•

If
D_{i}\in\textsc{RedCut}
, then
\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})}\textit{$d_{r}$}(D_{i})\geq\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})}^{2}
.

•

If
D_{i}\in\textsc{RedMerge}
, then
\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})}\textit{$d_{r}$}(D_{i})\geq\textit{$d_{r}$}(D_{i})^{2}
.

•

If
D_{i}\in\textsc{BlueCut}
, then
\textit{$s_{b}$}{(D_{i})}\textit{$d_{b}$}(D_{i})\geq\textit{$s_{b}$}{(D_{i})}^{2}
.

•

If
D_{i}\in\textsc{BlueMerge}
, then
\textit{$s_{b}$}{(D_{i})}\textit{$d_{b}$}(D_{i})\geq\textit{$d_{b}$}(D_{i})^{2}
.

•

If
D_{i}\in\textsc{RedMerge}\cap\textsc{BlueMerge}
, then
\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})}\textit{$d_{r}$}(D_{i})+\textit{$s_{b}$}{(D_{i})}\textit{$d_{b}$}(D_{i})\geq\textit{$d_{r}$}(D_{i})^{2}+\textit{$d_{b}$}(D_{i})^{2}\geq\dfrac{(\textit{$d_{r}$}(D_{i})+\textit{$d_{b}$}(D_{i}))^{2}}{2}
.

Now we utilize
Lemma

27
, which shows that

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})\geq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\{\textsc{RedCut},\textsc{RedMerge}\}\\
B\in\{\textsc{BlueCut},\textsc{merge}\}\end{subarray}}\left(\sum_{D_{i}\in R\cap B}\dfrac{\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})}\textit{$d_{r}$}(D_{i})+\textit{$s_{b}$}{(D_{i})}\textit{$d_{b}$}(D_{i})}{2}\right).

It follows that
\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{Q})+\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{Q})\leq 2\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})
.

\jmlrQED

Now we provide the proof of
Lemma

28

Proof of
Lemma

28
:

Combining
19
and
20
, it follows that

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})

\displaystyle=\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{Q})+\textit{cost}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})+\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{Q})+\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{Q})

\displaystyle\leq 4\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})+2\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})=6\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*}).

\jmlrQED

D.3.2
Approximation Guarantee in Cut-Merge Case

Cut-merge case (or Merge-Cut Case) occurs when the subroutine
AlgoforCut-Merge()
(
Section

D.1
) (or
AlgoforMerge-Cut()
) is called.

In this section, we provide the approximation guarantee of the algorithm
AlgoforGeneralPQ()
in the Cut-Merge case. The analysis of the Merge-Cut case is similar to the analysis of the Cut-Merge case only the roles of the red and blue points get reversed. So, in this analysis, we will focus solely on the Cut-Merge case, while the Merge-Cut case will be excluded from this discussion. We show that the cost paid by the algorithm
AlgoforGeneralPQ()
is at most
7.5\,\,\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})
where
\mathcal{Q}^{*}
is the
closest
Balanced clustering
of
\mathcal{D}
. More specifically,

Lemma 29
.

\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q})<7.5\,\,\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})
where
\mathcal{Q}
is the output of the algorithm
AlgoforGeneralPQ()
in the cut-merge case (
Section

D.1
of
Section

D.1
).

Similarly, for the Merge-Cut case, formally, we want to prove the following.

Lemma 30
.

\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q})<7.5\,\,\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})
where
\mathcal{Q}
is the output of the algorithm
AlgoforGeneralPQ()
in the merge-cut case (
Section

D.1
of
Section

D.1
).

We analyze in the following way: first, we calculate the cost paid by our algorithm
AlgoforGeneralPQ()
in the Cut-Merge case, and then we bound that cost with the cost paid by the optimal clustering
\mathcal{Q}^{*}
in the Cut-Merge case.

1) Cost paid by
AlgoforGeneralPQ()
in Cut-Merge case

The algorithm
AlgoforCut-Merge()
(
Section

D.1
) pays the following set of disjoint costs

•

From each cluster
D_{i}\in\textsc{RedCut}
,
AlgoforGeneralPQ()
cuts the red surplus part
s_{r}(D_{i})
from
D_{i}
. Hence, the cost paid for cutting these red surplus points is

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{1}(D_{i})^{\text{red}}=s_{r}(D_{i})(|D_{i}|-s_{r}(D_{i}))

(70)

•

For each cluster
D_{j}\in\textsc{RedMerge}
,
AlgoforGeneralPQ()
merges the deficit amount
\textit{$d_{r}$}(D_{j})
to these clusters. Hence, the cost paid for merging the deficit to
D_{j}
is

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{2}(D_{j})^{\text{red}}=\textit{$d_{r}$}(D_{j})|D_{j}|

(71)

•

The
\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})}
red points that are cut from
D_{i}
can get merged with the red surplus,
\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{k})}
of some other cluster
D_{k}
. We call the cost of merging
\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})}
red points with the red surplus of other clusters as
\textit{cost}_{3}(D_{i})^{\text{red}}
(similar to
p:1
case in
Appendix

C
).

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{3}(D_{i})^{\text{red}}\leq\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})}(q-\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})})

(72)

•

These
\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})}
red points from
D_{i}
can also further be split into several parts
W_{1},W_{2},\ldots,W_{t}
(say). These parts of
\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})}
points belong to different clusters in
\mathcal{Q}
(output of
AlgoforGeneralPQ()
) and thus would incur some cost. We call this cost as
\textit{cost}_{4}(D_{i})^{\text{red}}
(similar to
p:1
case in
Appendix

C
).

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{4}(D_{i})^{\text{red}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{t}|W_{j}|(\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})}-|W_{j}|)

(73)

•

Now, from the blue side, we may cut multiple subsets of size
p
and a single subset of size
\textit{$s_{b}$}{(D_{i})}
. Let us assume
W_{i,z}
denotes the
z
th such subset of the set
\textit{blue}(D_{i})
.
y_{i,z}
takes the value
1
if we cut
z
th such subset from
D_{i}
. The cost of cutting
z
th such subset from
D_{i}
is given as

\displaystyle\kappa_{0}(D_{i})=\textit{$s_{b}$}{(D_{i})}(|D_{i}|-\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})})

\displaystyle(\text{cost of cutting the $0$th subset})

\displaystyle\kappa_{z}(D_{i})=p(|D_{i}|-\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})}-zp)

\displaystyle(\text{cost of cutting the $z$th subset for $z\geq 1$})

and thus, we define

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{1}(D_{i})^{\text{blue}}=\sum_{D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}}\sum_{z=0}^{t}y_{i,z}\kappa_{z}(D_{i})\,\,(\text{refer}\,\,\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:cost-one-merge})

(74)

\displaystyle\left(\text{t}=\frac{\textit{blue}(D_{i})-\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})}-\textit{$s_{b}$}{(D_{i})}}{p}\right)

•

Suppose the algorithm
AlgoforGeneralPQ()
merges at a cluster
D_{j}\in\textsc{BlueMerge}
. Then, the cost paid for merging the deficit to
D_{j}
is

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{2}(D_{j})^{\text{blue}}=\textit{$d_{b}$}(D_{j})|D_{j}|\,\,(\text{refer}\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:cost-two-merge})

(75)

•

The
|W_{i,z}|
blue points that are cut from
D_{i}
can get merged with the blue subsets
|W_{j,z^{\prime}}|
of some other cluster
D_{j}
. We call the cost of merging a blue subset
W_{i,z}
of
D_{i}
with the blue subset
W_{j,z^{\prime}}
of another cluster
D_{j}
as
\textit{cost}_{3}(D_{i})^{\text{blue}}
(similar to
p:1
case refer
Eq.

21
).

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{3}(D_{i})^{\text{blue}}\leq|W_{i,z}|(p-|W_{i,z}|)

(76)

•

The
|W_{i,z}|
blue points that are cut from
D_{i}
can also further be split into several parts
W_{1},W_{2},\ldots,W_{t}
(say). These parts of
W_{i,z}
can belong to different clusters in
\mathcal{Q}
(output of
AlgoforGeneralPQ()
) and thus would incur some cost. We call this cost as
\textit{cost}_{4}(D_{i})^{\text{blue}}
(similar to
p:1
case refer
Eq.

22
).

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{4}(D_{i})^{\text{blue}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{t}|W_{j}|(|W_{i,z}|-|W_{j}|)

(77)

•

Suppose for a cluster
D_{j}\in\textsc{BlueMerge}\cap\textsc{RedMerge}
, both the blue deficit and the red deficit are filled up by the subsets of some other clusters
D_{k}
and
D_{\ell}
in
\mathcal{D}
respectively such that
k\neq\ell
then this would incur some cost and is denoted by

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{5}(D_{j})^{\text{blue}}=(p-\textit{$s_{b}$}{(D_{j})})(q-\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{j})})

(78)

Now, let us define the total cost paid by the algorithm over all clusters
D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}
.

•

\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{red}}=\sum_{D_{i}\in\textsc{RedCut}}\textit{cost}_{1}(D_{i})^{\text{red}}

•

\textit{cost}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{red}}=\sum_{D_{i}\in\textsc{RedMerge}}\textit{cost}_{2}(D_{i})^{\text{red}}

•

\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{red}}=\sum_{D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}}\textit{cost}_{3}(D_{i})^{\text{red}}

•

\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{red}}=\sum_{D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}}\textit{cost}_{4}(D_{i})^{\text{red}}

•

\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}=\sum_{D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}}\textit{cost}_{1}(D_{i})^{\text{blue}}

•

\textit{cost}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}=\sum_{D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}}\textit{cost}_{2}(D_{i})^{\text{blue}}

•

\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}=\sum_{D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}}\textit{cost}_{3}(D_{i})^{\text{blue}}

•

\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}=\sum_{D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}}\textit{cost}_{4}(D_{i})^{\text{blue}}

•

\textit{cost}_{5}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}=\sum_{D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}}\textit{cost}_{5}(D_{i})^{\text{blue}}

2) Cost paid by optimal clustering
\mathcal{Q}^{*}
in cut-merge case

Claim 21
.

\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{Red}}+\textit{cost}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{Red}}+\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{Red}}+\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{Red}}\leq 3.5\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})
.

Proof:
Recall in
p:1
case, where
\mathcal{T}
is the output of
AlgoforGeneral()
(
Section

C.1
) we have

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{T})

\displaystyle=\sum_{D_{i}\in\textsc{cut}}s_{i}(|D_{i}|-s_{i})

\displaystyle(\text{Refer to}\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{equn:cost-one})

\displaystyle=\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{Red}}

\displaystyle(\text{Replacing}\,\,s_{i}\,\,\text{with}\,\,\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})})

(79)

and again,

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{2}(\mathcal{T})

\displaystyle=\sum_{D_{i}\in\textsc{merge}}\textit{d}(D_{i})|D_{i}|

\displaystyle(\text{Refer to}\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{equn:cost-two})

\displaystyle=\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{Red}}

\displaystyle(\text{Replacing}\,\,d(D_{i})\,\,\text{with}\,\,\textit{$d_{r}$}(D_{i}))

(80)

Now since

\displaystyle\textsc{OPT}^{r}_{D_{i}}

\displaystyle\geq\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})}(|D_{i}|-\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})})+\frac{1}{2}\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})}\textit{d}(D_{i})

\displaystyle(\text{from}\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{equn:cut-red})

(81)

Hence, we get

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{Red}}+\textit{cost}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{red}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{D_{i}\in\textsc{RedCut}}\textsc{OPT}^{r}_{D_{i}}+2\,\,\sum_{D_{i}\in\textsc{RedMerge}}\textsc{OPT}^{r}_{D_{i}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\,\,\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})

(82)

Since the way we distribute the red surplus part into the clusters of size
q
in
AlgoforCut-Merge()
(
Section

D.1
) is similar to the way we distribute the surplus part in algorithm
AlgoforCut()
(
Section

C.1
) in
p:1
case in
Appendix

C
we have

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{T})=\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{red}},\text{and}

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{T})=\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{red}}

Hence, we can use the same proof of
15
to get the following bound.

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{Red}}+\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{Red}}\leq 1.5\,\,\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})

(83)

Now, from
Eq.

82
and
Eq.

83
we get,

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{Red}}+\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{Red}}+\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{Red}}+\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{Red}}\leq 3.5\,\,\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})

(84)

\jmlrQED

Claim 22
.

\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}+\textit{cost}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}+\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}+\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}\leq 3\,\,\textsc{OPT}
.

Proof:
Let
y_{i,z}
take the value
1
if we cut the
z
th blue subset of size
p
from a cluster
D_{i}
.

Now, let us compare the following costs

In
p:q
case (
Appendix

D
) the cost of cutting the
w_{i,z}
subset from a cluster
D_{i}
for
z\geq 1
is given by,

\textit{cost}_{1}(D_{i})^{\text{blue}}\leq p(|D_{i}|-\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})}-\textit{$s_{b}$}{(D_{i})}-zp)

whereas in
p:1
case (
Appendix

C
) the cost of cutting the
W_{i,z}
part from a cluster
D_{i}
for
z\geq 1
is given by,

\textit{cost}_{1}(D_{i})\leq p(|D_{i}|-\textit{$s_{b}$}{(D_{i})}-zp)

Thus,

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{1}(D_{i})^{\text{blue}}\leq\textit{cost}_{1}(D_{i})

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\sum_{D_{i}}\textit{cost}_{1}(D_{i})^{\text{blue}}\leq\sum_{D_{i}}\textit{cost}_{1}(D_{i})

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}\leq\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{T})

\displaystyle(\text{where $\mathcal{T}$ is the output of $\text{AlgoforGeneral()}$(\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{alg:algo-for-general})})

Now similarly, in
p:q
case (
Appendix

D
) we have

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{2}(D_{i})^{\text{blue}}=(p-\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})})(|D_{i}|-\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})})

(85)

whereas in
p:1
case (
Appendix

C
) we have

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{2}(D_{i})=(p-s(D_{i}))|D_{i}|

(86)

Hence,

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{2}(D_{i})^{\text{blue}}\leq\textit{cost}_{2}(D_{i})

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\sum_{D_{i}}\textit{cost}_{2}(D_{i})^{\text{blue}}\leq\sum_{D_{i}}\textit{cost}_{2}(D_{i})

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}\leq\textit{cost}_{2}(\mathcal{T})

\displaystyle(\text{where $\mathcal{T}$ is the output of $\text{AlgoforGeneral()}$(\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{alg:algo-for-general})})

Thus,

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}+\textit{cost}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}\leq\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{T})+\textit{cost}_{2}(\mathcal{T}).

(87)

Now, since when
\textit{$s_{b}$}{(D_{i})}\geq p/2
we have

\displaystyle\textsc{OPT}^{b}_{D_{i}}\geq\textit{d}(D_{i})(|D_{i}|-\textit{$s_{b}$}{(D_{i})})+\frac{1}{2}\textit{$s_{b}$}{(D_{i})}\textit{d}(D_{i})

\displaystyle(\text{from}\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{equn:merge-blue})

(88)

we can use the same proof as in
7
to show

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}+\textit{cost}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}\leq\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})

(89)

Since the way we merge the blue surplus part from a cluster
D_{i}
to another set of clusters
D_{j}
in
AlgoforCut-Merge()
(
Section

D.1
) is the same as the way we merge the surplus part in algorithm
AlgoforMerge()
(
Section

C.1
) in
p:1
case (
Appendix

C
). Thus we have

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{T})=\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}},\text{ and}

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{T})=\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}

Hence, we can use the same proof of
11
to get the following bound.

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}+\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}\leq 2\,\,\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})

(90)

Now, from
Eq.

89
and
Eq.

90
we get,

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}+\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}+\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{Red}}+\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{Red}}\leq 3\,\,\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})

(91)

\jmlrQED

Claim 23
.

\textit{cost}_{5}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}\leq\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})

Proof:

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{5}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{D_{i}}(q-\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})})(p-\textit{$s_{b}$}{(D_{i})})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{D_{i}}\,(p-\textit{$s_{b}$}{(D_{i})})\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})}

\displaystyle(\textbf{as}\,\,\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})}>\frac{q}{2})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{D_{i}}\,(p-\textit{$s_{b}$}{(D_{i})})(|D_{i}|-\textit{$s_{b}$}{(D_{i})})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{D_{i}}\,\textsc{OPT}_{D_{i}}^{b}

\displaystyle(\textbf{from}\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{equn:merge-blue})

\displaystyle=\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})

\jmlrQED

Now we are ready to prove
Lemma

29

Proof of
Lemma

29
:

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q})

\displaystyle=\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{Red}}+\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{Red}}+\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{Red}}

\displaystyle+\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}+\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}+\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}+\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}+\textit{cost}_{5}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}

\displaystyle\leq 3.5\,\,\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})+3\,\,\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})+\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})

\displaystyle=7.5\,\,\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})

\jmlrQED

D.3.3
Approximation Guarantee in Merge-Merge Case

In this section, we do the analysis for the algorithm
AlgoforMerge-Merge
(
Section

D.1
). We show that the cost paid by the algorithm
AlgoforMerge-Merge
(
Section

D.1
) is at most
7\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})
where
\mathcal{Q}^{*}
is the closest
p
-blue
q
-red clustering to
D
. More specifically,

Lemma 31
.

\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q})<7\,\,\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})
where
\mathcal{Q}
is the output of the algorithm
AlgoforGeneralPQ()
in the merge-merge case (
Section

D.1
of
Section

D.1
).

We analyze in the following way: first, we calculate the cost paid by our algorithm

AlgoforMerge-Merge
(
Section

D.1
), and then we calculate the cost paid by the optimal clustering
\mathcal{Q}^{*}
. After that, we bound the cost paid by the algorithm
AlgoforMerge-Merge
(
Section

D.1
) with the cost paid by
\mathcal{Q}^{*}
.

1) Cost paid by
AlgoforMerge-Merge
(
Section

D.1
)

The algorithm
AlgoforMerge-Merge
(
Section

D.1
) pays the following set of disjoint costs

•

From the red side, we cut multiple subsets of size
q
. Let us assume
W_{i,z,r}
denotes the
z
th red subset of the cluster
D_{i}
.
y_{i,z,r}
takes the value
1
if we cut
z
th red subset from
D_{i}
. The cost of cutting
z
th subset from
D_{i}
is given as

\displaystyle\kappa_{0}(D_{i})=\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})}(|D_{i}|-\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})})

\displaystyle\kappa_{z}(D_{i})=q(|D_{i}|-\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})}-zp)

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{1}(D_{i})^{\text{red}}=\sum_{z=0}^{t}y_{i,z,r}\kappa_{z}(D_{i})

(92)

\displaystyle\left(\text{t}=\frac{\textit{red}\left(D_{i}\right)-\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{i})}}{q}\right)

(93)

•

Each of these red subsets
W_{i,z,r}\in D_{i}
is merged with red subsets of some other clusters
W_{j,z^{\prime},r}\in D_{j}
. This we again indicate by
\textit{cost}_{3}(D_{i})^{\text{red}}
.

•

Each of these partitions
W_{i,z,r}\in D_{i}
is further partitioned into several parts
W_{1},W_{2},\ldots,W_{t}
(say) and merged into different clusters. The pairs between these several parts
W_{j}
s is indicated by
\textit{cost}_{4}(D_{i})^{\text{red}}

•

Suppose we merge in a cluster
D_{j}\in\textsc{RedMerge}
. The merge cost paid is denoted by

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{2}(D_{j})^{\text{red}}=(q-\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{j})})|D_{j}|

(94)

•

Now, from the blue side, again, we cut multiple subsets of size
p
. Let us assume
W_{i,z,b}
denotes the
z
th blue subset of the cluster
D_{i}
.
y_{i,z,b}
takes the value
1
if we cut
z
th blue subset from
D_{i}
. The cost of cutting
z
th subset from
D_{i}
is given as

\displaystyle\kappa_{0}(D_{i})=\textit{$s_{b}$}{(D_{i})}(|D_{i}|-\textit{$s_{b}$}{(D_{i})})

\displaystyle\kappa_{z}(D_{i})=p(|D_{i}|-\textit{$s_{b}$}{(D_{i})}-zp)

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{1}(D_{i})^{\text{blue}}=\sum_{z=0}^{t}y_{i,z}\kappa_{z}(D_{i})

(95)

\displaystyle\left(\text{t}=\frac{\textit{blue}(D_{i})-\textit{$s_{b}$}{(D_{i})}}{p}\right)

(96)

•

Each of these partitions
W_{i,z,b}\in D_{i}
is merged with partitions of some other clusters
W_{j,z^{\prime}}\in D_{j}
. This we again indicate by
\textit{cost}_{3}(D_{i})^{\text{blue}}

•

Each of these partitions
W_{i,z,b}\in D_{i}
is further partitioned into several parts
W_{1},W_{2},\ldots,W_{t}
(say) and merged into different clusters. The pairs between these several parts
W_{j}
s is indicated by
\textit{cost}_{4}(D_{i})^{\text{blue}}

•

Suppose we merge in a cluster
D_{j}\in\textsc{BlueMerge}
. The merge cost paid is denoted by

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{2}(D_{j})^{\text{blue}}=(p-\textit{$s_{b}$}{(D_{j})})|D_{j}|

(97)

•

Suppose, the blue deficit merged to the cluster
D_{j}
comes from the cluster
D_{i}
and the red deficit merged to the cluster
D_{j}
comes from a cluster
D_{k}
such that
D_{k}\neq D_{j}
. This cost is denoted by

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{5}(D_{j})^{\text{blue}}=(p-\textit{$s_{b}$}{(D_{j})})(q-\textit{$s_{r}$}{(D_{j})})

(98)

One thing to note is that the algorithm
AlgoforMerge-Merge
may pay the cost for the pairs in
W_{i,z,r}\times W_{i,z^{\prime},b}
, that is in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q})
(recall
\mathcal{Q}
is the output of
AlgoforMerge-Merge
) a pair
(u,v)\in W_{i,z,r}\times W_{i,z^{\prime},b}
may be counted because the subsets
W_{i,z,r}
and
W_{i,z^{\prime},b}
may get merged into different clusters
D_{j}
and
D_{k}
in
\mathcal{D}
where
j\neq k
. We are counting these pairs in
\textit{cost}_{1}(D_{i})^{\text{red}}
.

Now, let us define the total cost paid by the algorithm over all clusters
D_{i}\in\text{Cut-Merge}
.

•

\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{red}}=\sum_{D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}}\textit{cost}_{1}(D_{i})^{\text{red}}

•

\textit{cost}_{2}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{red}}=\sum_{D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}}\textit{cost}_{2}(D_{i})^{\text{red}}

•

\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{red}}=\sum_{D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}}\textit{cost}_{3}(D_{i})^{\text{red}}

•

\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{red}}=\sum_{D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}}\textit{cost}_{4}(D_{i})^{\text{red}}

•

\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}=\sum_{D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}}\textit{cost}_{1}(D_{i})^{\text{blue}}

•

\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}=\sum_{D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}}\textit{cost}_{2}(D_{i})^{\text{blue}}

•

\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}=\sum_{D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}}\textit{cost}_{3}(D_{i})^{\text{blue}}

•

\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}=\sum_{D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}}\textit{cost}_{4}(D_{i})^{\text{blue}}

•

\textit{cost}_{5}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}=\sum_{D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}}\textit{cost}_{5}(D_{i})^{\text{blue}}

2) Cost paid by optimal clustering
\mathcal{Q}^{*}
in merge-merge case

Claim 24
.

\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{red}}+\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{red}}+\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{red}}+\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{red}}\leq 3\,\,\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})
.

Claim 25
.

\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}+\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}+\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}+\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}\leq 3\,\,\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})
.

The proof of the previous two claims is the same as the proof given in
22
. So, we are skipping the proof of the above claims.

We also need to prove the following so that we can proceed to prove the main lemma of this section
Lemma

31
.

Claim 26
.

\textit{cost}_{5}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}\leq\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})
.

The proof of the above claim is similar to
23
.

Now we are ready to prove
Lemma

31
.

Proof of
Lemma

31
:

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q})

\displaystyle=\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{red}}+\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{red}}+\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{red}}+\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{red}}

\displaystyle+\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}+\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}+\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}+\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}+\textit{cost}_{5}(\mathcal{Q})^{\text{blue}}

\displaystyle\leq 3\,\,\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})+3\,\,\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})+\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})

\displaystyle=7\,\,\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*}).

\jmlrQED

D.4
Overall Approximation

We provide the proof of
Theorem

21
.

Proof of
Theorem

21
:

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be the output of the algorithm
AlgoforGeneralPQ()
(
Section

D.1
). We have four following cases.

If
\mathcal{Q}
is the output in the cut-cut case (
Section

D.1
of
Section

D.1
), then by
Lemma

28
, we have
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q})\leq 6\,\,\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})
.

If
\mathcal{Q}
is the output in the cut-merge case (
Section

D.1
of
Section

D.1
), then by
Lemma

29
, we have
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q})\leq 7.5\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})
.

If
\mathcal{Q}
is the output in the cut-merge case (
Section

D.1
of
Section

D.1
), then by
Lemma

30
, we have
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q})\leq 7.5\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})
.

If
\mathcal{Q}
is the output in the merge-merge case (
Section

D.1
of
Section

D.1
), then by
Lemma

31
, we have
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q})\leq 7\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*})
.

Therefore, in all cases, we have

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q})\leq 7.5\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q}^{*}).

\jmlrQED

Appendix E
Making the Balanced Clustering Fair

Given a clustering
\mathcal{T}=\{T_{1},T_{2},\dots,T_{\gamma}\}
such that in each
T_{i}
, the number of blue points
|\textit{blue}(T_{i})|
is a multiple
p
, and the number of red points
|\textit{red}\left(T_{i}\right)|
is a multiple of
q
, our goal is to find a closest fair clustering
\mathcal{F}^{*}
to
\mathcal{T}
. Recall that a fair clustering is a clustering such that the number of blue points in any cluster is
\ratio=\dfrac{p}{q}
times the number of red points. Our main result in this section is the following theorem.

See
19

Throughout this section, we assume
p>q
, and hence,
\ratio>1
. The case
q>p
can be handled similarly by switching the role of blue and red points.

In
Section

E.1
, we introduce
Section

E.1
, which takes
\mathcal{T}
as its input, and outputs a fair cluster
\mathcal{F}
. We analyze in
Section

E.2
that
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})\leq 3\textit{dist}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}^{*})
.

E.1
Algorithms

The high-level of
Section

E.1
is the following. The
\textsf{Balanced clustering}\ \mathcal{T}
is partitioned into two sets. Each cluster
T_{i}
is chosen to be in one of the two sets based on whether
|\textit{blue}(T_{i})|<\ratio|\textit{red}\left(T_{i}\right)|
or
|\textit{blue}(T_{i})|>\ratio|\textit{red}\left(T_{i}\right)|
. If it is the former case,
T_{i}
is placed into the first set
TypeRed
, while if it is the latter case,
T_{i}
is placed into the second set
TypeBlue
. After that, a set of
|\textit{red}\left(T_{i}\right)|-\dfrac{|\textit{blue}(T_{i})|}{\ratio}
red points are cut from each cluster
T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeRed}
. These points are merged to clusters in the set
TypeBlue
. Each cluster
T_{j}\in\textsc{TypeBlue}
are merged with exactly
\dfrac{|\textit{blue}(T_{j}|)}{\ratio}-|\textit{red}\left(T_{j}\right)|
red points.

Runtime Analysis of
MakeClustersFair()
: First note, each point is used at most twice during the execution of the algorithm
MakeClustersFair()
– once when we cut the red point
v\in V
from the cluster in the set
TypeRed
and again when we merge that red point
v\in V
to cluster that belongs to the set
TypeBlue
. Hence, the total runtime of the algorithm
MakeClustersFair()
would be
O(|V|)
.

{algorithm2e}

[htbp]

\DontPrintSemicolon
\text{MakeClustersFair()}(\mathcal{T})

\KwData
a
\textsf{Balanced clustering}\ \mathcal{T}
.

\KwResult
a fair clustering
\mathcal{F}
.

\textsc{TypeBlue},\textsc{TypeRed},\mathcal{F}\leftarrow\emptyset

\For
T_{i}\in\mathcal{T}

\If
|\textit{blue}(T_{i})|<\ratio|\textit{red}\left(T_{i}\right)|

add
T_{i}
to the set
TypeRed

\ElseIf
|\textit{blue}(T_{i})|>\ratio|\textit{red}\left(T_{i}\right)|

add
T_{i}
to the set
TypeBlue

\For

T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeRed}

\For
T_{j}\in\textsc{TypeBlue}

Let
k=\min(|\textit{red}\left(T_{i}\right)|-\dfrac{|\textit{blue}(T_{i})|}{\ratio},\dfrac{|\textit{blue}(T_{j})|}{\ratio}-|\textit{red}\left(T_{j}\right)|)

Move
k
red points from
T_{i}
to
T_{j}

\If
k=|\textit{red}\left(T_{i}\right)|-\dfrac{|\textit{blue}(T_{i})|}{\ratio}

\textsc{TypeRed}=\textsc{TypeRed}\setminus\{T_{i}\}

\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}\cup\{T_{i}\}

\If
k=\dfrac{|\textit{blue}(T_{j})|}{\ratio}-|\textit{red}\left(T_{j}\right)|

\textsc{TypeBlue}=\textsc{TypeBlue}\setminus\{T_{j}\}

\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}\cup\{T_{j}\}

\Return
\mathcal{F}

E.2
Analysis

In this section, we aim to prove
Theorem

19
.

Consider a cluster
T_{i}\in\mathcal{T}
. Suppose that in
\mathcal{F}^{*}
,
T_{i}
is spread through clusters
X_{i,1}\cup B_{i,1}\cup R_{i,1},X_{i,2}\cup B_{i,2}\cup R_{i,2},\dots,X_{i,t}\cup B_{i,t}\cup R_{i,t}
, such that
T_{i}=\cup_{j=1}^{t}X_{i,j}
, and
B_{i,j},R_{i,j}
are the set of blue points and red points from some clusters
T_{k}
(
k\neq i
), respectively. Then the distance between the two clusterings
\mathcal{F}^{*}
and
\mathcal{T}
is

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}^{*})

\displaystyle=\sum_{T_{i}\in\mathcal{T}}\left(\sum_{j<k}|X_{i,j}||X_{i,k}|+\sum_{j=1}^{t}\dfrac{|X_{i,j}|(|B_{i,j}|+|R_{i,j}|)}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{T_{i}\in\mathcal{T}}\textsc{OPT}_{T_{i}},

where
\textsc{OPT}_{T_{i}}=\sum_{j<k}|X_{i,j}||X_{i,k}|+\sum_{j=1}^{t}\dfrac{|X_{i,j}|(|B_{i,j}|+|R_{i,j}|)}{2}
.

Prior to proving the correctness of
Theorem

19
, we demonstrate some useful lower bounds for
\textsc{OPT}_{T_{i}}
. In particular, let
d=\frac{|\textit{blue}(T_{i})|}{\ratio}-|\textit{red}\left(T_{i}\right)|
,
s=|\textit{red}\left(T_{i}\right)|-\frac{|\textit{blue}(T_{i})|}{\ratio}
, then

•

If
T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeBlue}
, then
\textsc{OPT}_{T_{i}}\geq\dfrac{1}{2}d|T_{i}|
;

•

If
T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeRed}
, then
\textsc{OPT}_{T_{i}}\geq\dfrac{1}{2}s(|T_{i}|-s)
;

•

If
T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeRed}
, then
\textsc{OPT}_{T_{i}}\geq\dfrac{s_{i}^{2}}{2}
.

To show this, we leverage the following lemma.

Lemma 32
.

Given positive numbers
x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{t}
and a positive integer
S
such that
S\leq\sum_{i=1}^{t}x_{i}
. Then for any
a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{n}
such that
a_{i}\geq 0
, there exists non-negative integers
y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{t}
satisfying:

•

0\leq y_{i}\leq\left\lceil x_{i}\right\rceil
, for
i=1,2,\dots,t
.

•

\sum_{i=1}^{t}y_{i}=S
.

•

\sum_{i=1}^{t}a_{i}y_{i}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{t}a_{i}x_{i}
.

Proof:
Without loss of generality, assume that
a_{1}\leq a_{2}\leq\dots\leq a_{n}
.

We will show that the output of the following algorithm is the desired
y_{i}
’s.

{algorithm2e}

[htbp]

\DontPrintSemicolon
Choose
(y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{t})
.

\SetKwInOut
KwInInput

\SetKwInOut
KwOutOutput

y_{j}\leftarrow\lfloor x_{j}\rfloor,\forall j

\While

\sum_{i=1}^{t}y_{i}>S

\For
i=1\to t

\uIf
\sum_{i=1}^{t}y_{i}>S
and
y_{i}>0

y_{i}\leftarrow y_{i}-1

\While

\sum_{i=1}^{t}y_{i}<S

\For
i=1\to t

\uIf
\sum_{i=1}^{t}y_{i}<S
and
y_{i}<\lceil x_{i}\rceil

y_{i}\leftarrow y_{i}+1

\KwRet

(y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{t})

Let
y_{i}
’s be the output of
Section

E.2
.

\bullet

0\leq y_{i}\leq\left\lceil x_{i}\right\rceil
, for
i=1,2,\dots,t
.

Observe that
y_{i}
is decreased by
1
(
Section

E.2
) only if
y_{i}>0
, and is increased by
1
(
Section

E.2
) only if
y_{i}<\lceil x_{i}\rceil
. Hence, each
y_{i}
output by this algorithm satisfies
0\leq y_{i}\leq\lceil x_{i}\rceil
.

\bullet

\sum_{i=1}^{t}y_{i}=S
.

To see this, we consider
(y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{t})
after the execution of each while loop. Initially,
y_{i}
is set to
\lfloor x_{i}\rfloor
. If
\sum_{i=1}^{t}y_{i}>S
, the first while loop gradually decreases
\sum_{i=1}^{t}y_{i}
by
1
by decreasing a nonzero
y_{i}
by
1
, until
\sum_{i=1}^{t}y_{i}=y
. Since
\min\sum_{i=1}^{t}y_{i}=0
and
S\geq 0
, there must be an iteration where
\sum_{i=1}^{t}y_{i}=S
. After this iteration, the first while loop is done. The second while loop will not be executed, and the algorithm will return
(y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{t})
satisfying
\sum_{i=1}^{t}y_{i}=S
.

On the other hand, if
\sum_{i=1}^{t}\lfloor x_{i}\rfloor<y
, the first while loop is not executed. The second while loop gradually increases
\sum_{i=1}^{t}y_{i}
by
1
by increasing a
y_{i}<\lceil x_{i}\rceil
by
1
, until
\sum_{i=1}^{t}y_{i}
. Since
S\leq\sum_{i=1}^{t}x_{i}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{t}\lceil x_{i}\rceil
, there always exists some
y_{i}(\leq\lceil x_{i}\rceil)
that can be increased whenever
\sum_{i=1}^{t}y_{t}<S
. Hence, there must be an iteration where
\sum_{i=1}^{t}y_{i}=S
. After this iteration, the second while loop is done, and the algorithm outputs
(y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{t})
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{t}y_{i}=S
.

\bullet

\sum_{i=1}^{t}a_{i}y_{i}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{t}a_{i}x_{i}
.

We first claim that there exists
m\leq t
such that for all
i<m
, we have
y_{i}\geq x_{i}
, and for all
i\geq m
, we have
y_{i}\leq x_{i}
.

If
y_{i}\leq x_{i}
for all
j
, then
m=1
satisfies the claim. If there exists
y_{i}>x_{i}
, since
\sum_{i=1}^{t}y_{i}<\sum_{i=1}^{t}x_{i}
, there also exists
y_{i}<x_{i}
. Let
m
be the minimum index such that
y_{m}<x_{m}
, then
y_{i}\geq x_{i}
for all
j<m
. Now we show that
y_{i}\leq x_{i}
for all
j\geq m
.

To see this, note that in
Section

E.2
, if the second while loop is not executed, then the algorithm only decreases
y_{i}
. Thus
y_{i}\leq\lfloor x_{i}\rfloor\leq x_{i}
for all
j
. The second while loop is executed only if the first while loop is not. In that case, the second while loop starts with
y_{i}=\lfloor x_{i}\rfloor
. If
y_{i}
is increased (
Section

E.2
), then
y_{i}=\lceil x_{i}\rceil>x_{i}
. Assume to the contrary that there exists
j>m
such that
y_{j}>x_{j}
. This implies that before
y_{j}
is increased,
\sum_{i=1}^{t}y_{i}<S
. This means that in the iteration of the for loop where
i=m
, we have
\sum_{i=1}^{t}y_{i}<S
and by definition of
m
,
y_{m}<x_{m}
. It follows that
y_{m}
is increased and becomes greater than
x_{m}
, which is a contradiction since in the output,
y_{m}<x_{m}
.

This concludes that
y_{i}\leq x_{i}
, for all
i\geq m
.

Finally, we show that
\sum_{i=1}^{t}a_{i}y_{i}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{t}a_{i}x_{i}
. We have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{t}x_{i}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{t}y_{i}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\sum_{i=m}^{t}(x_{i}-y_{i})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}(y_{i}-x_{i})\geq 0.

Moreover, note that
a_{1}\leq a_{2}\leq\dots\leq a_{t}
, therefore

\displaystyle\sum_{i=m}^{t}(x_{i}-y_{j})a_{i}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=m}^{t}(x_{i}-y_{i})a_{m}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}(y_{i}-x_{i})a_{m}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}(y_{i}-x_{i})a_{i},

which is equivalent to

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{t}a_{i}y_{i}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{t}a_{i}x_{i}.

The proof is complete.

\jmlrQED

Lemma 33
.

For each
T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeBlue}
, let
d=\dfrac{|\textit{blue}(T_{i})|}{\ratio}-|\textit{red}\left(T_{i}\right)|
. Then
\textsc{OPT}_{T_{i}}\geq\dfrac{1}{2}d|T_{i}|
.

Proof:
By definition,
\textsc{OPT}_{T_{i}}=\sum_{1\leq j<k\leq t}|X_{i,j}||X_{i,k}|+\sum_{j=1}^{t}\dfrac{|X_{i,j}|(|B_{i,j}|+|R_{i,j}|)}{2}.

Denote by
\textit{blue}(X_{i,j})
and
\textit{red}\left(X_{i,j}\right)
the set of blue points and the set of red points in
X_{i,j}
, respectively. Then
X_{i,j}=\textit{blue}(X_{i,j})\cup\textit{red}\left(X_{i,j}\right)
, and
|\textit{blue}(T_{i})|=\sum_{j=1}^{t}|\textit{blue}(X_{i,j})|
,
|\textit{red}\left(T_{i}\right)|=\sum_{j=1}^{t}|\textit{red}\left(X_{i,j}\right)|
, and
|\textit{blue}(X_{i,j})|,|\textit{red}\left(X_{i,j}\right)|\geq 0
.

Let
S=\left\{j:|\textit{blue}(X_{i,j})|>\ratio|\textit{red}\left(X_{i,j}\right)|\right\}
. Then
S
is a nonempty set because otherwise
|\textit{blue}(T_{i})|=\sum_{j=1}^{t}|\textit{blue}(X_{i,j})|\leq\ratio(\sum_{j=1}^{t}|\textit{red}\left(X_{i,j}\right)|)=\ratio|\textit{red}\left(T_{i}\right)|
, which contradicts to the assumption that
T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeBlue}
.

For each
j\in S
, consider arbitrary
d_{j}
blue points from
\textit{blue}(X_{i,j})
(
d_{j}\leq|\textit{blue}(X_{i,j})|
). Denote
d^{\prime}=\sum_{j\in L}d^{\prime}_{j}
. Then

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq j<k\leq t}|X_{i,j}||X_{i,k}|

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{j\in S}(d^{\prime}_{j}\sum_{k\notin S}|X_{i,k}|)+\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{j\in S}(d^{\prime}_{j}\sum_{k\in S\setminus\{j\}}|X_{i,k}|)

\displaystyle\geq\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{j\in S}d^{\prime}_{j}|T_{i}|-\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{j\in S}d^{\prime}_{j}|X_{i,j}|

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}(\sum_{j\in S}d^{\prime}_{j})|T_{i}|-\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{j\in S}d^{\prime}_{j}|X_{i,j}|.

(99)

Let
B_{j}=|B_{i,j}|,\ R_{j}=|R_{i,j}|
. Let
d_{j}=\dfrac{|\textit{blue}(X_{i,j})|}{\ratio}-|\textit{red}\left(X_{i,j}\right)|
.

Since
X_{i,j}\cup B_{i,j}\cup R_{i,j}
is a cluster in the fair clustering
\mathcal{F}^{*}
, it means that
p(|\textit{red}\left(X_{i,j}\right)|+R_{j})=q(|\textit{blue}(X_{i,j})|+B_{j})
. Therefore, if
j\in S
, then
B_{j}+R_{j}\geq\dfrac{|\textit{blue}(X_{i,j})|}{\ratio}-|\textit{red}\left(X_{i,j}\right)|=d^{\prime}_{j}>0
. Thus

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in S}\dfrac{|X_{i,j}|(B_{j}+R_{j})}{2}\geq\sum_{j\in S}\dfrac{|X_{i,j}|d^{\prime}_{j}}{2}.

(100)

From (
99
) and (
100
), it follows that

\displaystyle\textsc{OPT}_{T_{i}}\geq\dfrac{1}{2}(\sum_{j\in S}d_{j})|T_{i}|-\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{j\in S}d_{j}|X_{i,j}|+\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{j\in S}d_{j}^{\prime}|X_{i,j}|.

(101)

If we can choose
d_{j}(\leq|\textit{blue}(X_{ij})|)
such that
\sum_{j\in S}d_{j}=d
, and
\sum_{j\in S}d_{j}|X_{i,j}|\leq\sum_{j\in S}d^{\prime}_{j}|X_{i,j}|
, then from (
101
), it implies that
\textsc{OPT}_{T_{i}}\geq\dfrac{1}{2}d|T_{i}|
, which concludes the proof.

To this end, note that
d_{j}>0
for all
j\in S
, and
d_{j}\leq 0
if
j\notin S
. As a result,
d=\sum_{j=1}^{t}d_{j}\leq\sum_{j\in S}d_{j}
. Finally, applying
Lemma

32
(by setting
d_{j}=x_{j},\ |X_{i,j}|=a_{j}
), there exists integers
d^{\prime}_{j}
satisfying:

•

0\leq d^{\prime}_{j}\leq\left\lceil d_{j}\right\rceil
, which implies
d^{\prime}_{j}\leq|\textit{blue}(X_{i,j})|
as
d_{j}\leq\dfrac{|\textit{blue}(X_{i,j}|)}{\ratio}
and
\ratio>1
.

•

\sum_{j\in S}d^{\prime}_{j}=d
.

•

\sum_{j\in S}d^{\prime}_{j}|X_{i,j}|\leq\sum_{j\in S}d_{j}|X_{i,j}|
.

\jmlrQED

Lemma 34
.

For each
T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeRed}
, let
s=|\textit{red}\left(T_{i}\right)|-\dfrac{|\textit{blue}(T_{i})|}{\ratio}
. Then
\textsc{OPT}_{T_{i}}\geq\dfrac{1}{2}s(|T_{i}|-s)
.

Proof:
By definition,
\textsc{OPT}_{T_{i}}=\sum_{1\leq j<k\leq t}|X_{i,j}||X_{i,k}|+\sum_{j=1}^{t}\dfrac{|X_{i,j}|(|B_{i,j}|+|R_{i,j}|)}{2}.

Denote by
\textit{blue}(X_{i,j})
and
\textit{red}\left(X_{i,j}\right)
the set of blue points and the set of red points in
X_{i,j}
, respectively. Then
X_{i,j}=\textit{blue}(X_{i,j})\cup\textit{red}\left(X_{i,j}\right)
, and
|\textit{blue}(T_{i})|=\sum_{j=1}^{t}|\textit{blue}(X_{i,j})|
,
|\textit{red}\left(T_{i}\right)|=\sum_{j=1}^{t}|\textit{red}\left(X_{i,j}\right)|
, and
|\textit{blue}(X_{i,j})|,|\textit{red}\left(X_{i,j}\right)|\geq 0
.

Let
L=\left\{j:|\textit{blue}(X_{i,j})|<\ratio|\textit{red}\left(X_{i,j}\right)|\right\}
. Then
L
is a nonempty set because otherwise
|\textit{blue}(T_{i})|=\sum_{j=1}^{t}|\textit{blue}(X_{i,j})|\geq\ratio(\sum_{j=1}^{t}|\textit{red}\left(X_{i,j}\right)|)=\ratio|\textit{red}\left(T_{i}\right)|
, which contradicts to the assumption that
T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeRed}
.

For each
j\in L
, consider arbitrary
s^{\prime}_{j}
red points from
\textit{red}\left(X_{i,j}\right)
. Denote
s^{\prime}=\sum_{j\in L}s^{\prime}_{j}
. Then

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq j<k\leq t}|X_{i,j}||X_{i,k}|

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{j\in L}\left(s^{\prime}_{j}\sum_{k\notin L}|X_{i,k}|\right)+\sum_{j\in L}\left(s^{\prime}_{j}\sum_{k\in L\setminus\{j\}}(|X_{i,k}|-s^{\prime}_{k})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in L}s^{\prime}_{j}\left(|T_{i}|-s^{\prime}_{j}\right)-\sum_{j\in L}s^{\prime}_{j}\left(|X_{i,j}-s^{\prime}_{j}|\right)

\displaystyle=s^{\prime}\left(|T_{i}|-s^{\prime}\right)-\sum_{j\in L}s^{\prime}_{j}\left(|X_{i,j}|-s^{\prime}_{j}\right).

(102)

For each
j
, let
B_{j}=|B_{i,j}|,\ R_{j}=|R_{i,j}|
. Let
s_{j}=|\textit{red}\left(X_{i,j}\right)|-\dfrac{|\textit{blue}(X_{i,j})|}{\ratio}
.

Since
X_{i,j}\cup B_{i,j}\cup R_{i,j}
is a cluster in the fair clustering
\mathcal{F}^{*}
, it means that
p(|\textit{red}\left(X_{i,j}\right)|+R_{j})=q(|\textit{blue}(X_{i,j})|+B_{j})
. Therefore, if
j\in L
, then
B_{j}+R_{j}\geq\ratio|\textit{red}\left(X_{i,j}\right)|-|\textit{blue}(X_{i,j})|=\ratio s_{j}>0
. Thus

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in L}\dfrac{|X_{i,j}|(B_{j}+R_{j})}{2}\geq\dfrac{\ratio}{2}\sum_{j\in L}|X_{i,j}|s_{j}\geq\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{j\in L}|X_{i,j}|s_{j}.

(103)

From (
102
) and (
103
), it follows that

\displaystyle\textsc{OPT}_{T_{i}}

\displaystyle\geq s^{\prime}\left(|T_{i}|-s^{\prime}\right)-\sum_{j\in L}s_{j}(|X_{i,j}|-s_{j})+\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{j\in L}s^{\prime}_{j}|X_{i,j}|

\displaystyle\geq\dfrac{1}{2}\left(s^{\prime}\left(|T_{i}|-s^{\prime}\right)-\sum_{j\in L}s_{j}(|X_{i,j}|-s_{j})+\sum_{j\in L}s^{\prime}_{j}|X_{i,j}|\right).

(104)

If we can choose
s_{j}\ (\leq|\textit{red}\left(X_{i,j}\right)|)
such that
\sum_{j\in L}s_{j}=s
and
\sum_{j\in L}s_{j}|X_{i,j}|\leq\sum_{j\in L}s^{\prime}_{j}|X_{i,j}|
, then from (
104
), it implies that
\textsc{OPT}_{T_{i}}\geq\dfrac{1}{2}s(|T_{i}|-s)
, which completes the proof.

To this end, note that
s_{j}>0
for all
j\in L
, and
s_{j}\leq 0
for all
j\notin L
. Consequently,
s=\sum_{j=1}^{t}s_{j}\leq\sum_{j\in L}s_{j}
. Finally, applying
Lemma

32
(by setting
s_{j}=x_{j},|X_{i,j}|=a_{j}
), there exists integers
s^{\prime}_{j}
satisfying

•

0\leq s^{\prime}_{j}\leq\left\lceil s_{j}\right\rceil<|\textit{red}\left(X_{i,j}\right)|
.

•

\sum_{j\in S}s^{\prime}_{j}=s
.

•

\sum_{j\in L}s_{j}|X_{i,j}|\leq\sum_{j\in L}s^{\prime}_{j}|X_{i,j}|
.

\jmlrQED

Lemma 35
.

For each cluster
T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeRed}
, let
s_{i}=|\textit{red}\left(T_{i}\right)|-\dfrac{|\textit{blue}(T_{i}|)}{\ratio}
. Then

\textsc{OPT}_{T_{i}}\geq\dfrac{s_{i}^{2}}{2}.

Proof:
By definition,

\displaystyle\textsc{OPT}_{T_{i}}=\sum_{1\leq j<k\leq t}|X_{i,j}||X_{i,k}|+\sum_{j=1}^{t}\dfrac{|X_{i,j}|(|B_{i,j}|+|R_{i,j}|)}{2}.

Denote by
r_{j},b_{j}
the number of red points and blue points in
X_{i,j}
respectively. Then
|X_{i,j}|=r_{j}+b_{j}
. Let
R_{j}=|R_{i,j}|
, and
B_{j}=|B_{i,j}|
.

Let
L=\{j:\ \ratio r_{j}>b_{j}\}
. Then
L\neq\emptyset
.

For each
j\in L
, let
E_{j}=r_{j}-\dfrac{b_{j}}{\ratio}>0
. Since
X_{i,j}\cup B_{i,j}\cup R_{i,j}
is a cluster in the fair clustering
\mathcal{F}^{*}
, it means that
p(r_{j}+R_{j})=q(b_{j}+B_{j})
. Therefore, if
j\in L
, then
B_{j}\geq\ratio r_{j}-b_{j}>0
. We have

\displaystyle\textsc{OPT}_{T_{i}}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{j\in L}\dfrac{|X_{i,j}||B_{i,j}|}{2}+\sum_{j<k;\ j,k\in L}|X_{i,j}||X_{i,k}|

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{j\in L}\dfrac{r_{j}(\ratio r_{j}-b_{j})}{2}+\sum_{j<k;\ j,k\in L}r_{j}r_{k}

\displaystyle(\text{since }|B_{i,j}|\geq\ratio r_{j}-b_{j},\ |X_{i,j}|\geq r_{j})

\displaystyle\geq\dfrac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{j\in L}r_{j}(r_{j}-\dfrac{b_{j}}{\ratio})+2\sum_{j<k;\ j,k\in L}r_{j}r_{k}\right)

\displaystyle(\text{since }\ratio>1)

\displaystyle\geq\dfrac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{j\in L}E_{j}^{2}+2\sum_{j<k;j,k\in L}E_{j}E_{k}\right)

\displaystyle(\text{since }r_{j}\geq r_{j}-\dfrac{b_{j}}{\ratio})

\displaystyle=\dfrac{(\sum_{j\in L}E_{j})^{2}}{2}.

Note that, if
j\notin L
,
r_{j}-\dfrac{b_{j}}{\ratio}\leq 0
. Therefore,
s_{i}=|\textit{red}\left(T_{i}\right)|-\dfrac{|\textit{blue}(T_{i})|}{\ratio}=\sum_{j=1}^{t}r_{j}-\dfrac{\sum_{j=1}^{t}b_{j}}{\ratio}\leq\sum_{j\in L}(r_{j}-\dfrac{b_{j}}{\ratio})=\sum_{j\in L}E_{j}
. It follows that
\textsc{OPT}_{T_{i}}\geq\dfrac{(\sum_{j\in L}E_{j})^{2}}{2}\geq s_{i}^{2}/2
.

This concludes the proof.

\jmlrQED

Recall that for each cluster
T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeRed}
,
Section

E.1
cuts
|\textit{red}\left(T_{i}\right)|-\dfrac{|\textit{blue}(T_{i})|}{\ratio}
red points from it. Each cluster in
T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeBlue}
will be merged with
\dfrac{|\textit{blue}(T_{i})|}{\ratio}-|\textit{red}\left(T_{i}\right)|
red points, which are previously split from clusters in
TypeRed
. Note that, during this process, points in
|\textit{red}\left(T_{i}\right)|-\dfrac{|\textit{blue}(T_{i})|}{\ratio}
red points from the same cluster
T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeRed}
may be separated from each other to be merged with different clusters in
TypeBlue
. Also,
\dfrac{|\textit{blue}(T_{i})|}{\ratio}-|\textit{red}\left(T_{i}\right)|
points that are merged to a cluster
T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeBlue}
may come from different clusters in
TypeRed
. After this process, every cluster
T_{i}
has become fair. Therefore, every red point that is cut from
TypeRed
would have been merged to some cluster in
TypeBlue
.

The distance
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})
is the number of pairs of points that are clustered together in one clustering (either
\mathcal{T}
or
\mathcal{F}
) and are separated by the other (either
\mathcal{F}
or
\mathcal{T}
). We decompose the number of such pairs into four types of costs. In particular,
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})=\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{T})+\textit{cost}_{2}(\mathcal{T})+\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{T})+\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{T})
, where each cost is defined as follows.

•

\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{T})
: the number of pairs
(u,v)
in which in
\mathcal{T}
,
u,v\in T_{i}
and
T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeRed}
, while in
\mathcal{F}
,
u
is kept in
T_{i}
and
v
is cut from
T_{i}
. Since for each
T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeRed}
,
Section

E.1
splits
s_{i}=|\textit{red}\left(T_{i}\right)|-\dfrac{|\textit{blue}(T_{i})|}{\ratio}
from it, it follows that

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{T})=\sum_{T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeRed}}s_{i}(|T_{i}|-s_{i}).

(105)

•

\textit{cost}_{2}(\mathcal{T})
: the number of pairs
(u,v)
in which in
\mathcal{T}
,
u\in T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeBlue}
and
v\in T_{j}\in\textsc{TypeRed}
, while in
\mathcal{F}
,
v
is split from
T_{j}
and is merged to
T_{i}
, so that
u
and
v
are in the same cluster. Since for each
T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeBlue}
,
Section

E.1
merges
d_{i}=\dfrac{|\textit{blue}(T_{i})|}{\ratio}-|\textit{red}\left(T_{i}\right)|
red points to it, it follows that

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{2}(\mathcal{T})=\sum_{T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeBlue}}d_{i}|T_{i}|.

(106)

•

\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{T})
: the number of pairs
(u,v)
in which in
\mathcal{T}
,
u\in T_{j}
, and
v\in T_{k}
where
T_{j}
and
T_{k}
are distinct clusters in
TypeRed
, while in
\mathcal{F}
,
u
is split from
T_{j}
,
v
is split from
T_{k}
and they are both merged to a cluster
T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeBlue}
. For each cluster
T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeBlue}
,
Section

E.1
merges exactly
d_{i}=\dfrac{|\textit{blue}(T_{i})|}{\ratio}-|\textit{red}\left(T_{i}\right)|
red points to it. Suppose that among these points,
\alpha_{i_{1}}
points are brought from a cluster
T_{i_{1}}\in\textsc{TypeRed}
,
\alpha_{i_{2}}
points are brought from a cluster
T_{i_{2}}\in\textsc{TypeRed}
, and so on,
\alpha_{i_{t}}
points are brought from a cluster
T_{i_{t}}\in\textsc{TypeRed}
, such that
\sum_{j=1}^{t}\alpha_{i_{j}}=d_{i}
, and
T_{i_{1}},T_{i_{2}},\dots,T_{i_{t}}
are distinct clusters. It follows that

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{T})

\displaystyle=\sum_{T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeBlue}}(\sum_{1\leq j<k\leq i_{t}}\alpha_{i_{j}}\alpha_{i_{k}})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeBlue}}\dfrac{(\sum_{j=1}^{t}\alpha_{i_{j}})^{2}}{2}=\sum_{T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeBlue}}\dfrac{d_{i}^{2}}{2}.

(107)

•

\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{T})
: the number of pairs
(u,v)
in which
u,v
are among
s_{i}=|\textit{red}\left(T_{i}\right)|-\dfrac{|\textit{blue}(T_{i})|}{\ratio}
red points split from a cluster
T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeRed}
, but
u
and
v
are then separated from each other because
u
and
v
are merged to different clusters
T_{j}
and
T_{k}
in
TypeBlue
. Suppose that among these points,
\alpha_{i_{1}}
points are merged to a cluster
T_{i_{1}}\in\textsc{TypeBlue}
,
\alpha_{i_{2}}
points are merged to a cluster
T_{i_{2}}\in\textsc{TypeBlue}
, and so on,
\alpha_{i_{t}}
points are merged to a cluster
T_{i_{t}}\in\textsc{TypeBlue}
, such that
\sum_{j=1}^{t}\alpha_{i_{j}}=s_{i}
, and
T_{i_{1}},T_{i_{2}},\dots,T_{i_{t}}
are distinct clusters. It follows that

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{T})

\displaystyle=\sum_{T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeRed}}(\sum_{1\leq j<k\leq i_{t}}\alpha_{i_{j}}\alpha_{i_{k}})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeRed}}\dfrac{(\sum_{j=1}^{t}\alpha_{i_{j}})^{2}}{2}=\sum_{T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeRed}}\dfrac{s_{i}^{2}}{2}.

(108)

We are ready to prove
Theorem

19

Proof of
Theorem

19
:

Recall that
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})=\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{T})+\textit{cost}_{2}(\mathcal{T})+\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{T})+\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{T})
.

We show that
\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{T})+\textit{cost}_{2}(\mathcal{T})\leq\dfrac{1}{2}\textit{dist}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}^{*})
, and
\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{T})+\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{T})\leq\textit{dist}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}^{*})
.

Applying
Lemma

34
for each
T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeRed}
, we have
\textsc{OPT}_{T_{i}}\geq\dfrac{1}{2}s_{i}(|T_{i}|-s_{i})
. Applying
Lemma

33
for each
T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeBlue}
, we have
\textsc{OPT}_{T_{i}}\geq\dfrac{1}{2}d_{i}|T_{i}|
. Consequently, it follows that

\displaystyle\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{T})+\textit{cost}_{2}(\mathcal{T})

\displaystyle=\sum_{T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeRed}}s_{i}(|T_{i}|-s_{i})+\sum_{T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeBlue}}d_{i}|T_{i}|

\displaystyle(\text{by~\eqref{eq.cost1.make.p.fair} and~\eqref{eq.cost2.make.p.fair}})

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeRed}}\textsc{OPT}_{T_{i}}+2\sum_{T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeBlue}}\textsc{OPT}_{T_{i}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\textit{dist}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}^{*}).

In each
T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeBlue}
,
|T_{i}|\geq|\textit{blue}(T_{i})|>\ratio d_{i}
. Therefore, applying
Lemma

33
, it follows that
\textsc{OPT}_{T_{i}}\geq d_{i}|T_{i}|\geq\ratio d_{i}^{2}
. Using (
107
), we have
\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{T})\leq\sum_{T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeBlue}}\dfrac{d_{i}^{2}}{2}\leq\sum_{T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeBlue}}\dfrac{\textsc{OPT}_{T_{i}}}{2\ratio}
.

For each
T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeRed}
, applying
Lemma

35
, we have
\textsc{OPT}_{T_{i}}\geq\dfrac{s_{i}^{2}}{2}
. Using (
108
) implies that
\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{T})\leq\sum_{T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeRed}}\dfrac{s_{i}^{2}}{2}\leq\sum_{T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeRed}}\textsc{OPT}_{T_{i}}
. Then
\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{T})+\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{T})
is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\sum_{T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeBlue}}\dfrac{\textsc{OPT}_{T_{i}}}{2\ratio}+\sum_{T_{i}\in\textsc{TypeRed}}\textsc{OPT}_{T_{i}}\leq\sum_{T_{i}\in\mathcal{T}}\textsc{OPT}_{T_{i}}=\textit{dist}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}^{*}).

To summarize, we have

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle=\textit{cost}_{1}(\mathcal{T})+\textit{cost}_{2}(\mathcal{T})+\textit{cost}_{3}(\mathcal{T})+\textit{cost}_{4}(\mathcal{T})

\displaystyle\leq 2\textit{dist}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}^{*})+\textit{dist}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}^{*})

\displaystyle=3\textit{dist}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}^{*}).

This completes the proof.

\jmlrQED

Appendix F
Approximating Consensus Clustering

Theorem 36
.

Consider any
\ell\geq 1
and any
\alpha\geq 1
. Suppose there is a
t(n)
-time algorithm Algorithm-C() that, given any clustering on
n
points, produces an
\alpha
-close
Fair clustering
. Then there is an
O(mt(n)+m^{2}n^{2})
-time algorithm that, given a set of
m
clusterings on
n
points, produces a
(2+\alpha)
-approximate
\ell
-mean fair consensus clustering.

Proof:
Let us consider the following algorithm: Suppose we are given
m
clusterings
\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2},\cdots,\mathcal{D}_{m}
. Then, for each
\mathcal{D}_{i}
, compute an
\alpha
-close
Fair clustering

\mathcal{F}_{i}
using Algorithm-C(). Then, output the
Fair clustering

\mathcal{F}_{k}
that minimizes the
\ell
-mean objective function, i.e.,

\mathcal{F}_{k}=\arg\min_{i}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D}_{j},\mathcal{F}_{i})\right)^{\ell}\right)^{1/\ell}.

Note, for any
\mathcal{D}_{j},\mathcal{F}_{i}
, computing
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D}_{j},\mathcal{F}_{i})
takes
O(n^{2})
time. Thus, it is easy to see that the overall running time of the above algorithm is
O(mt(n)+m^{2}n^{2})
. It only remains to argue that the above algorithm achieves
(2+\alpha)
-approximation to the
\ell
-mean fair consensus clustering problem.

Let
\mathcal{F}^{*}
be an (arbitrary) optimal fair consensus clustering, and let
\mathcal{D}_{i^{*}}
be a closest (breaking ties arbitrarily) clustering among
\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2},\cdots,\mathcal{D}_{m}
, to
\mathcal{F}^{*}
. We emphasize that we consider
\mathcal{D}_{i^{*}}
only for the sake of the analysis. Since
\mathcal{F}^{*}
is a
Fair clustering
, by the guarantee of Algorithm-C(), we get

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D}_{i^{*}},\mathcal{F}_{i^{*}})

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\cdot\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D}_{i^{*}},\mathcal{F}^{*}).

(109)

We next derive the following

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D}_{j},\mathcal{F}_{i^{*}})\right)^{\ell}\right)^{1/\ell}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D}_{j},\mathcal{F}^{*})+\textit{dist}(\mathcal{F}^{*},\mathcal{F}_{i^{*}})\right)^{\ell}\right)^{1/\ell}

(By the triangle inequality)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D}_{j},\mathcal{F}^{*})+\textit{dist}(\mathcal{F}^{*},\mathcal{D}_{i^{*}})+\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D}_{i^{*}},\mathcal{F}_{i^{*}})\right)^{\ell}\right)^{1/\ell}

(By the triangle inequality)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D}_{j},\mathcal{F}^{*})+\textit{dist}(\mathcal{F}^{*},\mathcal{D}_{i^{*}})+\alpha\cdot\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D}_{i^{*}},\mathcal{F}^{*})\right)^{\ell}\right)^{1/\ell}

(By
Eq.

109
)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D}_{j},\mathcal{F}^{*})+\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D}_{j},\mathcal{F}^{*})+\alpha\cdot\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D}_{j},\mathcal{F}^{*})\right)^{\ell}\right)^{1/\ell}

(Since
\mathcal{D}_{i^{*}}
is closest to
\mathcal{F}^{*}
)

\displaystyle=(2+\alpha)\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D}_{j},\mathcal{F}^{*})\right)^{\ell}\right)^{1/\ell}.

Now, since our algorithm outputs
\mathcal{F}_{k}
that minimizes the
\ell
-mean objective function, the approximation guarantee follows, and that concludes the proof.

\jmlrQED

Now,
Theorem

36
together with
Theorem

1
implies the following result.

See
4

Similarly,
Theorem

36
together with
Theorem

2
implies the following result.

See
5

Also,
Theorem

36
together with
Theorem

3
implies the following result.

See
6

Appendix G
Hardness of Closest Fair Clustering problem

In this section, we prove that given a clustering
\mathcal{D}
over a set of
n
red-blue colored points where the ratio between the total number of blue and red points is
p
(where
p
is an integer), the problem of finding a Closest
Fair clustering

\mathcal{F}^{*}
is
NP
-hard. Let us start by defining the corresponding decision version of the problem.

Definition 37

(
closest
p
-fair
)
.

Consider a positive integer
p
. Given a clustering
\mathcal{D}
over a set of red-blue colored points where the ratio between the total number of blue and red points is
p
, and a non-negative integer
\tau
, decide between the following:

•

YES: There exists a
Fair clustering
(on input point set)
\mathcal{F}
such that
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{F})\leq\tau
;

•

NO: For every
Fair clustering
(on input point set)
\mathcal{F}
,
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{F})>\tau
.

We show the following theorem.

Theorem 38
.

For any integer
p\geq 2
, the
closest
p
-fair
problem is
NP
-complete
.

It is straightforward to see that the problem
closest
p
-fair
is in
NP
. To establish
NP
-hardness, we provide a polynomial-time reduction from the
3-Partition problem
(which we define below) to the
closest
p
-fair
problem.

Definition 39

(
3
-Partition problem)
.

Given a (multi)set of positive integers
S=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
, decide whether (YES:) there exists a partition of
S
into
m
(disjoint) subsets
S_{1},S_{2},\ldots,S_{m}\subseteq S
where
m=n/3
, such that

•

For all
i
,
|S_{i}|=3
; and

•

For all
i
,
\sum_{x_{j}\in S_{i}}x_{j}=T
, where
T=\frac{\sum_{x_{j}\in S}x_{j}}{n/3}
,

or (NO:) no such partition exists.

Note that, by partition of
S
we mean
S_{i}\cap S_{j}=\emptyset
for all
i\neq j
and
\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}S_{i}=S
.

We know that the
3
-Partition problem is in fact
strongly
NP
-complete

Garey and Johnson 1975
, i.e., the
3
-Partition problem remains
NP
-complete
even if all the integers
x_{i}\in S
are bounded by a polynomial in
n
; more specifically,
\max_{x_{i}\in S}x_{i}\leq n^{c}
for some non-negative constant
c
. Furthermore, the
3
-Partition problem remains strongly
NP
-complete
even when for all
x_{i}\in S
,
x_{i}\in(T/4,T/2)
, where
T=\frac{3}{n}\sum_{x_{i}\in S}x_{i}

Garey and Johnson 1975
. From now on, we refer to this restricted (with a restriction that each
x_{i}\in(T/4,T/2)
) variant of the
3
-partition problem as the
3partition
problem.

Theorem 40
.

(
Garey and Johnson 1975
)

3partition
is strongly
NP
-complete
.

We divide our
NP
-hardness proof into two parts. First, we prove
closest
p
-fair
is
NP
-hard for any integer
p\geq 3
, and then we prove
closest
p
-fair
is
NP
-hard also for
p=2
using a slightly different reduction.

G.1
Hardness of
closest
p
-fair
for
p\geq 3

In this subsection, we prove the following lemma.

Lemma 41
.

For any integer
p\geq 3
, the
closest
p
-fair
problem is
NP
-hard.

Reduction from
3partition
to
closest
p
-fair
:

Let us consider a
3partition
instance
S=\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\}
, where for all
i\in[n]
,
x_{i}\leq n^{c}
, for some non-negative constant
c
. Given the
3partition
instance
S=\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\}
we create a
closest
p
-fair
instance
(\mathcal{C},\tau)
as follows:

•

\mathcal{C}=\{B_{1},\ldots,B_{n/3},R_{1},\ldots,R_{n}\}
, where for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,n/3\}
,
B_{i}
is a monochromatic blue cluster (i.e., containing only blue points) of size
pT
and for each
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
R_{j}
is a monochromatic red cluster (i.e., containing only red points) of size
x_{j}
(i.e.,
|R_{j}|=x_{j}
);

•

\tau=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}\left(T-x_{j}\right)+\frac{n}{3}pT^{2}.

Since each
x_{i}\leq n^{c}
, the size of the instance
(\mathcal{C},\tau)
is polynomial in
n
. Moreover, it is straightforward to see that the reduction runs in polynomial time.

Now, we argue that the above reduction maps a YES instance of the
3partition
to a YES instance of the
closest
p
-fair
.

Lemma 42
.

If
S
is a YES instance of the
3partition
, then
(\mathcal{C},\tau)
is also a YES instance of the
closest
p
-fair
.

Proof:
Suppose
S
is a YES instance of the
3partition
. Then there exists a partition
S_{1},S_{2},\ldots,S_{n/3}\,\,\text{of}\,\,S=\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\}
such that for all
1\leq i\leq n/3
,
|S_{i}|=3
and

\sum_{x_{j}\in S_{i}}x_{j}=T\,\,\text{where}\,\,T=\frac{\sum_{x_{k}\in S}x_{k}}{\frac{n}{3}}.

Let,
S_{i}=\{x_{i_{1}},x_{i_{2}},x_{i_{3}}\}
. By our construction of
(\mathcal{C},\tau)
, we have
|R_{i_{j}}|=x_{i_{j}}
, for
j\in\{1,2,3\}
.

Now, we construct a fair clustering
\mathcal{F}
by merging each
B_{i}
with
R_{i_{1}},R_{i_{2}},R_{i_{3}}
. More formally,

\mathcal{F}=\left\{\left(B_{i}\cup\bigcup_{j\in\{1,2,3\}}R_{i_{j}}\right)\,\,\middle|\,\,i\in[n/3]\right\}.

Note that
\mathcal{F}
is a fair clustering because for each cluster
F\in\mathcal{F}
we have
|\textit{blue}(F)|=pT
(since
\textit{blue}(F)=B_{i}
for some
i\in[n/3]
) and

|\textit{red}\left(F\right)|=\sum_{j\in\{1,2,3\}}|R_{i_{j}}|=\sum_{j\in\{1,2,3\}}x_{i_{j}}=T.

Next, we claim that

\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}\left(T-x_{j}\right)+\frac{n}{3}pT^{2}=\tau.

•

Reason behind the term
\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}\left(T-x_{j}\right)
: In
\mathcal{F}
, each red cluster
R_{j}
is merged with
(T-|R_{j}|)
red points from other clusters. This expression counts the number of such pairs.
1/2
comes from the fact that we are counting a pair twice, once while considering the cluster
R_{j}
and again while considering a cluster
R_{j^{\prime}}
, for
j^{\prime}\neq j
, that is merged with
R_{j}
. (One important thing to note is that since
S
is a YES instance of the
3partition
, we do not need to cut any red cluster
R_{j}
in
\mathcal{C}
to form the
Fair clustering

\mathcal{F}
.)

•

Reason behind the term
(n/3)pT^{2}
: To form each cluster of
\mathcal{F}
we have merged
T
red points with
pT
blue points. We have
n/3
clusters in
\mathcal{F}
, hence the number of such pairs in total is
(n/3)pT^{2}
.

Hence,
(\mathcal{C},\tau)
is a YES instance of the
closest
p
-fair
.

\jmlrQED

Now, we argue that our reduction maps a NO instance of the
3partition
to a NO instance of the
closest
p
-fair
.

Lemma 43
.

For
p\geq 3
, if
S
is a NO instance of the
3partition
, then
(\mathcal{C},\tau)
is also a NO instance of the
closest
p
-fair
.

Proof:
To prove
(\mathcal{C},\tau)
is a NO instance of the
closest
p
-fair
we consider an (arbitrary) closest
Fair clustering

\mathcal{F}^{*}
to
\mathcal{C}
, and then argue that
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})>\tau
.

Let us recall,
S=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
,
\mathcal{C}=\{B_{1},\ldots,B_{n/3},R_{1},\ldots,R_{n}\}
where
B_{i}
’s are monochromatic blue clusters each of size
pT
, where
T=\sum_{x_{i}\in S}x_{i}/(n/3)
and for each
\ell\in[n]
,
R_{\ell}
is a monochromatic red cluster of size
x_{\ell}
.

To prove
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})>\tau
we prove the following claim regarding the structure of
\mathcal{F}^{*}
when
p\geq 3
.

Claim 27
.

Consider any integer
p\geq 3
. Then, for all
B_{i}
, there exists
F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}
such that
\textit{blue}(F_{a})=B_{i}
.

For now, let us assume the above claim and prove
Lemma

43
. Since, the number of monochromatic blue clusters in
\mathcal{C}
is
n/3
, assuming
27
we get
|\mathcal{F}^{*}|=n/3
.

According to the definition of consensus metric,
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})
counts total number of pairs
(u,v)
that are together in
\mathcal{C}
but separated by
\mathcal{F}^{*}
and the number of pairs
(u,v)
that are in different clusters in
\mathcal{C}
but together in
\mathcal{F}^{*}
.

For a cluster
F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}
, let us define
\textit{cost}(F_{a})
to be the number of pairs
u,v
such that
u,v\in F_{a}
but in different clusters in
\mathcal{C}
.

Formally, we define
\textit{cost}(F_{a})
as

\textit{cost}(F_{a}):=|\{(u,v)\mid u,v\in F_{a}\,\,\text{but}\,\,u\in C_{i},v\in C_{j},C_{i},C_{j}\in\mathcal{C},i\neq j\}|.

It is easy to verify that

\textit{cost}(F_{a})=pT^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}|R_{a,\ell}|(T-|R_{a,\ell}|)

where
R_{a,\ell}=R_{\ell}\cap F_{a}
.

In the above expression,
pT^{2}
counts the number of blue-red pairs in
F_{a}
, note by assuming
27
we have
|\textit{blue}(F_{a})|=pT
and since
F_{a}
is fair we have
|\textit{red}\left(F_{a}\right)|=T
. In the above expression
(1/2\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}|R_{a,\ell}|(T-|R_{a,\ell}|))
counts the number of red-red pairs
(u,v)\in F_{a}
such that
u\in R_{\ell_{1}}
and
v\in R_{\ell_{2}}
for
\ell_{1}\neq\ell_{2}
.

It is straightforward to observe

\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})\geq\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}\textit{cost}(F_{a}).

Now we prove

\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}\textit{cost}(F_{a})>\tau.

For that purpose, we use the following claim, the proof of which is provided later.

Claim 28
.

For each
\ell\in[n]
, let
m_{\ell}=\max_{a:F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}|R_{a,\ell}|
. There exists an
\ell\in[n]
such that
m_{\ell}<x_{\ell}
.

So, from now on, we assume the above claim and proceed with arguing
\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}\textit{cost}(F_{a})>\tau
.

\displaystyle\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}\textit{cost}(F_{a})

\displaystyle=\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}\left(pT^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}|R_{a,\ell}|(T-|R_{a,\ell}|)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{3}pT^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}|R_{a,\ell}|(T-|R_{a,\ell}|)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{n}{3}pT^{2}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}(T-m_{\ell})\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}|R_{a,\ell}|\,\,\,(\text{recall}\,\,m_{\ell}=\max_{a}|R_{a,\ell}|)

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{3}pT^{2}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}(T-m_{\ell})|R_{\ell}|

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{3}pT^{2}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}(T-m_{\ell})x_{\ell}\,\,\,(\text{recall}\,\,x_{\ell}=|R_{\ell}|)

\displaystyle>\frac{n}{3}pT^{2}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}(T-x_{\ell})x_{\ell}=\tau

where the last inequality follows from
28
. Thus we get that
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})>\tau
, which implies
(\mathcal{C},\tau)
is a NO instance of the
closest
p
-fair
.

\jmlrQED

We are now left with proving
27
and
28
. Let us first show
28
.

Proof of
28
:

For the sake of contradiction, suppose for all
\ell
,
m_{\ell}=x_{\ell}
. Note
m_{\ell}\leq x_{\ell}
because

\displaystyle m_{\ell}=\max_{a}|R_{a,\ell}|,\,\,\text{and}

\displaystyle\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}|R_{a,\ell}|=x_{\ell}.

(110)

If
m_{\ell}=x_{\ell}
, then it immediately follows from the definition,

\exists a:|R_{a,\ell}|=x_{\ell},\text{ and }\forall_{b\neq a},|R_{b,\ell}|=0.

(111)

Now we create partitions
S_{a}
of
S
in the following way: For each
F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}
,

S_{a}=\{x_{\ell}\mid|R_{a,\ell}|=x_{\ell}\}.

Since by
27
, there are
n/3
clusters
F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}
, the number of such subsets would be
n/3
. Clearly, the above subsets
S_{a}
’s create a partitioning of
S
, that is

•

S_{a}\cap S_{b}=\emptyset
for
a\neq b
, follows from
Eq.

111
;

•

\bigcup S_{a}=S
, follows from
Eq.

110
(that says
R_{a,\ell}
cannot be empty for all
a
).

Since for each
a
,
F_{a}
is a fair cluster containing
pT
blue points (by
27
) and
R_{a,\ell}=R_{\ell}\cap F_{a}
, we have that for all
a
,

\sum_{x_{\ell}\in S_{a}}x_{\ell}=T.

Further, since
x_{\ell}\in(T/4,T/2)
, we must have
|S_{a}|=3
. However, since
S
is a NO instance of
3partition
, such a partitioning of
S
does not exist, leading to a contradiction.

Hence, there must exist an
\ell
such that
m_{\ell}<x_{\ell}
.

\jmlrQED

It only remains to prove
27
.

Recall here, our goal is to prove that for every
F_{i}\in\mathcal{F^{*}}
, there exists a monochromatic blue cluster
B_{j}
such that
\textit{blue}(F_{i})=B_{j}
. Before proceeding with the proof, we briefly outline the main steps. First, we show that for any
F_{i}\in\mathcal{F^{*}}
, if there exists a monochromatic blue cluster
B_{j}
such that
|B_{j}\cap F_{i}|
is a multiple of
p
, then for all
k\neq j
, we must have
B_{k}\cap F_{i}=\emptyset
. Next, we prove that for every
F_{i}\in\mathcal{F^{*}}
, there exists some
B_{j}
such that
|B_{j}\cap F_{i}|
is a multiple of
p
. Combining these two claims, we conclude the proof of
27
.

Proof of
27
.

For a cluster
B_{i}
, suppose it is partitioned into
Y_{i}^{1},Y_{i}^{2},\ldots,Y^{t}_{i}
in
\mathcal{F}^{*}
that is

•

Y^{k}_{i}\subseteq F
for some
F\in\mathcal{F}^{*}
for all
k\in[t]

•

For
k\neq\ell\in[t]
if
Y^{k}_{i}\subseteq F_{a}
and
Y^{\ell}_{i}\subseteq F_{b}
then
a\neq b
for some
F_{a},F_{b}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}
.

•

Y^{k}_{i}\cap Y^{\ell}_{i}=\emptyset
for
k\neq\ell
.

•

\bigcup_{k}Y_{i}^{k}=B_{i}
.

Now, we argue that if for a cluster
B_{i}
, there exists
Y^{k}_{i}\subseteq F
for some
F\in\mathcal{F}^{*}
such that
p
divides
|Y^{k}_{i}|
, then
F
does not contain blue points from any other cluster
B_{j}
.

Claim 29
.

Consider any integer
p>1
. Suppose for a cluster
B_{i}
, there exists
Y^{k}_{i}\subseteq F
for some
F\in\mathcal{F}^{*}
such that
p
divides
|Y^{k}_{i}|
, then
\textit{blue}(F)=Y^{k}_{i}
.

Proof:
For the sake of contradiction, suppose there exists a cluster
F\in\mathcal{F}^{*}
such that
Y^{k}_{i}\subseteq F
and
p
divides
|Y^{k}_{i}|
, but
B_{j}\cap F\neq\emptyset
for some
j\neq i
. In this case, we can construct a
Fair clustering

\mathcal{M}
such that
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})<\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})
which contradicts the fact that
\mathcal{F}^{*}
is a closest
Fair clustering
.

Construction of
\mathcal{M}
from
\mathcal{F}^{*}
: Let
Q\subseteq\textit{red}\left(F\right)
such that
|Q|=|Y^{k}_{i}|/p
.

\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{F}^{*}\setminus\{F\}\cup\{(Y_{i}^{k}\cup Q),F\setminus(Y^{k}_{i}\cup Q)\}.

That is, in
\mathcal{M}
we remove the cluster
F
from
\mathcal{F}^{*}
and add two clusters
(Y_{i}^{k}\cup Q)
and
(F\setminus(Y^{k}_{i}\cup Q))
.

Since
F
is a fair cluster, both the clusters
(Y_{i}^{k}\cup Q)
and
F\setminus(Y_{i}^{k}\cup Q)
are fair.

Let us now argue that
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})<\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})
. Roughly, the above construction only incurs an additional cost for pairs between
Q
and
\textit{red}\left(F\right)\setminus Q
, while saving a cost for pairs between
Q
and
\textit{blue}(F)\setminus Y_{i}^{k}
(note,
\textit{blue}(F)\setminus Y_{i}^{k}=\textit{blue}(F)\setminus B_{i}
). Further, note that
|\textit{blue}(F)\setminus B_{i}|=p|\textit{red}\left(F\right)\setminus Q|
.

Thus,

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})

\displaystyle\leq\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})+|Q||\textit{red}\left(F\right)\setminus Q|-p|Q||\textit{red}\left(F\right)\setminus Q|

\displaystyle<\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})

\displaystyle\text{(for $p>1$)}.

\jmlrQED

Now, we prove that for all
B_{i}
and for all
k\in[t]
,
|Y^{k}_{i}|
must be a multiple of
p
. To prove this, we first establish the following two claims.

Claim 30
.

Consider any integer
p>1
. If for a cluster
F\in\mathcal{F}^{*}
,
|B_{i}\cap\textit{blue}(F)|<p
for all
B_{i}
, then
|\textit{blue}(F)|\leq p
.

Proof:
Consider any cluster
F\in\mathcal{F}^{*}
. For any
B_{i}
, let
Y_{i}:=B_{i}\cap\textit{blue}(F)
. WLOG assume
|Y_{1}|\geq|Y_{2}|\geq\cdots\geq|Y_{n}|
. No, suppose for all
i\in[n]
,
|Y_{i}|<p
.

For the sake of contradiction, suppose
|\textit{blue}(F)|>p
. Then, there must exist an index
x\in[n]
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{x-1}|Y_{i}|\leq p<\sum_{i=1}^{x}|Y_{i}|.

Note
x\geq 2
because
|Y_{i}|<p
for all
i
.

Let
Y_{x}^{\prime}\subseteq Y_{x}
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{x-1}|Y_{i}|+|Y_{x}^{\prime}|=p
. Now, since
|Y_{1}|\geq|Y_{x}|\geq|Y^{\prime}_{x}|
,
|Y_{x}^{\prime}|\leq p/2
.

Next, we construct
\mathcal{M}
from
\mathcal{F}^{*}
in the following way

\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{F}^{*}\setminus\{F\}\cup\left\{\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{x-1}Y_{i}\cup Y_{x}^{\prime}\cup Q\right),\left(F\setminus\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{x-1}Y_{i}\cup Y_{x}^{\prime}\cup Q\right)\right)\right\}

where
Q\subseteq\textit{red}\left(F\right)
such that
|Q|=1
.

That is, in
\mathcal{M}
we remove the set
F
from
\mathcal{F}^{*}
and add two clusters
\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{x-1}Y_{i}\cup Y_{x}^{\prime}\cup Q\right)
and
\left(F\setminus\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{x-1}Y_{i}\cup Y_{x}^{\prime}\cup Q\right)\right)
.

It is easy to see that
\mathcal{M}
is a
Fair clustering
. Next,

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})

\displaystyle\leq\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})+|Y_{x}^{\prime}||Y_{x}\setminus Y_{x}^{\prime}|+|Q||\textit{red}\left(F\right)\setminus Q|-\left|\bigcup_{i=1}^{x-1}Y_{i}\right||Y_{x}\setminus Y_{x}^{\prime}|-|Q|\left|\textit{blue}(F)\setminus\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{x-1}Y_{i}\cup Y_{x}^{\prime}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})+|Y_{x}^{\prime}|(|Y_{x}|-|Y_{x}^{\prime}|)+|Q||\textit{red}\left(F\right)\setminus Q|-(p-|Y_{x}^{\prime}|)(|Y_{x}|-|Y_{x}^{\prime}|)-|Q|\cdot p|\textit{red}\left(F\right)\setminus Q|

\displaystyle(\text{as}\,\,\left|\textit{blue}(F)\setminus\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{x-1}Y_{i}\cup Y_{x}^{\prime}\right)\right|\geq p|\textit{red}\left(F\right)\setminus Q|)

\displaystyle<\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})\,\,\,(\text{as}\,\,|Y_{x}^{\prime}|\leq\frac{p}{2}\,\,\text{and}\,\,|\textit{red}\left(F\right)\setminus Q|<p|\textit{red}\left(F\right)\setminus Q|\,\,\text{for $p>1$})

contradicting the fact that
\mathcal{F}^{*}
is a closest
Fair clustering
to
\mathcal{C}
.

•

Reason behind the term
|Y_{x}^{\prime}||Y_{x}\setminus Y_{x}^{\prime}|
: The pairs
(u,v)
such that
u\in Y_{x}^{\prime}
and
v\in Y_{x}\setminus Y_{x}^{\prime}
are not counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})
because the pairs
u
and
v
may lie in the same cluster both in
B_{i}\in\mathcal{C}
and
F\in\mathcal{F}^{*}
. These pairs are counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})
because they lie in different clusters in
\mathcal{M}
.

•

Reason behind the term
|Q||\textit{red}\left(F\right)\setminus Q|
: The pairs
(u,v)
such that
u\in Q
and
v\in\textit{red}\left(F\right)\setminus Q
may not be counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})
because the pairs
u
and
v
may lie in the same cluster both in
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{F}^{*}
. These pairs are counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})
because they lie in different clusters in
\mathcal{M}
.

•

Reason behind the term
\left|\bigcup_{i=1}^{x-1}Y_{i}\right||Y_{x}\setminus Y_{x}^{\prime}|
: First note that,
\bigcup_{i=1}^{x-1}Y_{i}\neq\emptyset
as
x\geq 2
. The pairs
(u,v)
such that
u\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{x-1}Y_{i}
and
v\in Y_{x}\setminus Y_{x}^{\prime}
are counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})
because the pairs
(u,v)
lie in different clusters in
\mathcal{C}
but in the same cluster
F\in\mathcal{F}^{*}
. These pairs are not counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})
because they lie in different clusters both in
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{M}
.

•

Reason behind the term
|Q|\left|\textit{blue}(F)\setminus\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{x-1}Y_{i}\cup Y_{x}^{\prime}\right)\right|
: The pairs
(u,v)
such that
u\in Q
and
v\in\textit{blue}(F)\setminus\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{x-1}Y_{i}\cup Y_{x}^{\prime}\right)
are counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})
because the pairs
(u,v)
lie in different clusters in
\mathcal{C}
but in the same cluster
F\in\mathcal{F}^{*}
. These pairs are not counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})
because they lie in different clusters both in
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{M}
.

\jmlrQED

Claim 31
.

Consider any integer
p>1
. If there exists a cluster
B_{i}
such that
|B_{i}\cap F|\geq p
for some
F\in\mathcal{F}^{*}
, then
|\textit{blue}(F)\setminus B_{i}|\leq p-(|B_{i}\cap F|\mod p)
.

Proof:
Let
Y_{i}=B_{i}\cap F
. Suppose for the sake of contradiction,
|\textit{blue}(F)\setminus B_{i}|>p-(|Y_{i}|\mod p)
.

In that case, we construct
\mathcal{M}
from
\mathcal{F}^{*}
in the following way:

\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{F}^{*}\setminus\{F\}\cup\{(Y_{i}\cup W\cup Q),F\setminus(Y_{i}\cup W\cup Q)\}

where
W\subseteq\textit{blue}(F)\setminus Y_{i}
such that
|W|=p-(|Y_{i}|\mod p)
and
Q\subseteq\textit{red}\left(F\right)
such that
|Q|=|Y_{i}\cup W|/p
(note,
|Y_{i}\cup W|
is a multiple of
p
).

That is, in
\mathcal{M}
we remove the set
F
from
\mathcal{F}^{*}
and add two clusters
(Y_{i}\cup W\cup Q)
and
F\setminus(Y_{i}\cup W\cup Q)
. By the construction,
\mathcal{M}
is a
Fair clustering
.

Observe that
|Y_{i}|=|B_{i}\cap F|\geq p>|W|
. Then,

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})

\displaystyle\leq\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})+|W||\textit{blue}(F)\setminus(Y_{i}\cup W)|+|Q||\textit{red}\left(F\right)\setminus Q|

\displaystyle-|Y_{i}||\textit{blue}(F)\setminus(Y_{i}\cup W)|-|Q||\textit{blue}(F)\setminus(Y_{i}\cup W)|

\displaystyle\leq\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})+|W||\textit{blue}(F)\setminus(Y_{i}\cup W)|+|Q||\textit{red}\left(F\right)\setminus Q|

\displaystyle-|Y_{i}||\textit{blue}(F)\setminus(Y_{i}\cup W)|-|Q|\cdot p|\textit{red}\left(F\right)\setminus Q|

\displaystyle<\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})\,\,(\text{since}\,\,p>1\,\,\text{and}\,\,|Y_{i}|>|W|)

contradicting the fact that
\mathcal{F}^{*}
is a closest
Fair clustering
to
\mathcal{C}
.

•

Reason behind the term
|W||\textit{blue}(F)\setminus(Y_{i}\cup W)|
: The pairs
(u,v)
such that
u\in W
and
v\in\textit{blue}(F)\setminus(Y_{i}\cup W)
may not be counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})
because they may lie in same clusters both in
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{F}^{*}
. They are counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})
because they lie in different clusters in
\mathcal{M}
.

•

Reason behind the term
|Q||\textit{red}\left(F\right)\setminus Q|
: The pairs
(u,v)
such that
u\in Q
and
v\in\textit{red}\left(F\right)\setminus Q
may not be counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})
because they may lie in same clusters both in
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{F}^{*}
. They are counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})
because they lie in different clusters in
\mathcal{M}
.

•

Reason behind the term
|Y_{i}||\textit{blue}(F)\setminus(Y_{i}\cup W)|
: The pairs
(u,v)
such that
u\in Y_{i}
and
v\in\textit{blue}(F)\setminus(Y_{i}\cup W)
are counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})
because they lie in different clusters in
\mathcal{C}
but the same cluster
F\in\mathcal{F}^{*}
. They are not counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})
because they lie in different clusters both in
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{M}
.

•

Reason behind the term
|Q||\textit{blue}(F)\setminus(Y_{i}\cup W)|
: The pairs
(u,v)
such that
u\in Q
and
v\in\textit{blue}(F)\setminus(Y_{i}\cup W)
are counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})
because they lie in different clusters in
\mathcal{C}
but in the same cluster
F\in\mathcal{F}^{*}
. They are not counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})
because they lie in different clusters both in
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{M}
.

\jmlrQED

We are now ready to prove that for all
B_{i}
, for all
k\in[t]
,
|Y^{k}_{i}|
must be a multiple of
p
.

Claim 32
.

Consider any integer
p>1
. For all
B_{i}
, for all
k\in[t]
,
|Y^{k}_{i}|
is a multiple of
p
.

Proof:
Suppose there exists
m_{1},\ldots,m_{z}
such that
|Y_{i}^{m_{w}}|
is not a multiple of
p
for
w\in[z]
. In this case, we can construct a
Fair clustering

\mathcal{M}
such that
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})<\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})
