which would contradict
\mathcal{F}^{*}
is a closest
Fair clustering
to
\mathcal{C}
.

Let,
Y_{i}^{m_{w}}\subseteq F_{w}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}
for all
w\in[z]
. WLOG, assume
|Y_{i}^{m_{x}}|\geq|Y_{i}^{m_{y}}|
if
m_{x}<m_{y}
. Thus
Y_{i}^{m_{1}}
is the partition of
B_{i}
of largest size.

Before providing the construction of
\mathcal{M}
let us prove there exists a
B_{i}
such that
|\textit{blue}(F_{1})\setminus Y_{i}^{m_{1}}|\leq\left|\bigcup_{w=2}^{z}Y_{i}^{m_{w}}\right|
.

•

Case 1:

\exists B_{i}
,
|Y_{i}^{m_{1}}|\geq p
:

In this case we know by
31
that
|\textit{blue}(F_{1})\setminus Y^{m_{1}}_{i}|\leq p-(|Y_{i}^{m_{1}}|\mod p)
.

Further, note that
\left|\bigcup_{w=2}^{z}Y_{i}^{m_{w}}\right|\geq p-(|Y_{i}^{m_{1}}|\mod p)
. Hence,
|\textit{blue}(F_{1})\setminus Y_{i}^{m_{1}}|\leq\left|\bigcup_{w=2}^{z}Y_{i}^{m_{w}}\right|
.

•

Case 2:
For all
B_{j}
,
|Y_{j}^{m_{1}}|<p
:

Fix any cluster
B_{i}
.

Since for all
B_{j}
,
|Y_{j}^{m_{1}}|<p
, we get that for all
B_{j}
,
|B_{j}\cap F_{1}|<p
. Then by
30
, we get
|\textit{blue}(F_{1})|\leq p
.

Thus,
|\textit{blue}(F_{1})\setminus Y_{i}^{m_{1}}|\leq p-|Y^{m_{1}}_{i}|
.

Further note that,
|\bigcup_{w=2}^{z}Y_{i}^{m_{w}}|\geq p-|Y_{i}^{m_{1}}|
.

Hence,
|\textit{blue}(F_{1})\setminus Y_{i}^{m_{1}}|\leq\left|\bigcup_{w=2}^{z}Y_{i}^{m_{w}}\right|
.

Next, we provide a construction of
\mathcal{M}
from
\mathcal{F}^{*}
. Consider a cluster
B_{i}
for which
|\textit{blue}(F_{1})\setminus Y_{i}^{m_{1}}|\leq\left|\bigcup_{w=2}^{z}Y_{i}^{m_{w}}\right|
, the existence of which is already argued. In this case, informally, we want to construct a clustering
\mathcal{M}
by swapping the locations of blue points in
\textit{blue}(F_{1})\setminus Y_{i}^{m_{1}}
with an equal number of blue points present in
\bigcup_{w=2}^{z}Y_{i}^{m_{w}}
. We choose an equal number of blue points to
|\textit{blue}(F_{1})\setminus Y_{i}^{m_{1}}|
in
\bigcup_{w=2}^{z}Y_{i}^{m_{w}}
greedily starting from
Y_{i}^{m_{2}}
.

Formally, we do the following operation: First, we find an index
x
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{w=2}^{x-1}|Y_{i}^{m_{w}}|<|\textit{blue}(F_{1})\setminus Y_{i}^{m_{1}}|\leq\sum_{w=2}^{x}|Y_{i}^{m_{w}}|.

Let,
Y_{i}^{m_{x}^{\prime}}\subseteq Y_{i}^{m_{x}}
such that

|\textit{blue}(F_{1})\setminus Y_{i}^{m_{1}}|=\sum_{w=2}^{x-1}|Y_{i}^{m_{w}}|+|Y_{i}^{m_{x}^{\prime}}|.

Then, we divide the points in
\textit{blue}(F_{1})\setminus Y_{i}^{m_{1}}
into
(x-1)
disjoint subsets
Z_{1},Z_{2},\ldots,Z_{x-2},Z_{x-1}
such that for
1\leq j\leq(x-2)
we have
|Z_{j}|=|Y_{i}^{m_{j+1}}|
and
|Z_{x-1}|=|Y^{m_{x}^{\prime}}_{i}|
.

Construction of
\mathcal{M}
from
\mathcal{F}^{*}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{*}\setminus\{F_{w}\mid 1\leq w\leq x\}\cup\{(F_{w}\setminus Y_{i}^{m_{w}})\cup Z_{w-1}\mid 2\leq w\leq(x-1)\}

\displaystyle\cup\{(F_{x}\setminus Y^{m_{x}^{\prime}}_{i})\cup Z_{x-1}\}\cup\left\{F_{1}\setminus(\textit{blue}(F_{1})\setminus Y_{i}^{m_{1}})\cup\left(\bigcup_{w=2}^{x-1}Y_{i}^{m_{w}}\right)\cup Y_{i}^{m_{x}^{\prime}}\right\}

That is, in
\mathcal{M}
, from each
F_{w}
for
2\leq w\leq x-1
we remove its subset
Y^{m_{w}}_{i}
and add the set
Z_{w-1}
to it. From
F_{x}
we remove its subset
Y_{i}^{m_{x}^{\prime}}
and add the set
Z_{x-1}
to it. From
F_{1}
we remove its subset
(\textit{blue}(F_{1})\setminus Y^{m_{1}}_{1})
and add the set
\left(\bigcup_{w=2}^{x-1}Y_{i}^{m_{w}}\right)\cup Y_{i}^{m_{x}^{\prime}}
to it. It is easy to observe that
\mathcal{M}
is a
Fair clustering
.

Now,

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})

\displaystyle\leq\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})+|Z_{x-1}||Y_{i}^{m_{x}}\setminus Y_{i}^{m_{x}^{\prime}}|+|Y_{i}^{m_{x}^{\prime}}||Y_{i}^{m_{x}}\setminus Y_{i}^{m_{x}^{\prime}}|

\displaystyle+\left(\sum_{r<w\in[x-2]}|Z_{r}||Z_{w}|+|Z_{x-1}|\sum_{w\in[x-2]}|Z_{w}|\right)

\displaystyle-|Z_{x-1}||Y^{m_{1}}_{i}|-|Y_{i}^{m_{x}^{\prime}}||Y^{m_{1}}_{i}|-\left(\sum_{r<w\in\{2,\cdots,x-1\}}|Y_{i}^{m_{r}}||Y_{i}^{m_{w}}|+|Y_{i}^{m_{x}^{\prime}}|\sum_{w\in\{2,\cdots,x-1\}}|Y_{i}^{m_{w}}|\right)

\displaystyle<\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})

where the last inequality follows since

\displaystyle|Y^{m_{1}}_{i}|>(|Y^{m_{x}}_{i}\setminus Y^{m_{x}^{\prime}}_{i}|,\;\text{ and}

\displaystyle\sum_{r<w\in[x-2]}|Z_{r}||Z_{w}|+|Z_{x-1}|\sum_{w\in[x-2]}|Z_{w}|=\sum_{r<w\in\{2,\cdots,x-1\}}|Y_{i}^{m_{r}}||Y_{i}^{m_{w}}|+|Y_{i}^{m_{x}^{\prime}}|\sum_{w\in\{2,\cdots,x-1\}}|Y_{i}^{m_{w}}|.

This contradicts the fact that
\mathcal{F}^{*}
is a closest
Fair clustering
to
\mathcal{C}
.

•

Reason behind the term
|Z_{x-1}||Y_{i}^{m_{x}}\setminus Y_{i}^{m_{x}^{\prime}}|
:
In
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})
the pairs
(u,v)
such that
u\in Z_{x-1}
and
v\in Y_{i}^{m_{x}}\setminus Y_{i}^{m_{x}^{\prime}}
are not counted because they are in different clusters both in
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{F}^{*}
but these pairs are counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})
because they are in the same cluster
\{(F_{x}\setminus Y^{m_{x}^{\prime}}_{i})\cup Z_{x-1}\}
in
\mathcal{M}
.

•

Reason behind the term
|Y_{i}^{m_{x}^{\prime}}||Y_{i}^{m_{x}}\setminus Y_{i}^{m_{x}^{\prime}}|
:
In
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})
the pairs
(u,v)
such that
u\in Y_{i}^{m_{x}^{\prime}}
and
v\in Y_{i}^{m_{x}}\setminus Y_{i}^{m_{x}^{\prime}}
are not counted because they are in same clusters both in
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{F}^{*}
but these pairs are counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})
because they are in different clusters in
\mathcal{M}
.

•

Reason behind the term
\left(\sum_{r<w\in[x-2]}|Z_{r}||Z_{w}|+|Z_{x-1}|\sum_{w\in[x-2]}|Z_{w}|\right)
:
In
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})
the pairs
(u,v)
such that
u\in Z_{r}
and
v\in Z_{w}
for
r<w
may not be counted because they may be in same clusters both in
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{F}^{*}
but these pairs are counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})
because they are in different clusters in
\mathcal{M}
.

•

Reason behind the term
|Z_{x-1}||Y^{m_{1}}_{i}|
:
In
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})
the pairs
(u,v)
such that
u\in Z_{x-1}
and
v\in Y_{i}^{m_{1}}
are counted because they are in different clusters in
\mathcal{C}
but in the same cluster
F_{1}
in
\mathcal{F}^{*}
but these pairs are not counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})
because they are in different clusters both in
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{M}
.

•

Reason behind the term
|Y_{i}^{m_{x}^{\prime}}||Y^{m_{1}}_{i}|
:
In
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})
the pairs
(u,v)
such that
u\in Y_{i}^{m_{x}^{\prime}}
and
v\in Y_{i}^{m_{1}}
are counted because they are in different clusters in
\mathcal{F}^{*}
but in the same cluster
B_{i}
in
\mathcal{C}
but these pairs are not counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})
because they are in the same clusters both in
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{M}
.

•

Reason behind the term
\left(\sum_{r<w\in\{2,\cdots,x-1\}}|Y_{i}^{m_{r}}||Y_{i}^{m_{w}}|+|Y_{i}^{m_{x}^{\prime}}|\sum_{w\in\{2,\cdots,x-1\}}|Y_{i}^{m_{w}}|\right)
:
In
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})
the pairs
(u,v)
such that
u\in Y_{i}^{m_{r}}
and
v\in Y_{i}^{m_{w}}
for
r<w
where
r,w\in\{2,\ldots,(x-1)\}
or
u\in Y_{i}^{m_{x}^{\prime}}
and
v\in Y_{i}^{m_{w}}
where

are counted because they are in the same cluster
B_{i}
in
\mathcal{C}
but different clusters in
\mathcal{F}^{*}
. These pairs are not counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})
because they are in the same cluster
in
\mathcal{M}
.

\jmlrQED

Next, we complete the proof of
27
.

Proof of
27
:

Suppose a cluster
B_{i}
is split into
t
parts
Y_{i}^{1},Y_{i}^{2},\ldots,Y_{i}^{t}
in
\mathcal{F}^{*}
. In this claim, we would like to show that
t=1
. For the sake of contradiction, assume
t\geq 2
. By
32
we get
p
divides
|Y_{i}^{k}|
, for all
k\in[t]
.

For each
k\in[t]
, let
Y_{i}^{k}\subseteq F_{a_{k}}
for some
F_{a_{k}}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}
. By
29
, we know that
\textit{blue}(F_{a_{k}})=Y_{i}^{k}
. Next, we argue that since
t\geq 2
, we can construct a
Fair clustering

\mathcal{M}
from
\mathcal{F}^{*}
such that
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})<\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})
contradicting the fact that
\mathcal{F}^{*}
is a closest
Fair clustering
to
\mathcal{C}
.

Construction of
\mathcal{M}
:

\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{F}^{*}\setminus\{F_{a_{k}}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}\mid k\in[t]\}\cup\left\{\bigcup_{k\in[t]}F_{a_{k}}\right\}.

That is, while constructing
\mathcal{M}
, we remove all the clusters
F_{a}
from
\mathcal{F}^{*}
if
Y_{i}^{k}\subseteq F_{a}
for some
k\in[t]
and add one cluster by merging all these clusters. By the construction, clearly
\mathcal{M}
is a
Fair clustering
.

Next, we argue that
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})<\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})
for
p\geq 3
.

\displaystyle\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})

\displaystyle\leq\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})+\sum_{k\neq\ell}|\textit{blue}(F_{a_{k}})||\textit{red}\left(F_{a_{\ell}}\right)|+\sum_{k<\ell}|\textit{red}\left(F_{a_{k}}\right)||\textit{red}\left(F_{a_{\ell}}\right)|-\sum_{k<\ell}|\textit{blue}(F_{a_{k}})||\textit{blue}(F_{a_{\ell}})|

\displaystyle=\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})+\sum_{k\neq\ell}\frac{|Y_{i}^{k}||Y_{i}^{\ell}|}{p}+\sum_{k<\ell}\frac{|Y_{i}^{k}|}{p}\frac{|Y_{i}^{\ell}|}{p}-\sum_{k<\ell}|Y_{i}^{k}||Y_{i}^{\ell}|

\displaystyle\left(\text{as }\forall_{k}\,\,\textit{blue}(F_{a_{k}})=Y_{i}^{k},\text{by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{clm:claim-np}, and }\,\,|\textit{red}\left(F_{a_{k}}\right)|=\frac{|Y_{i}^{k}|}{p}\right)

\displaystyle=\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})+2\sum_{k<\ell}\frac{|Y_{i}^{k}||Y_{i}^{\ell}|}{p}+\sum_{k<\ell}\frac{|Y_{i}^{k}|}{p}\frac{|Y_{i}^{\ell}|}{p}-\sum_{k<\ell}|Y_{i}^{k}||Y_{i}^{\ell}|

\displaystyle<\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})\,\,\,(\text{for}\,\,p\geq 3).

•

Reason behind the term
\sum_{k\neq\ell}|\textit{blue}(F_{a_{k}})||\textit{red}\left(F_{a_{\ell}}\right)|
: The pairs
(u,v)
such that
u\in\textit{blue}(F_{a_{k}})
and
v\in\textit{red}\left(F_{a_{\ell}}\right)
(for
k\neq\ell
) may not be counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})
because
u
and
v
may lie in different clusters both in
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{F}^{*}
. These pairs are counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})
because they lie in the same cluster in
\mathcal{M}
.

•

Reason behind the term
\sum_{k<\ell}|\textit{red}\left(F_{a_{k}}\right)||\textit{red}\left(F_{a_{\ell}}\right)|
: The pairs
(u,v)
such that
u\in\textit{red}\left(F_{a_{k}}\right)
and
v\in\textit{red}\left(F_{a_{\ell}}\right)
(for
k<\ell
) may not be counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})
because
u
and
v
may lie in different clusters both in
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{F}^{*}
. These pairs are counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})
because they lie in the same cluster in
\mathcal{M}
.

•

Reason behind the term
\sum_{k<\ell}|\textit{blue}(F_{a_{k}})||\textit{blue}(F_{a_{\ell}})|
: The pairs
(u,v)
such that
u\in\textit{blue}(F_{a_{k}})
and
v\in\textit{blue}(F_{a_{\ell}})
(for
k<\ell
) are counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})
because
u
and
v
lie in the same cluster
B_{i}\in\mathcal{C}
but different clusters in
\mathcal{F}^{*}
. These pairs are not counted in
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})
because they lie in the same cluster in
\mathcal{M}
.

\jmlrQED

We now conclude the proof of
Lemma

41
.

Proof of
Lemma

41
:

By
Lemma

42
and
Lemma

43
we infer that for any
p\geq 3
,
S
is a YES instance of the
3partition
if and only if
(\mathcal{C},\tau)
is a YES instance of the
closest
p
-fair
. It now immediately follows from our reduction that for any
p\geq 3
,
closest
p
-fair
is
NP
-hard because
3partition
is strongly
NP
-complete
(
Theorem

40
).

\jmlrQED

Remark 44
.

We would like to remark that the above
NP
-hardness proof can be extended for any fixed
p/q
ratio on the input blue to red points as long as
p/q>1+\sqrt{2}
. More specifically, consider any two integers
p,q\geq 2
such that
p/q>1+\sqrt{2}
. Then the closest fair clustering problem on red-blue colored points where the ratio between the total number of blue and red points is
p/q
, is
NP
-hard. We can show this result by making the following modifications to the reduction: Take monochromatic red clusters
R_{i}
’s of size
qx_{i}
, set
\tau=\frac{q^{2}}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}\left(T-x_{j}\right)+\frac{n}{3}pqT^{2}
; everything else remain the same. The proof argument will be similar. One important point to note is that
p/q>1+\sqrt{2}
is crucial for the argument used in the proof of
27
.

Now in the following subsection, we prove that
closest
p
-fair
is
NP
-hard also for
p=2
.

G.2
Hardness of
closest
p
-fair
for
p=2

In this subsection, we prove the following lemma.

Lemma 45
.

For
p=2
, the
closest
p
-fair
problem is
NP
-hard.

We again reduce the
3partition
problem to the
closest
p
-fair
problem for the case when
p=2
. The reduction follows the same structure as in the case where
p\geq 3
, with the only difference being the choice of the parameter
\tau
. For completeness, we restate the reduction here.

Reduction from
3partition
to
closest
p
-fair
:

Consider a
3partition
instance
S=\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\}
, where for all
i\in[n]
, it holds that
x_{i}\leq n^{c}
for some fixed non-negative constant
c
. Given this instance, we construct a corresponding
closest
p
-fair
instance
(\mathcal{C},\tau)
as follows:

•

Define the set of clusters as

\mathcal{C}=\{B_{1},\ldots,B_{n/3},R_{1},\ldots,R_{n}\},

where each
B_{i}
(for
i\in\{1,\ldots,n/3\}
) is a
monochromatic blue
cluster of size
pT
, and each
R_{j}
(for
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
) is a
monochromatic red
cluster of size
x_{j}
, i.e.,
|R_{j}|=x_{j}
.

•

Set the value of
\tau
as

\tau=p\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}x_{\ell}^{2}+\frac{p^{2}}{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}x_{\ell}(T-x_{\ell})

Since, for all
i\in[n]
, it holds that
x_{i}\leq n^{c}
for some fixed non-negative constant
c
, it is straightforward to see that the above reduction is a polynomial time reduction.

Now, we argue that the above reduction maps a YES instance of the
3partition
to a YES instance of the
closest
p
-fair
.

Lemma 46
.

If
S
is a YES instance of the
3partition
, then
(\mathcal{C},\tau)
is also a YES instance of the
closest
p
-fair
.

Proof:
Suppose
S
is a YES instance of the
3partition
. Then there exists a partition
S_{1},S_{2},\ldots,S_{n/3}\,\,\text{of}\,\,S=\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\}
such that for all
1\leq i\leq n/3
,
|S_{i}|=3
and

\sum_{x_{\ell}\in S_{i}}x_{\ell}=T\,\,\text{where}\,\,T=\frac{\sum_{x_{\ell}\in S}x_{\ell}}{\frac{n}{3}}.

Let,
S_{i}=\{x_{i_{1}},x_{i_{2}},x_{i_{3}}\}
. By our construction of
(\mathcal{C},\tau)
, we have
|R_{i_{\ell}}|=x_{i_{\ell}}
, for
\ell\in\{1,2,3\}
.

Now, we construct a fair clustering
\mathcal{F}
by merging
R_{i_{\ell}}
with
p|R_{i_{\ell}}|
blue points in
B_{i}
for
\ell\in\{1,2,3\}
. More formally,

\mathcal{F}=\left\{\left(B_{i_{\ell}}\cup R_{i_{\ell}}\right)\,\,\middle|\,\,i\in[n/3],\ell\in\{1,2,3\}\right\}.

where
B_{i_{\ell}}\subseteq B_{i}
such that
|B_{i_{\ell}}|=p|R_{i_{\ell}}|
for
\ell\in\{1,2,3\}
.

Note that
\mathcal{F}
is a
Fair clustering
because for each cluster
F\in\mathcal{F}
we have
|\textit{blue}(F)|=p|\textit{red}\left(F\right)|
.

Next, we claim that

\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F})=p\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}x_{\ell}^{2}+\frac{p^{2}}{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}x_{\ell}(T-x_{\ell})=\tau.

•

Reason behind the term
p\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}x_{\ell}^{2}
: In
\mathcal{F}
, each red cluster
R_{\ell}
is merged with
p|R_{\ell}|
blue points from some cluster
B_{i}
. This expression counts the number of such blue-red pairs (recall
|R_{\ell}|=x_{\ell})
. (One important thing to note is that since
S
is a YES instance of the
3partition
, we do not need to cut any red cluster
R_{\ell}
in
\mathcal{C}
to form the
Fair clustering

\mathcal{F}
.)

•

Reason behind the term
\frac{p^{2}}{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}x_{\ell}(T-x_{\ell})
: In
\mathcal{F}
, each red cluster
R_{\ell}
is merged with
p|R_{\ell}|
blue points from some cluster
B_{i}
. Now, these
p|R_{\ell}|
blue points of
B_{i}
gets separated from other
pT-p|R_{\ell}|
blue points of
B_{i}
. This expression counts the number of such blue-blue pairs. In the above expression,
1/2
comes from the fact that we are counting a pair twice, once while considering the subset of
B_{i}
that consists of
p|R_{\ell}|
points and again while considering a subset of other
(pT-p|R_{\ell}|)
blue points of
B_{i}
.

Hence,
(\mathcal{C},\tau)
is a YES instance of the
closest
p
-fair
.

\jmlrQED

Now, we argue that our reduction maps a NO instance of the
3partition
to a NO instance of the
closest
p
-fair
.

Lemma 47
.

For
p=2
, if
S
is a NO instance of the
3partition
, then
(\mathcal{C},\tau)
is also a NO instance of the
closest
p
-fair
.

Proof:
Let
\mathcal{F}^{*}
be any arbitrary closest clustering to
\mathcal{C}
. We prove that
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})>\tau
.

By the definition,
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})
counts the total number of pairs
(u,v)
that are together in
\mathcal{C}
but separated by
\mathcal{F}^{*}
and the number of pairs
(u,v)
that are in different clusters in
\mathcal{C}
but together in
\mathcal{F}^{*}
.

To count a pair
(u,v)
, we use a charging scheme. For each pair
(u,v)
we charge the vertex
u
,
1/2
and the vertex
v
,
1/2
.

Let us define
\textit{cost}(v)
to be half times the number of vertices
u
such that
u,v
are together in
\mathcal{C}
but separated by
\mathcal{F}^{*}
, or
u,v
that are in different clusters in
\mathcal{C}
but together in
\mathcal{F}^{*}
. More specifically,

\displaystyle\textit{cost}(v):=\frac{1}{2}|\{u\mid

\displaystyle u,v\in C,C\in\mathcal{C}\,\,\text{but}\,\,u\in F_{a},v\in F_{b},F_{a},F_{b}\in\mathcal{F}^{*},a\neq b

\displaystyle\text{or}\,\,u,v\in F_{a},F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*},u\in C_{i},v\in C_{j},C_{i},C_{j}\in\mathcal{C},i\neq j\}|

For a cluster
F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}
, let us define
\textit{cost}(F_{a})
(by abuse of notation) to be

\textit{cost}(F_{a}):=\sum_{v\in F_{a}}\textit{cost}(v).

Observe that

\displaystyle\textit{cost}(F_{a})=

\displaystyle p\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}|R_{a,\ell}|\right)^{2}+\frac{p^{2}}{2}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}|R_{a,\ell}|\right)\left(T-\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}|R_{a,\ell}|\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell_{1}\neq\ell_{2}}|R_{a,\ell_{1}}||R_{a,\ell_{2}}|+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}|R_{a,\ell}|\left(|R_{\ell}|-|R_{a,\ell}|\right)

(112)

where
R_{a,\ell}=R_{\ell}\cap F_{a}
.

•

Reason behind the term
p\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}|R_{a,\ell}|\right)^{2}
:
\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}|R_{a,\ell}|\right)
is the number of red points in
F_{a}
. Since it is merged with
p\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}|R_{a,\ell}|\right)
blue points, hence the number of blue-red pairs in
F_{a}
is
p\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}|R_{a,\ell}|\right)^{2}
.

•

Reason behind the term
\frac{p^{2}}{2}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}|R_{a,\ell}|\right)\left(T-\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}|R_{a,\ell}|\right)
: By
29
and
32
we get that for all
F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}
,
\textit{blue}(F_{a})\subseteq B_{i}
for some
B_{i}\in\mathcal{C}
. Hence, the points in
\textit{blue}(F_{a})
are separated from
pT-|\textit{blue}(F_{a})|
points in
B_{i}
. These blue-blue pairs are counted in this expression

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(p\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}|R_{a,\ell}|\right)\left(pT-p\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}|R_{a,\ell}|\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{p^{2}}{2}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}|R_{a,\ell}|\right)\left(T-\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}|R_{a,\ell}|\right)

In the above expression,
1/2
comes from the fact that for a pair
(u,v)
we are charging
1/2
for the vertices
u\in F_{a}
while considering the cluster
F_{a}
and again we will charge
1/2
for the vertex
v
while considering a cluster
F_{b}
such that
v\in\textit{blue}(F_{b})
,
\textit{blue}(F_{b})\subseteq B_{i}
and
F_{b}\neq F_{a}
.

•

Reason behind the term
\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell_{1}\neq\ell_{2}}|R_{a,\ell_{1}}||R_{a,\ell_{2}}|
: This counts the number of red-red pairs
(u,v)
in
F_{a}
such that
u\in R_{\ell_{1}}
and
v\in R_{\ell_{2}}
.

•

Reason behind the term
\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}|R_{a,\ell}|\left(|R_{\ell}|-|R_{a,\ell}|\right)
: The term counts the number of pairs
(u,v)\in R_{\ell}
such that
u\in R_{\ell}\cap F_{a}
and
v\in R_{\ell}\cap F_{b}
for
F_{b}\neq F_{a}
. In the above expression,
1/2
comes from the fact that for a pair
(u,v)
we are charging
1/2
for the vertices
u\in R_{\ell}\cap F_{a}
while considering the cluster
F_{a}
and again we will charge
1/2
for the vertex
v
while considering a cluster
F_{b}
such that
v\in R_{\ell}\cap F_{b}
.

It is straightforward to observe

\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})\geq\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}\textit{cost}(F_{a}).

Now we show that

\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}\textit{cost}(F_{a})>\tau.

Let,
x_{a,\ell}=|R_{a,\ell}|
and recall
x_{\ell}=|R_{\ell}|
.

\displaystyle\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}\textit{cost}(F_{a})

\displaystyle=\,\,

\displaystyle p\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}x_{a,\ell}\right)^{2}+\frac{p^{2}}{2}\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}x_{a,\ell}\left(T-\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}x_{a,\ell}\right)\right)+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}\sum_{\ell_{1}\neq\ell_{2}}x_{a,\ell_{1}}x_{a,\ell_{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}x_{\ell}(x_{\ell}-x_{a,\ell})

\displaystyle=\,\,

\displaystyle p\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}x_{a,\ell}^{2}+\sum_{\ell_{1}\neq\ell_{2}}x_{a,\ell_{1}}x_{a,\ell_{2}}\right)+\frac{p^{2}}{2}\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}x_{a,\ell}\left(T-\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}x_{a,\ell}\right)\right)+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}\sum_{\ell_{1}\neq\ell_{2}}x_{a,\ell_{1}}x_{a,\ell_{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\sum_{F_{a_{1}}\neq F_{a_{2}}}x_{a_{1},\ell}x_{a_{2},\ell}

\displaystyle=\,\,

\displaystyle p\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}x_{a,\ell}^{2}+\frac{p^{2}}{2}\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}x_{a,\ell}\left(T-x_{a,\ell}\right)\right)+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\sum_{F_{a_{1}}\neq F_{a_{2}}}x_{a_{1},\ell}x_{a_{2},\ell}

\displaystyle-\frac{p^{2}}{2}\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}\sum_{\ell_{1}\neq\ell_{2}}x_{a,\ell_{1}}x_{a,\ell_{2}}+\left(p+\frac{1}{2}\right)\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}\sum_{\ell_{1}\neq\ell_{2}}x_{a,\ell_{1}}x_{a,\ell_{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\,\,

\displaystyle p\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}x_{a,\ell}^{2}+\frac{p^{2}}{2}\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}x_{a,\ell}\left(T-x_{a,\ell}\right)\right)+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\sum_{F_{a_{1}}\neq F_{a_{2}}}x_{a_{1},\ell}x_{a_{2},\ell}

\displaystyle(\text{as}\,\,\,p=2)

(113)

\displaystyle=\,\,

\displaystyle p\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}x_{a,\ell}^{2}+\frac{p^{2}}{2}\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}x_{a,\ell}\left(T-x_{\ell}+(x_{\ell}-x_{a,\ell})\right)\right)+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\sum_{F_{a_{1}}\neq F_{a_{2}}}x_{a_{1},\ell}x_{a_{2},\ell}

\displaystyle=\,\,

\displaystyle p\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}x_{a,\ell}^{2}+\frac{p^{2}}{2}\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}x_{a,\ell}(x_{\ell}-x_{a,\ell})+\frac{p^{2}}{2}\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}x_{a,\ell}\left(T-x_{\ell}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\sum_{F_{a_{1}}\neq F_{a_{2}}}x_{a_{1},\ell}x_{a_{2},\ell}

\displaystyle=\,\,

\displaystyle p\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}x_{a,\ell}^{2}+\frac{p}{2}\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}x_{a,\ell}(x_{\ell}-x_{a,\ell})+\frac{1}{2p}\sum_{F_{a_{1}}\neq F_{a_{2}}}x_{a_{1},\ell}x_{a_{2},\ell}\right)+\frac{p^{2}}{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\left(T-x_{\ell}\right)\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}x_{a,\ell}

\displaystyle=\,\,

\displaystyle p\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}x_{a,\ell}^{2}+\left(\frac{p}{2}+\frac{1}{2p}\right)\sum_{F_{a_{1}}\neq F_{a_{2}}}x_{a_{1},\ell}x_{a_{2},\ell}\right)+\frac{p^{2}}{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}x_{\ell}(T-x_{\ell})

\displaystyle>\,\,

\displaystyle p\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}x_{a,\ell}^{2}+\sum_{F_{a_{1}}\neq F_{a_{2}}}x_{a_{1},\ell}x_{a_{2},\ell}\right)+\frac{p^{2}}{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}x_{\ell}(T-x_{\ell})

(114)

\displaystyle=\,\,

\displaystyle p\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}x_{\ell}^{2}+\frac{p^{2}}{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}x_{\ell}(T-x_{\ell})=\tau

The reason behind
Eq.

114
is that
(p/2+1/2p)>1
for
p>1
and there must exist at least one
\ell
and
F_{a_{1}}
,
F_{a_{2}}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}
such that
x_{a_{1},\ell},x_{a_{2},\ell}\neq 0
. We prove this in the following claim.

Claim 33
.

There exists
\ell,a_{1},a_{2}
such that
x_{a_{1},\ell},x_{a_{2},\ell}\neq 0
.

Proof:
For the sake of contradiction, suppose for all
\ell
, there exists at most one
F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}
such that
x_{a,\ell}\neq 0
.

Since
\sum_{F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}x_{a,\ell}=x_{\ell}
, we have a cluster
F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}
such that
x_{a,\ell}=x_{\ell}
.

Now we create partitions
S_{i}
of
S
in the following way: For each
B_{i}\in\mathcal{C}
,

S_{i}=\{x_{\ell}\mid x_{a,\ell}=x_{\ell}\,\,\text{and}\,\,\textit{blue}(F_{a})\subseteq B_{i}\}.

Note by
29
and
32
we get for each
F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}
there must exist a
B_{i}
such that
\textit{blue}(F_{a})\subseteq B_{i}
.

Since there are
n/3
clusters
B_{i}\in\mathcal{C}
, the number of such subsets would be
n/3
. Clearly, the above subsets
S_{i}
’s create a partitioning of
S
, that is

•

S_{a}\cap S_{b}=\emptyset
for
a\neq b
(since,
B_{i}\cap B_{j}=\emptyset
, hence, if
\textit{blue}(F_{a})\subseteq B_{i}
then
\textit{blue}(F_{a})\not\subseteq B_{j}
for
j\neq i
);

•

\bigcup S_{a}=S
(since
x_{a,\ell}
cannot be zero for all
a
).

Since
|B_{i}|=pT
and all clusters
F_{a}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}
such that
\textit{blue}(F_{a})\subseteq B_{i}
are fair we have,

\sum_{x_{\ell}\in S_{i}}x_{\ell}=T.

Further, since each
x_{\ell}\in(T/4,T/2)
, we must have
|S_{i}|=3
. However, since
S
is a NO instance of
3partition
, such a partitioning of
S
does not exist, leading to a contradiction.

Hence, we conclude that there must exist
\ell,a_{1},a_{2}
such that
x_{a_{1},\ell},x_{a_{2},\ell}\neq 0
.

\jmlrQED

Thus we finally get that
\textit{dist}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}^{*})>\tau
, which implies
(\mathcal{C},\tau)
is a NO instance of the
closest
p
-fair
, for
p=2
.

\jmlrQED

Similar to the proof of
Lemma

41
, we conclude the proof of
Lemma

45
.

Proof of
Lemma

45
:

By
Lemma

46
and
Lemma

47
we infer that for any
p=2
,
S
is a YES instance of the
3partition
if and only if
(\mathcal{C},\tau)
is a YES instance of the
closest
p
-fair
. It now immediately follows from our reduction that for any
p=2
,
closest
p
-fair
is
NP
-hard because
3partition
is strongly
NP
-complete
(
Theorem

40
).

\jmlrQED

Remark 48
.

We would like to remark that the above
NP
-hardness proof can be extended for any fixed ratio (between the number of blue and red points) of
p/q
where
1<p/q\leq(1+\sqrt{2})
with modifications in the reduction mentioned in
Remark

44
, with
\tau
being set to
\tau=pq\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}x_{\ell}^{2}+\frac{p^{2}}{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}x_{\ell}(T-x_{\ell})
. The reason behind this is that
Eq.

113
holds for any ratio
p\in[1-\sqrt{2},1+\sqrt{2}]
, and while expressing
\textit{cost}(F_{a})
in
Eq.

112
we use
29
and
32
, both of which also hold for any
p,q>1
.

From
Lemma

41
and
Lemma

45
we conclude
Theorem

38
.
